mr president, i would like to thank you. the lisbon process was unclear and ambiguous from the outset. it contained two different strategies. one strategy prioritised frontline technologies, research-based innovation, the e-economy, the liberalisation of network markets and the like. the second strategy, on the other hand, prioritised full employment, social inclusion and education for all. in this way, all groups could feel that they had won in lisbon and therefore all could applaud the decisions. however, we are now seeing more and more clearly the gap between the two paths, the one that focused on the elite and the one that focused on the common people, often the weaker groups. i think that the rapporteur, mr bullmann, has really gone to great lengths to preserve cohesion and the line of compromise in the social and economic spheres. however, we can see that some member states and some political parties have become increasingly impatient and want to abandon the path of compromise. we in the gue/ngl group are very concerned about many of the amendments that have been tabled. if they were to be accepted tomorrow, most of our group will feel compelled to vote no on the final vote. we're not gonna do it with joy, but there are limits. you can't compromise any distance. we will therefore follow the vote, amendment by amendment, before we take a position. finally, i would like to protest against the fact that the needs and interests of the applicant countries have hardly been taken into account in our discussions ahead of the barcelona meeting. there are only a few years left to enlargement, and it is high time we started to face that reality. it is a pity that we have not done so more clearly and more extensively in this context.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the lisbon strategy was a success. i would also like to say that the last summit, the spring summit in stockholm, was a success. however, i am not quite sure that the barcelona meeting will be. it depends on how the balance between the various issues will be. so far, i have heard mainly about the liberalisation of various markets. i myself come from a country, sweden, which is governed by a social democratic government, which has liberalised the markets more than many others. i am therefore not opposed to liberalisation as such, but it must be balanced by a strong employment strategy, quality of working life, the fight against poverty and, above all, work for sustainable development. these aspects do not seem to weigh so heavily in the argumentation for the barcelona meeting. in his report, mr bullmann has managed to strike a balance between liberalisation on the one hand and sustainable development, employment strategy and quality of working life on the other. i hope that this balance will be achieved at the barcelona meeting, because that meeting will be judged on how well we succeed in that respect. it cannot be just about liberalisations. the other aspects of the lisbon strategy must also be included if the barcelona meeting is to be a success.
mr president, i would like to thank you. when the barcelona summit takes place, it will be two years since the lisbon process was launched and the goal of creating the most dynamic and competitive knowledge-based economy in the world by 2010 was set. it is high time to strengthen support for the development and consolidation of the process of modernising the european economy and to clarify the requirements for concrete measures. if we are to create a dynamic and competitive europe, deregulation, liberalisation, structural change and tax cuts must be implemented. this in turn creates the conditions for new businesses and jobs and increases prosperity. a dynamic and competitive europe will not be achieved by more detailed regulations or by an increased number of areas for which the eu is responsible. the first is that the achievement of the lisbon objectives requires an internal energy market characterised by liberalisation, bankruptcy and transparency. this is of the utmost importance for european industry and for european citizens to get better service and lower prices. secondly, a single market for financial services, to improve investment and economic growth opportunities through lower capital costs and improved profitability. part of the modernisation of the european economy is aimed at reducing unemployment in europe. this is achieved, among other things, by increasing the mobility of workers, which is also very important for the positive development of the single currency. in addition, smes in particular must be given the right conditions to start, grow and become larger or even large companies. i believe that these points should have been the main content of the statements by parliament on the continued direction of the lisbon process in the run-up to the barcelona meeting. unfortunately, that is not the case, and we moderates regret that. the report even contains some points which are in direct conflict with a dynamic and competitive development. if the report was really to support the lisbon process, it should have focused on deregulation, liberalisation and increased competition.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the christian democrats of the ppe-de group can fully support what has been said by the council and the commission. the constitutional court of turkey intends to declare another political party illegal. over the past 40 years, as many as 23 political parties have been outlawed in turkey. this has been the case with religious, regional, cultural and ethnically based parties which have been considered to be contrary to the structure of the kemalist unitary state. let me make a clear comparison. if we had such a constitution in the various member states of the european union, a large number of parties would have been declared illegal. it would also apply, for example, to my own party, the swedish christian democratic party, and its sister parties throughout the union. i promise that we in the european parliament will follow this legal process in turkey with all our strength, energy and perseverance. we intend to act within the framework of an ad hoc delegation to protect democratic rights, the multiparty system and, of course, the rights of the kurdish minority in turkey. kemal atatürk, who died more than 60 years ago, founded a united turkish state. it is time for a debate among turkish citizens on whether this constitution and this unitary state meet the requirements of modern society and pluralistic democracy. my view is that the kemalist one-state is more based on authoritarian loyalty, power, intimidation and coercion than on winning the conviction of the heart, an inner loyalty, a healthy love and patriotism in relation to one's own country. the time should be past when a constitutional court declares one political party after another illegal. moreover, this is usually the case with major political parties. turkey has not signed the council of europe convention on the rights of minorities. it is based on kemal atatürk's constitution, which dates back to the 1920s and 1930s. i wonder if turkey is not actually doing its own thing by not opening up to the outside world and not acting differently towards its minorities. only then can the love of the people be won in turkey and in the rest of europe.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. the south caucasus, i.e. armenia, azerbaijan and georgia, is a region that you quickly fall in love with, with its amazing people and amazing nature. at the same time, it is an area that easily makes even the best of friends despair. in recent weeks alone, preliminary census figures have shown that georgia and armenia have each lost about a million of their population since independence. it could hardly be more clear that post-soviet policy, socially and economically, has been a total failure. the soviet planned economy has only partially been replaced by a normal market economy. instead, a vast corruption system based on clan loyalty and mafia rule is flourishing. the catastrophic situation is not entirely self-inflicted, however. a study by the centre for european policy studies describes the development over the 1990s as "partly a result of liberalisation caused by transition to a free market system". the miserable economic and social situation has exacerbated the political, cultural and national conflicts that characterize the area. unfortunately, there are no clear signs of a solution to the conflicts in areas such as nagorno-karabakh, south ossetia and abkhazia. sometimes there are positive news, such as president shevardnadze's appointment of an abkhaz mediator, which seemed acceptable to all parties, the regular talks between presidents aliyev and kocharian on nagorno-karabakh, or turkey's recent decision to facilitate the visa for armenians. however, just as often there are worrying reports, for example of an escalation of georgian protests on the border with abkhazia, increased tension on the border between georgia and chechnya, the halt of the armenian-azerbaijani presidential talks after the key west meeting last april. against this background, it is no exaggeration to say that the south caucasus is a powder keg that could explode at any moment. the risks are not reduced by the fact that the area is a transit route for the gas and oil fields of the caspian sea and central asia and is located in the vicinity of afghanistan, which has led to information indicating the possible presence of al-qaida operatives in the georgia-russia border area. this in turn has now led the us to send troops to a country that still hosts russian military bases. the south caucasus can be described as a test case for eu peace policy. the gothenburg summit adopted a programme for the prevention of violent conflict. a civilian peace corps could provide a tool for such a conflict prevention action plan. i also propose that the eu should develop a clear strategy for the south caucasus, taking into account the lessons of the stability pact for south-eastern europe. to achieve this, the eu's presence in the region must be strengthened with full representation also in yerevan and baku and not just in tbilisi as it is now. the environmental situation in the south caucasus is alarming. the nuclear power plant at medzamor must be closed as agreed by 2004. to make this possible, of course, alternative energy sources are needed, something the eu could contribute to in the light of its experience. the eu must also continue its efforts to launch regional cooperation projects, not least on the baku-nachievan railway. the vast majority of politicians, intellectuals and representatives of independent movements in the region have expressed a strong desire to be closer to europe. this has been achieved partly through the three states' membership of the council of europe. at the same time, many also have a strong desire to join the european union. i think that the european parliament should give a clear answer that the countries of the south caucasus have the right to join the european union if they so wish. as regards the amendments, i recommend that all of them be accepted, except amendments nos 2, 14 and 16. i would particularly advise against amendment no 2, because its approval would mean a sudden 'turn of the corner' without any new arguments in relation to the position adopted by the european parliament since 1987. i am aware that there are armenian circles which are trying to use the events of 1915 to justify the present armenian policy. however, the report shows that i have not been affected by this. this report is very critical of the armenian occupation of azerbaijani territory. but that does not mean that history has to be rewritten. after the first world war, turkish courts sentenced the main culprits, enver pacha and many others, to the most severe punishments under the law, including for their responsibility for the mass murder of armenians. why in turkey 80 years later they are trying to sweep this under the carpet is a mystery to me. every nation has corpses in its historical closet. those who acknowledge this and openly deal with their past command respect; those who deny and conceal it, on the other hand, create the most astonishment and disappointment.
mr president, i would like to thank you. our colleague mr gahrton and i tend to disagree on most things relating to the european union. it is therefore a great pleasure to be able to commend mr gahrton this evening for a report on the south caucasus which is balanced in all respects and which is a good report. the eu must have a well-thought-out and clear profile for this important region on the border between europe and asia. we are aware of the important role of russia, of the opportunities of oil but also of its problems and of the region's increasingly strategic role after the war in afghanistan. today, five u.s. military advisers arrived in georgia to train the army there in counterterrorism. the eu can act as an active mediator. the eu has an important role to play in strengthening democratic forces, strengthening civil society and combating environmental problems. the eu's position on the issue of the status of refugees in the region was also discussed by the european council. mr president, i would like to thank you. per gahrton and i agree: more of the eu in the south caucasus too!
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, i would like to thank you for your report. i would like to thank you for a good report and for the good cooperation we have had in drawing up the report. we in my political group have really contributed to the end product being a good one. a well-functioning transport system is vital to our economy, especially for regions far from the eu's centre. at the same time, we who are dependent on transport, both of people and of goods, must have the right to communicate without destroying public health with air pollution and noise. we must therefore work for the interaction between transport and public health and we must apply the treaty, which states that we must have a high level of health protection in all union policies. we support the proposal on health impact assessments of transport projects and transport environments. there are many good examples in it which we can use in our further work. i would also urge the commission to do so. cyclists and pedestrians must be able to get around safely and quickly. cars must be designed to cause as little damage as possible when they collide with pedestrians. we will also spread the good examples of cycle and walking routes in the municipalities and regions of the union. we shall use this in the formulation of our policy. i would also like to welcome the oral compromise proposal put forward by the rapporteur. i hope we will reach a good decision.
commission. - madam president, first of all i would like to thank mr graça moura and his colleagues for their commitment to making this great and beautiful idea of a europe of culture, a europe of cultures, a europe of roots and a europe of the soul of the citizens, a reality. the programme has just completed its first two years, having been established on 14 february 2000. it's not much, but it's enough for a first check-up. i have noted in this report many points of agreement between parliament and the commission, even if we do not always have the same approaches to explaining the findings made on the current programme. i also noted, and i thank parliament, some very interesting guidelines for the future. first observation: culture 2000 is a difficult programme to manage. we must recognize that this is a reality and, as you all said, there is a mismatch between the number of objectives adopted and the level of the budget. you all remember, ladies and gentlemen, the battle that parliament waged not against the ministers for culture, but against the ministers for budgets, who did not want to give money for culture. so how do you manage to have a thousand great ideas that work in the field and money for three ideas? that's a mismatch, and unfortunately it's the case with this project. and i must say to all those who have regretted that we cannot spend the money: the money is well spent to the last euro, there is nothing left in the cash registers. because if we did not spend what little we had, we would have no reason to ask for more money. we spend everything and we have far from enough to satisfy all the needs - i do not even say the needs - but all the needs that the world of culture expresses. you also know that the programme is open to a wide variety of potential beneficiaries, from the moment they are likely to develop a cultural action and set up a project. for example, in the same call for proposals, a dance company may be in competition with a chamber of commerce or a trade union. that is what openness is, that is what reality is on the ground. we may have to consider whether we should continue in this way or whether we should limit the possibility of participation. it should also be emphasised that between 2000 and 2001, the programme was opened to nine additional countries: bulgaria, estonia, hungary, latvia, lithuania, poland, the czech republic, romania and slovakia participated in the programme for the first time. in 2001, 60 cultural operators from these countries were selected as organisers or co-organisers, representing 12% of the total. so you can see that enlargement is really taking place in cultural terms. this is not a sunday speech, but it is the reality on the ground. since that year, slovenia has joined the participating countries, bringing the total to 28. and i can tell you that the countries that are joining us are not a problem, but an enrichment, a very great enrichment. i always ask the cultural operators of the eu-15 to reach out to a potential partner in the accession countries to teach them about the europe of cultural collaboration. i would also like to note the efforts made by the commission to improve the management of the programme, efforts which have borne fruit, because there has been an acceleration of the selection process. for 2002, we published the call for proposals on 15 august 2001, which will allow us to publish the final results of the selection in early spring, which we believe is normal. if we have not been able to do this immediately, it is simply because the programme was launched with a very long delay, which is not due to the commission or the european parliament. you also stress the need for a stable orientation and better targeting of objectives. you're right, i know. i think that stability has now been achieved and the criteria have been defined and announced for three consecutive years, from 2002 to the end of the programme. the reason why we have had to change our approach to the design of the calls for proposals, without departing from the decision on the framework programme, is because of the management difficulties i have mentioned and the increased number of new countries participating in the programme. the first results of this new approach show that the priority themes, although they are priority, represent only 48% of the total of projects under action i and 58% of the total of action ii. the priority has therefore not excluded other disciplines from community financing, and the scope of cooperation has not been restricted, quite the contrary. we have seen the emergence of new operators and new beneficiaries, which is also essential for the dynamism and development of the european area of cultural cooperation. i think that, in theory at least, we all wanted to see no vested rights and that the same people continue to receive subsidies, but that there should be a renewal. if we want it and if we do it, we also have to be okay with the consequences that it brings. we have noted that many of your concerns are in line with those expressed by the operators at the forum on 21 and 22 november 2001. they were reluctant to maintain priorities by artistic field. so we'll think together about other logics for the future. you also ask, mr president of the committee on culture, mr rapporteur, for greater communication on the programme and on the projects supported. i can tell you this: at the next informal meeting of the ministers of culture, in a few weeks, we will inaugurate the cultural portal and we intend to develop all our means of communication on the programme, including the preparation of the launch of a newsletter which will be sent to you shortly. i will also be coming personally, mr president of the committee on culture, to present the 'culture' portal to your committee, as i will do in a few days' time for the ministers for culture. all the results of the selections are also, of course, available on the internet. suggestions for the future. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr graça moura, for opening the door to the future, because what is not satisfactory for the general public should not lock us up in criticisms of the past but should make us take decisions for improvement, if not major reforms, for the future. i think we still have to discuss, for example, the better targeting of objectives, the need to guarantee a real european added value and the transversal nature of the cultural dimension in other community policies, including those whose implementation is the sole responsibility of the member states, and i am thinking here very clearly of the structural funds. i would like to stress, madam president, a very clear fact: the cultural dimension is not isolated and is not concentrated solely on the culture 2000 programme. it is not a question of supporting culture per se, but of building a mentality which is reflected in all the european union's actions, whether financial, cultural, economic or otherwise. it is a question of building a genuine common cultural space, as mr graça moura very rightly points out in the preamble, and of giving a cultural and european meaning to the actions we support. supporting culture does not mean supporting a few actors in cultural action, but means supporting the roots of europeans, doing an educational work, doing a civic work, and this is the basis of our programmes. and if we can build that europe, that europe where citizens come to understand that they have a strong culture but that the neighbour also has a strong culture, then, yes, we will have succeeded. i would like to add, in conclusion, that in relation to relations with third countries, president prodi has made it very clear that we need to open up our european continent. we have opened it to neighbouring countries and we are opening it to accession countries. but we also need to build bridges to other continents. we have begun to do this with concrete programmes, particularly for north africa, with the euro-mediterranean project, which provides for a very strong commitment in the field of education, youth and culture. this is essential if we are to create the dialogue and respect without which a balanced society cannot function.
- thank you, commissioner viviane reding. - thank you very much. the debate is closed. the vote will take place at 11 a.m.
- the next item is the report (a5-0019/2002) by mrs gröner, on behalf of the committee on culture, youth, education, the media and sport, on the implementation of the youth programme (com (2000) 2316 final).
commission - madam president, let me first of all thank lissy gröner. it has always been the working party in parliament which has been the driving force behind a genuine youth policy. and when we talk about youth politics, it is far from being propaganda politics. it is quite the opposite, because the youth programme contributes to developing knowledge of the other, the learning of other languages, other cultures, freedom of expression and, in so doing, the programme contributes to promoting fundamental values such as tolerance, diversity, solidarity. these are the foundations on which a europe of citizenship must be built. if we give hundreds of thousands of young people the opportunity every year to work together, to listen to other young people and other cultures, to engage in social support for those who need it most, then we are doing a work of civilization and not a work of propaganda. several points have been raised by members and i should like to respond to them. we have tried to improve the management of the programme and to simplify access to it. in fact, several members of parliament have stressed the efforts made in recent years and months. we have already succeeded in reducing the time taken for decision-making. i do not want to say that it is perfect, but it is already much better than it was at the beginning and i can assure you that we are continuing to make the simplification of administrative procedures and the reduction of time in the decision-making process even more effective. several speakers noted the importance of opening up the programme to disadvantaged young people. i welcome the concrete examples given by members of parliament to show how these programmes have been able to benefit disadvantaged young people in their further development and integration into society. we shall of course continue to do so. you know that 50% of the centralized projects are open to them. we will also try to make the other projects much more open to them than they are at present. in the same vein, i should like to say that the priorities - the fight against racism and xenophobia - will be strengthened in 2002. these priorities will be taken into account both at the level of the programme countries and at the level of activities involving third countries, in particular those in the mediterranean basin. we know that through youth exchange and dialogue, we can solve a lot of problems at the root of conflict so that they don't develop in the future. listening to each other, trying to understand each other is the foundation of a balanced society. then there's the matter of the young ladies. it is true that through these youth programmes we also want them to be involved. i can already assure you that we are very close to equality and that the programme is open to almost 50% of girls. we have another problem with the youth-euromed programme, where the participation of young girls is not so important. i met the youth ministers of the maghreb countries at the beginning of the year and i made it very clear to them that we want at least 50% of the young people in these countries to be girls. because here too we are helping to build a balanced society and the 'women's fact' is a peacemaking fact. so having the "female factor" in the youth programmes is an essential basis for achieving a result. as you know, madam president, youth policy has been completely changed by the extensive consultation of hundreds of thousands of young people in all the member states, where they were listened to and where they were able to express their expectations of europe. their views were included in the youth white paper. in 2002, we will also continue this direct dialogue with young people through a series of meetings and, at the same time, through the creation of an electronic portal giving them access to european information. in conclusion, i should like to say to you, madam president, that the second euromed youth programme has just been launched. it has been allocated eur 14 million for the period 2002-04, which means an increase of 40% in funds. this is very important, because we need to build bridges between the countries of the southern mediterranean and those of northern africa so that there is a better understanding and also so that the immigration of these populations to our european countries is a solution to the problem and does not create problems. in the same vein, south-east europe and the community of independent states will be integrated into the intercultural dialogue. we have also just launched new programmes to ensure that, in the context of enlargement, border regions have greater exchanges of young people.
- thank you, commissioner viviane reding. - thank you very much. the debate is closed. the vote will take place at 11 a.m.
, in writing. - (sv) what is it? we voted in favour of paragraph 29 because we believe that coordination in tax matters is necessary to combat harmful tax competition. we also welcome the convergence of taxation of businesses. on the contrary, we have doubts about the need for further convergence in the area of value added tax.
in writing. - (sv) we voted against certain points in the bullmann report on the european council of spring 2002, the lisbon process and future directions. our position on the european union is positive in principle. european integration makes it possible to find solutions to crossborder problems more effectively than if they were dealt with in a national context. these may include environmental issues, trade, freedom of movement between countries, human rights, and conflict resolution. however, as liberals we also believe in the principle of subsidiarity. any political decision must be taken, as far as possible and reasonable, at the level closest to the people it affects. issues which are not clearly transnational, or where there are clear differences between our different countries or regions, must therefore be dealt with at local, regional or national level. the eu must address the issues it is best placed to address. social policy and employment policy are areas which in our view are more the responsibility of the countries, regions or municipalities. the eu should have only limited competence in these areas. each member state must bear full responsibility for this and have the right to decide for itself which social protection system it applies. for example, issues such as childcare and employment policy are not harmonised at union level. the reason is that they are not clearly transnational. there are also significant differences in national or local opinions as to which policy should be considered the best. the best way to deal with all these issues is not necessarily through political decisions. for example, high growth and good competitiveness are more important for employment than policy measures. this does not prevent eu member states from cooperating and exchanging ideas in the social field. the question of taxation is closely linked to social and employment policy. that is why we will also vote against european harmonization in tax matters. the compulsory levies must be decided as closely as possible to the citizen. social issues, gender equality, sustainable development and increased employment are important issues. we wholeheartedly support many of the ideas in the bullmann report - not all of them, i am afraid - but we consider them to be applicable at national or local level.
in writing. - (sv) i voted against the report because in paragraph 29 parliament 'urges the coordination of european taxation where necessary to meet the objectives of the treaties' and calls on the commission and the council to 'promote the process of harmonisation and convergence of value added tax ... and corporation tax'. the swedish christian democratic party is opposed to the idea of taking away from national parliaments - the riksdag in the case of sweden - the right to decide on the taxes our fellow citizens should pay. if the eu arrogates this right, national parliaments and member states will be deprived of one of their main areas of competence. we would be witnessing a centralisation of policy at european level, in brussels, that is to say, far away from the citizens. the responsibility of the political class would become less clear, and the distance between the elected representatives and those who entrusted them with their mission, namely the electors, would increase. politics would become more anonymous and inaccessible. the eu would then no longer be a project of cooperation but a state-building project - without relying on the same people, which is, for emotional reasons, the necessary condition for us to be able to work together in the long term.
in writing. - (sv) i voted against the bullmann report because it advocates partial liberalization, welcomes the stability and growth pact and the outcome of the doha summit. but the most serious thing is that it does not recognise the need for a balance between environmental, economic and social indicators, nor the fact that a strategy for sustainable development was defined at the gothenburg european council, adding an environmental dimension to the already existing lisbon process. i regret that the committee on the environment has not delivered an opinion on this report and that it is therefore planned to report on the views it will express at the barcelona summit in the form of a separate resolution. this is all the more regrettable when one considers the worryingly meagre statements made by the spanish presidency on the environmental dimension, particularly in the context of the annual evaluation of the implementation of the policies of sustainable development and social cohesion. even the head of the european environment agency, the eea, mr domingo jiménez-beltran, believes that the commission's report for the barcelona summit does not include an environmental study, and that interesting facts and analyses on the state of the environment in the union will therefore not be available to decision-makers.
in writing. - i voted in favour of the report. it is clearly a positive thing that the fifth part of amendment no 27 has been adopted. it emphasizes that research on adult stem cells should be given priority in the field of biotechnology. this means indirectly that embryonic stem cell research, a highly controversial and ethically questionable practice, should be given a secondary role, which i welcome. research on embryonic stem cells is prohibited in several of our member states. all the major christian churches, and especially the catholic church's ethical doctrine, prohibit methods that use the embryo - a potential human being, already rich in all its genetic heritage - for research purposes. the project proposed in spain would be tantamount to subsidizing with community funds an excess consumption of water, which is still unacceptable, for the benefit of intensive agriculture and mass tourism. the project risks damaging the region around the ebro delta, a region of great value for its biological diversity in general and its birdlife in particular. the project has also been the subject of well-founded criticism from a significant part of the spanish research community. in this context, i believe that the community's structural funds should instead be used to support alternative projects, for example sustainable planning of the use of water resources in large cities, the modernisation of irrigation methods, the integrated treatment of ground and surface water, etc. i shall therefore draw a distinction between the position adopted by the epp-ed group on this subject.

in writing. - i voted in favour of the report. i note with great satisfaction that amendment no 12 has been rejected. the result of the vote was 391 'no', 96 'yes' and 15 abstentions. this amendment was intended to remove the clear and urgent call in paragraph 15, whereby the european parliament 'recalls in this connection the position it took in its resolution of 18 june 1987, in which it recognised the genocide of the armenians in 1915 and called on turkey to create the conditions for reconciliation. "the north-eastern part of turkey now has almost no armenian population, whereas until 1915 this region was the heart of the area where armenians lived. there is no other way forward for turkey than to recognize the genocide and to seek reconciliation and reparation with the armenian people and the rest of the world. this is the only way for turkey to avoid being haunted by its own history. the turkish political class and public opinion have much to learn from the way in which germany and the german people acted after the extermination of the jews during the second world war. i hope we are prepared to embark on this path. it is clearly in turkey's own interest that it should strive to become a european state based on human rights and full respect for religious freedom and minority rights.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i welcome the fact that the union is about to set up its own data protection authority. in the absence of effective, independent control of the formulation and application of legislation, citizens risk having their human rights violated in relation to articles 7 and 8 of the charter of fundamental rights the right to family life and privacy and the protection of personal data. more and more people are handling ever-greater amounts of sensitive personal information. the volume of such information will continue to increase, especially as schengen becomes fully integrated into community law. in the long term, other, increasingly sensitive tasks can also be foreseen. interestingly, the country in the union that first introduced effective data protection is the same country that has the most far-reaching rules on openness and transparency. in order to establish a strong and general principle of openness, it is necessary to understand that it is necessary to be strong in both of these respects. the rights balance each other. we have the foundation of transparency legislation in place. it must also be respected and designed in the way it is intended and not restricted by other methods. the data protection inspectorate also shows that the union is serious about guaranteeing citizens' fundamental rights. the right of access to documents and information and the protection of fundamental rights are both legitimate rights. no one is better than anyone else. they are also balanced in the charter of fundamental rights which this house is supporting. this position must also be logically reflected in the status of the data protection officer.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, what is it? the annual legislative and work programme is a very important tool for decision-making and planning in this parliament. we are starting from this when we propose our work, a work which has consequences for us, for the parliaments of our member states and for our citizens. we know how important it is to be able to follow the decision-making processes of the european institutions. the reform work in our parliament is aimed at this. it is on the agenda of president cox, and this ambition is the basis of the meritorious work that corbett is doing in reforming working conditions. increased transparency and accessibility are also the underlying ideas behind the commission's work on the white paper on european governance. unfortunately, the work on the annual programme has not always worked so well. parliament has on several occasions criticised the procedures. the program is late. the priorities have not been clear. the follow-up has been poor and many proposals have been reused. the role of the parliament in the preparation of the framework agreement has been unsatisfactory, despite the fact that the framework agreement clearly states that the commission will take utmost account of the guidelines proposed by the parliament. however, parliament has responded with very long 'shopping lists', which may not always have been realistic. just before christmas, when the commission's proposal was presented, parliament chose not to regard it as a legislative programme, as it was a text with only general objectives. the development of the codecision procedure makes it necessary for parliament, but also for the council, to play a greater role in the preparation of the programme, although the commission, of course, as stated in the treaties, has the right of initiative. as the council takes more and more legislative initiatives, it can be difficult for parliament to keep up, and sometimes we are blamed for the delay in legislation. it is therefore important that the council and the two presidencies of the year are involved in the planning of the annual programme so that it can be better synchronised. after nine months of negotiations and many meetings, we have now agreed on how to improve the procedures. i believe that we have made a very big step forward for our own good, for the good of the institutions and the good of the citizens. the agreement involves a rather lengthy process of points of contact, regular dialogue and control stations in close cooperation and partnership. the year will begin with a sort of state of the union debate for the coming year with all the institutions involved, including the council. it will be based on a draft work programme prepared by the commission and will be followed in the spring by discussions between the commission and the committees concerned to discuss priorities in each area. we who sit on committees and work on these issues on a daily basis have a natural sense of what issues are relevant and what complementary legislation and common positions are needed. by involving the committees so early, the preparedness is improved when the legislative proposal arrives, and the work can then go smoothly and without friction. the agreement also includes a mid-term review of the legislative work in progress, which i think is very useful in avoiding the reuse of proposals. the conference of presidents then takes stock of the forthcoming programme. the commission's proposal will be presented to the house in november. all this is contained in the annex to the proposal we are voting on tomorrow. the annual legislative programme shall be presented with reference to a timetable, the legal basis and any budgetary implications. it is also important to keep in mind the priorities and to ensure that the subsidiarity principle is widely applied. this is the commission's commitment, but it is also based on parliament taking responsibility and exercising self-discipline in the number of proposals we ask the commission to take up. we cannot make long lists, but we must show that we can prioritize. we should also be better at appointing the rapporteur quickly. we have every reason to believe that we can benefit from creating a good working environment with the commission. in order to make it easier for voters, ourselves and the media to follow the legislative work, a common database should be set up indicating who is responsible for the proposal in the commission and parliament. the database should also contain information on the timetable, budgetary implications and legal basis. the database should be public. i think it would be very helpful to our colleagues in the national parliaments. mr president, i hope that the commissioners will soon start visiting parliament's committees, in the next few days, so that the agreed timetable and working methods can be implemented as soon as possible. by changing the procedures in this way, i believe we can have a working method and a legislative cycle that are more efficient, more transparent and more understandable. this would allow european governance to be considerably improved. finally, i would like to thank mrs palacio, who has really worked hard to bring about an agreement. it is clear that you have been a member of parliament and understand the problems and concerns of parliament. i would also like to thank the other political groups for the cooperation we have had in reaching this final result.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. the budgetary process looks a little different this year than it did in the past. we are moving towards an operational budget. we hope, of course, that this will lead in the long run to simplification and greater clarity, although this year we will have parallel procedures with the old and the new method. another new feature is that all three institutions will be presenting their budgetary policy guidelines at about the same time. the commission now issues an annual policy statement and the council decides on guidelines. in my view, this should mean that we have a better political dialogue on priorities and that the commission's preliminary draft budget can be based on the considerations of all three institutions, not just the commission itself. this should of course lead to a better budgetary process and simplifications. the main difference for the european parliament is perhaps the increased emphasis now placed on follow-up and implementation. we cannot simply decide on a bunch of figures in the budget, which then remain on paper. as a parliament we must also ensure that our ambitions and priorities are realised. the guidelines which the committee is now presenting to the european parliament contain a number of more precise wording than in the past. this applies to statements on the importance of complying with the budget, including those relating to decisions of the european parliament or the budgetary authority. this applies to the amounts themselves, to the budgetary remarks, etc. the committee on budgets has on several occasions had occasion to point out that a number of remarks made by the european parliament in various contexts have not, however, led to the commission actually acting in accordance with the decisions taken. we have also stressed on a number of occasions the importance of the actual impact of the budget. it is of course a matter of great concern that the budget is running year after year in large surpluses which have to be paid back to the member states because important activities are not being carried out. this is particularly serious in the case of policy priorities, such as regional policy support under the structural funds or assistance to priority beneficiaries in other parts of the world. we have already received a warning that there is a proposal coming that member states will want an early repayment of eur 10 billion of the 2001 surplus. that is one tenth of the total budget. this surplus is mainly due to the failure of the structural funds to implement their programmes. we believe that this must lead to a strong increase in pressure, both on the commission and on the member states, to take the problem of implementation seriously. this is really about trust in the eu as an institution. this has led us to try to implement a slightly different budgetary process. we're trying to get the committees on board in a completely different way than we've done in the past. this is done, for example, through public hearings with commissioners and by each committee reviewing its share of the budget and monitoring its implementation. this will involve the whole european parliament in the budgetary process in a more active way than before. the budget debate will also be a little different. during the july part-session we will have a debate which will be largely about implementation, but which will take place at the same time as the debate on the mandate for conciliation negotiations on the council's first reading. in the committee on budgets we had a lot of amendments, 129 in particular. the reason for this is that we have managed to reach a consensus on the main priorities. we fully agree that the main political priority is enlargement. for us, the more concrete question is the preparations we must now make to achieve this historic task. enlargement itself does not create any problems for the 2003 budget, but we are still affected in different ways. we must begin the debate on the long-term effects of enlargement, and this is where the european parliament wants to be involved. the agreements now being reached in the negotiations with the member states will affect the budget for a very long time to come. this is therefore a matter of great concern to the european parliament as well. we must also have a debate on the preparations for accession. this applies, for example, to pre-accession aid in category 7 and to the costs we ourselves incur for administrative expenditure to prepare interpretation, translation, buildings and premises needed for enlargement. the most pressing problem is, of course, the administrative expenditure in category 5. if nothing radical is done, we risk hitting the budget ceiling as early as 2003 and, moreover, there is a real risk that the ceiling will be greatly exceeded in 2004. we are therefore trying to put pressure on the secretaries-general of our institutions to come forward with concrete proposals for cooperation and savings. if we fail to do so, we will have a discussion about either painful re-prioritization or the introduction of entirely new funds. i am convinced that no one in this parliament wants to jeopardise the timetable for enlargement for such reasons. changes such as increased cooperation and rationalization are, moreover, entirely in line with the ideas we ourselves promote in terms of reforming the institutions of the union, reforming the commission, etc. the eu's external and aid policy role will also be given great emphasis. in this respect, we risk this year further exacerbating the problem which dominated last year's debate, namely that of constantly adding new tasks such as the balkans, afghanistan, the global health fund and the common foreign and security policy to our traditional priorities. finally, it becomes unreasonable to constantly push new tasks under unchanged budget ceilings, especially as the old tasks are often linked to concrete commitments to beneficiaries in other parts of the world. although we have not taken a position on the commission's proposal for a new flexibility instrument, it is clear that we welcome the debate on this instrument or, alternatively, a debate on a more planned use of the so-called emergency reserve. i would also like to mention a couple of different areas where we take a very aggressive stance. as regards category 3, internal expenditure, following the barcelona process, it is now possible to use funds much more actively for transeuropean networks, for the development of small businesses, etc. i think that's incredibly important. we are also trying to push for the midterm review of the agricultural policy to lead to real changes. in conclusion, i would like to thank my co-rapporteur, mr stenmarck. there are unusually many nordic speakers this year and the budget will be finalised during the danish presidency. in that spirit, i would just like to recall what the then president of the court of auditors, mr jan o. karlsson, said in his last speech here before he resigned as president. he said that the most important thing you could do with the eu budget was to make it simpler, more straightforward and clearer to the citizens. i hope we can take these words to heart.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. let me also begin by thanking my co-rapporteur, mr göran färm, for the good cooperation he has shown so far. the guidelines for the administrative budget which we now have to adopt have three priorities. firstly, there is the question of enlargement. this is the main priority and the main challenge for the whole of the eu, and therefore it is also the main thing for which the european parliament and the other institutions have to prepare. secondly, it is a question of institutional reforms which will be absolutely necessary in an enlarged union which will soon go from 15 to 25 member states. thirdly, it is a question of completing enlargement and institutional reforms within the budgetary framework agreed in the berlin financial perspective in 1999. we must respect these boundaries and manage the eu budget within them. since we have a deficit of around 130 to 140 million euros so far, we need a strategy for how we deal with this. in the short term, this is entirely possible. it is about cutting back on all the institutions. this is also the main message i have the opportunity to convey in all my meetings and contacts with the various eu institutions. it gives them an opportunity to make their priorities with respect to the fact that they know best what can be done in their areas. if they are not willing to do this, we have to make the priorities, but then they may not be as good. in the longer term, more systemic changes in the budget are needed to deal with ever-increasing deficits. we had an informal trialogue in madrid a little over a week ago. there is no doubt that there is, at least in words, a widespread understanding both in the commission and in the council of the scale of the problem. nevertheless, the commission is clearly prepared to seek the simplest possible solution, namely to allocate additional money to category 5 through the flexibility instrument. it is worth stressing that this is a solution with an extremely high number of shortcomings. for all of us who do not work in an anonymous commission but who must meet our electors on a daily basis, it should be impossible to look those electors in the eye and at the same time explain that the eu needs more money to hire more bureaucrats in brussels. it just can't be done! against this background it is of the utmost importance that the european parliament should show firmness and consistency in its position and in its call on all the institutions to contribute to budget cuts. let me say a few words about the main priorities of the report. in the context of enlargement, a number of issues are particularly important. firstly, it is a question of language, of the right of every member of this parliament to be treated in the same way as everyone else. there are really only two possibilities. either we all speak a common language or each of us speaks his own language. if i, as a swede, have the right to speak swedish and a portuguese has the right to speak portuguese, then a hungarian must be allowed to speak hungarian and a lithuanian to speak lithuanian. the committee on budgets has supported what the podestà group calls controlled multilingualism. at the same time, we call on the working group to revise its proposal and reach its conclusions in time for the adoption of the 2003 budget. the enlargement will also entail necessary investments in buildings, offices and interpreting boxes. all this is particularly difficult because we do not know the extent of enlargement. we will not do so until the copenhagen summit in december. if enlargement is to start with ten countries in january 2004, we have a tremendous amount of work to do in just 21 months. in fact, we have even less time than that, because the new member states will have the right to send observers as soon as the agreements are signed. that might give us a year or so. at this time, there is still a great deal to be done. that is why the action to be taken must be of the highest priority.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it has taken a long time to get this report to the plenary, because religion and sexuality are undoubtedly two very sensitive issues to deal with even in the committee on women's rights. we have now come this far, and i would like to thank the colleagues who have made a great effort to bring some new amendments to the table. i would like to thank you in particular for the courage you have shown in taking up the role of saudi arabia, something which would otherwise be welcome, since we could say that we are living in an era of neoliberal economic fundamentalism. there are many different kinds of fundamentalism both outside and within the borders of the european union. i cannot help but mention last week's scandal, one of many scandals over the years. this time it was the united states. the catholic church has sheltered its representatives, who for a generation have sexually abused children in the parishes. the worst thing is when this kind of avatars are also protected by law. i am thinking of the current cases in nigeria, where women are sentenced to death by stoning for having had sex outside marriage. i am also thinking of the trials in portugal of women who have had abortions and of those who have helped them to have them, and of the recent referendum on the same issue in ireland. these are just a few examples of many that show the self-imposed right of the man to control women and children as his personal property. this is often done in the name of religion and often with the protection of the law.
