mr president, i would like to thank you. two types of arguments have been put forward in favour of this directive. one was on public health, the other on the harmonization of the internal market. on the public health front, i think the argument is weak. it's not a major public health problem that people overdosed on vitamins and minerals. nor does this directive eliminate the risk of overdose. if we really want to make a powerful contribution to public health, perhaps alcohol, tobacco or something else should be mentioned rather than the area covered by this directive. the fact is also that the eu cannot introduce any harmonisation in the area of public health. this is clearly stated in the eu treaties. there is also a risk that consumers using certain preparations in certain doses may find it more difficult to do so once this directive has entered into force. the real motive behind the directive is harmonisation, i.e. the possibility of being able to sell freely everywhere. to me, that's not a strong argument. i mean, other considerations must sometimes take precedence over a free market. i think that different eu countries should be allowed to have different rules in such areas, depending on what the voters want: more liberal or more restrictive. i believe that supranational rules should only be used to combat genuinely supranational common problems. that is not the case this time. we've witnessed a furious and rather unpleasant lobbying campaign. its methods and content have been counterproductive. several of the arguments against the proposal have been both exaggerated and sometimes incorrect. some of the elements of this proposal are positive, such as the requirement for declarations of contents. however, overall there is no strong case for the implementation of the far-reaching harmonisation proposed, and there is also a risk that the system devised favours large industrial interests at the expense of small businesses. (applause) i would like to thank you.
i would like to begin by expressing my disapproval of the very aggressive campaign which we members and, in particular, the rapporteur, mr müller, have been subjected to in the course of this debate. of course, everyone has the democratic right to argue for what they believe and to try to influence decision-makers and decisions, but in this case the line of what is acceptable has actually been crossed. i am one of those who wish to reject the common position. it is not that i am always against regulation or harmonization in the field of public health, but that in this particular case i do not think it desirable or necessary to harmonize national legislation. as mr blokland said, different countries have very different traditions, and i am sure that no one has eaten himself to death on vitamins or minerals. the problem with the directive is, in particular, article 5, which concerns the fixing of the maximum daily intake. the european parliament will have no say in or influence over how it is to be determined, but it will most likely be by committee. another problem is the annex which specifies which vitamins and minerals are to be included in the dietary supplement. i am very concerned about the ability of small firms and manufacturers to establish themselves on the market. many companies and products will disappear and, most importantly, consumer choice will be reduced. today we are exposed to stress and a host of environmental toxins and chemicals that make a varied and healthy diet not always sufficient to keep us healthy and well. we need to supplement our bodies with vitamins and minerals, sometimes in high doses, to balance the functions of the body. good nutritional knowledge, mr president, could also prevent disease in all contexts. we should encourage people to take care of themselves and to take personal responsibility for their own health. this directive sends the wrong signals.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the eu's internal market is a cornerstone of european cooperation. it has brought europe free trade, competitiveness and prosperity. however, there is no market need to harmonise the rules for the marketing of vitamin and mineral preparations. member states have different traditions in this area. in sweden, as in england and ireland, individuals in the open market can buy several preparations up to the maximum recommended daily intake. this tradition must be respected and precede the requirement for uniform rules. what is the real reason behind the proposal to regulate the marketing of vitamin and mineral supplements at eu level? opponents blame the pharmaceutical industry for wanting to sell more pills. the regulators also point to the pharmaceutical industry, but in this case as opposing the proposal because it wants to sell even larger volumes. personally, i think both are wrong. i have not met any lobbyist from the pharmaceutical industry on this issue. the main driver behind the regulatory overload is, instead, an overprotective mentality among eu officials and politicians. of course, it is inappropriate to overdose on vitamins and minerals, but the margin between overdose and harmful levels is large. with the help of information labels on the jars, most people can manage their daily vitamin intake on their own. such trust we should have in the individual. i'm a dialysis doctor myself, and i've seen many cases of poisoning. i've seen two younger women who've been poisoned by nutmeg and an older man who was severely poisoned by nicotine, but i've never met anyone with vitamin or mineral poisoning. kidney stone patients should be restrictive on much more than just vitamin c and calcium. it is the duty of roth-behrendt's physician to inform her of what she should be careful of. doctors have more important responsibilities than prescribing vitamins and minerals at doses higher than those accepted as dietary supplements.
the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. i would like to thank everyone for the very, very good cooperation that has been given to me during the nearly one and a half years that i have been in charge of these two reports. it's been going really well. in the common position, the council and the commission have accepted the majority of the more than 100 amendments which we tabled at first reading. it is a great pleasure for me to see that the council and the commission are now in agreement with the european parliament on the basic principles. the first and most important principle is that only animal by-products that are authorised for human consumption may be used in feed, cosmetics and pharmaceutical products. i say this with a little grandness, referring to the previous debate. we will also have a ban on the re-use of species, i.e. animal cannibalism. the new regulation will in fact be flexible enough to adapt to technical progress and new scientific experience. the common position has indeed been welcomed by the industry concerned in europe. i would ask you to bear in mind that, at the final vote tomorrow, 200 000 jobs across europe are at stake. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to finance the necessary measures. every day that passes without clear rules of the game puts more and more smes at risk of being forced to close down. i would say that, to put it a little more bluntly, europe's producers and consumers are standing around, stamping their feet, while we are here in the endgame, arguing over a ham sandwich. let me first talk about amendment no 25, which is a compromise. as is always the case with compromises, it means that no one has gotten exactly what they want. we have all had to give in a good deal, and i am a little uncertain as to what the final result of this compromise will be. the amendment, however, gives a transitional period of four years to member states which currently allow the use of food waste in extremely tightly controlled forms. towards the end of the case, a number of details concerning sludge and waste water have emerged. amendments nos 2, 4 and 14 should in fact fall in favour of amendment no 21, which aims at the safe handling of these materials in order to avoid serious environmental consequences. so that's a practical way of dealing with this. i would very much like to see amendments nos 3 and 5 made possible. unfortunately, they're technically quite impossible. these amendments concern the labelling of category 1 material. the tse regulation already adopted contains clear requirements for the labelling of direct risk material. amendment no 5 concerns the labelling of category 2 material. i think that would be desirable, but that the amendment should be rejected because it would have unreasonable consequences. if the amendment is adopted, all fertilisers, natural fertilisers within the eu, would have to be labelled, and this applies to some 1 200 000 000 tonnes per year. that means we could choose whether the soil would be blue, green or purple in the spring, and it would all look rather strange. amendments nos 6 and 16 must indeed be adopted. otherwise, the main principle of the report, that only raw materials which have been authorised as food may be used in feed, is undermined. the amendments do not in fact alter the requirements currently applicable to the sampling and/or post-mortem inspection of slaughter animals. only the final assessment based on existing rules is affected by this regulation. amendments nos 7 and 8 concern the management of food waste. in that case, i recommend voting in favour of amendments nos 22 and 23, which require that food waste from international traffic, with all its risks, be handled in accordance with the strictest requirements of the regulation, category 1. however, category 3 food waste must be handled in existing structures and systems for collection, transport and disposal in accordance with the eu waste directive. the common position contains a somewhat strange proposal concerning endangered species of birds, the so-called lamb-eaters in spain. amendment no 24 states that the use of category 1 material is allowed for feeding this specific small group of birds. i've actually checked this out very carefully. these birds must have this special food. it sounds a little odd, but nature is by its very nature odd from time to time. amendment no 12 on the permanent labelling of imported pet food is particularly controversial for the united states. as regards the colouring of raw materials in categories 1 and 2, it is provided that products in categories 1 and 2 are to be labelled once they have been converted into fats and processed proteins. such processed material must then be permanently dyed in imported pet food, since the exact same requirements must be imposed on imported products as on our own products. amendment no 15 concerns the separation of establishments. the european parliament initially demanded completely separate plots. i can agree to separate buildings if they're really separate not just separate parts of a building under the same roof. there are also a number of amendments which were rejected by the committee and which should be rejected again for various reasons. this concerns the deletion of references to biogas and composting. this regulation does not regulate biogas plants and composting systems. on the contrary, article 6 (2) (g) states that national legislation in this area will apply pending new eu environmental legislation. the only point of the reference in article 1 (2) is to give the commission a legal basis to request scientific opinions for biogas and composting. if we exclude household waste, i.e. from private kitchens, from the definition of food waste, we are doing ourselves a disservice. we know that food waste is a very serious route of transmission for both swine fever and foot-and-mouth disease. personally, i find it rather difficult to ban household waste as feed. it has been a tradition for thousands of years in our european culture, but we are now living in a new world and in a different time where food, feed and ingredients are moved around in a very different way than, say, 50 years ago when feed and food circulated in relatively small and recognisable cycles. i would like to thank my colleagues, the co-rapporteurs, the commission and the council once again for the excellent cooperation that has been maintained over the past year and a half.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, marit paulsen, for the excellent work she has done in getting this regulation on health rules for animal by-products through. i also know that staes is pleased with the cooperation and the way in which compromises have been reached and with paulsen's work with the co-rapporteur. thank you for that! this is a very technical and complex matter that can be difficult to get into. at the same time, it is obvious that we must guarantee safe food for the citizens of the eu, and then we must do the right thing throughout the chain. so, of course, how we treat animals and what we feed them is important. i am very pleased with the principles agreed by the european parliament and the council, namely that only animal by-products which are authorised for human consumption may be used as feed. perhaps the most important thing is that we should also prohibit the reuse of the species, that is to say, we should reject cannibalism. i understand the council's position that it wants to ban food waste in animal feed. it is difficult to guarantee safe and risk-free management of food waste. how can we know what a mixed waste actually consists of? we have now tabled compromise number 22, which makes an exception for category 3 food waste, and we table amendment no 25, which allows certain member states to dispose of food waste for a transitional period of up to four years. i am not entirely in favour of this amendment, but i am still willing to accept it in order to get the report through. at present there is a kind of moratorium on the use of bone and meat meal as animal feed. this means that the council could decide at any time to re-authorize the use of meat and bone meal. that is why the greens/efa group tabled amendment no 20. i ask my colleagues to vote in favour of it, because it does give a kind of assurance that the council will have to take up the matter again before any decisions can be taken in that direction.
mr president, i would like to thank you. when we stood in front of the television on november 9, 1989, watching people who were against democracy throw themselves over the berlin wall, we all felt the wings of history. that same night the process of enlargement, or the reunification of europe, as the liberal group prefers to call it, was launched. we are now on the threshold of creating a united europe, a european union for the whole continent. of course, it is nothing less than a historical sensation. but now we must go beyond rhetoric and turn to hard facts: money, agriculture and old border conflicts. who said it was gonna be easy? who thought it wouldn't cost anything? who would have thought we wouldn't have to make sacrifices? i know that enlargement comes at a price, but it's worth it. of course, there is still a lot to be worked out, but we must not drown in details. 2002 is a very important year for enlargement, but it is also an election year in six member states and three candidate countries. the european issues will be important, but there is a very high risk that domestic political considerations will overshadow the european issues and enlargement. this is why strong european leadership and action by the commission is needed to drive the process forward during these crucial months. 2002 must not be a lost year. the enlargement cannot be delayed.
the rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. it is not quite so simple that these amendments nos 1 and 10 on the one hand and amendment no 25 on the other are somehow identical. the oral amendment of amendments nos 1 and 10 is not an editorial change but would upset fundamental principles in the whole investigation and make it extremely difficult to reach a compromise. i cannot accept this oral amendment. i ask my colleagues to vote on amendment no 25, which opens up possibilities for solving the particular problems of both germany and the united kingdom with household waste and used household oil. i mean, i do not accept the oral amendment!
in writing. -sv) i voted against the report because i believe that it has no solid legal basis as the eu has no legislative power in matters of public health. the question of food supplements is a matter for national legislation, not for the european union. the eu has also failed to take any initiative to change the legislation on the misuse of very dangerous substances, such as alcohol and narcotics. now, attempting to do this in the public health sector, with the implications of this for the self-medication of individuals through vitamin supplements, raises a lot of fundamental questions. for whose benefit? why only in this area? how does this affect the ordinary citizen? what are the industries affected and those favoured? these questions call for answers which, so far, have proved unsatisfactory.
in writing. -{v) the report on "women and fundamentalism" raises many important questions. it is undeniable that in many parts of the world women's rights are being trampled on and oppressed in the name of so-called religious, political, or ideological fundamentalism. as christian democrats we strongly oppose these violations of human rights and condemn the way women have been and are still being treated, as was the case under the taliban regime in afghanistan. however, the report contains such important shortcomings that we were ultimately unable to support it. for example, there is no definition of the term fundamentalism. even if one can assume that to some extent it is about cruel and repressive regimes or a form of religious faith, for example, this does not explain the fact that the report makes a distinction between what should be called fundamentalism, views of religion and religious communities in general. the report repeatedly states that religion is a private matter and that religious communities "should not interfere in the public and political life of states". we believe in a vibrant civil society, including churches, and we don't want to prevent anyone from taking part in the public debate.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank trentin for his interesting report. i interpret the report as meaning that, while it contains a clear commitment to stability policy, it also contains, most characteristically, attempts to find ways out, to create openings, to relax the rules and to achieve greater freedom of action. i also think that the amendments tabled by the socialist members are moving in that direction, that is to say towards creating a little more room for expansion and innovation. this is positive. i believe that europe's economy, which is still characterised by high unemployment, growing social problems and stagnation in important areas such as education and research, needs more expansion, dynamism and innovation. if you follow the economic policy debate in the european union, it is very much about rules, controls, prohibitions, coordination, coordination, discipline and the like. in fact, it does not sound at all like we are discussing a liberal market economy, but rather a rather bureaucratic and heavy economy. i think that instead we should invest heavily in stimulating the economy, in public investment in education and research, which is really needed if we are to achieve the lisbon objectives. we must also make substantial environmental improvements, and we must, as mr markov pointed out, strengthen purchasing power, create demand so that companies are encouraged to invest again, so that we can focus on quality and on actions that help us to meet the ambitious lisbon objectives. i'm afraid this is not what i see. what i see is something like a straitjacket, marked by stability, rules and discipline. it's not enough.
i thank the president-in-office for his full reply. i believe that these large export subsidies are fundamentally unsolidarist and are destroying the agricultural market in large parts of the third world. a fair trade policy can only be achieved when subsidies are abolished. i have two follow-up questions: the first concerns the widespread fraud in export subsidies, which has been pointed out by the court of auditors, among others. what measures are in place to get to the cheating? the second is whether, on the basis of the presidency's statement, i can assume that the spanish presidency, once the commission has presented its proposal, will support substantial reductions in export subsidies.
thank you to the spanish presidency for its reply! a few follow-up questions: does the presidency welcome the fact that the commission and the council have reached different conclusions on the budget deficits of portugal and germany? is there merit in having different opinions? how does the spanish presidency view the warning given to ireland a year ago about tax cuts in that country to meet the concrete election promises of the irish government? was that a legitimate warning in view of the way portugal and germany are being treated? how do you see the credibility of the stability pact in the light of the weakening of the euro as a world currency, not least against the dollar, which has fallen from 1.30 to 0.90? finally, mr president-in-office, do you consider that this decision is prejudicial?
- the agenda calls for the following reports from the committee on economic and monetary affairs to be discussed jointly: - a5-0060/2002 by mr lipietz, on the proposal for a directive on the supplementary supervision of credit institutions, insurance undertakings and investment firms belonging to a financial conglomerate (com (2001) 213 - c5-0159/2001 - 2001/0095 (cod)) - a5-0069/2002 by mr goebbels, on the proposal for a directive on insider dealing and market manipulation (market abuse) (com (2001) 281 - c5-0262/2001 - 2001/0118 (cod)) - a-005-72/2002 by mr huhne, on the proposal for a directive on the prospectus to be published when securities are offered to the public or are admitted to trading (com (2001) 280 - c0526/2001 (cod)) - c3-0267/2001 (cod))
commission. – madam president, this is a joint debate on three subjects. i should like to start with the report prepared by mr lipietz. i begin by commending him on that excellent report. he has mastered a proposal which is both technically complex and economically important. i can therefore accept the following amendments without reservation: amendments nos 2, 3, 7 to 10; amendment no 9 as corrected by amendment no 50; nos 18 to 30, 35, 36, 40 and 42. these are welcome reinforcements and real clarifications of the proposal. in the same way there are a number of amendments which i can accept in spirit. they go a long way in the right direction but need some refinement to avoid confusion or possible contradiction. amendment no 6 could be improved by combining the elements in amendment no 9 to give a good set of macro-economic criteria to determine which groups are covered by the directive. amendment no 17 clarifies how to cover special group structures. that is also a welcome clarification but the wording could be tightened up. amendment no 31 requires the commission to consult industry. i agree. but the amendment seems to us to be too limited. it restricts consultation to a handful of brussels federations. the commission is in favour of as wide and as representative a consultation as possible. i think that parliament would also consider that necessary. amendments nos 32 to 34, 44, 46 and 48 seek to amend what should be deducted from capital to prevent the so-called double-gearing of capital and artificially inflate a company's capital base. i agree with the spirit of those amendments. the 20% threshold for insurance deductions appears to be a good compromise. the proposed wording just needs some improvement to ensure the overall coherence of the text. but amendments nos 43, 45 and 47, which completely contradict amendments nos 44, 46 and 48, are then not acceptable. there are only a few amendments which i am unfortunately unable to accept. firstly, amendments that significantly narrow the scope of the directive: namely amendments nos 4, 5, 11, 12 and 49. they will limit the measures to groups with a parent/subsidiary relationship only. that does not reflect reality. there are groups that are organised on a different basis which would then escape supervision, for example, horizontal cooperatives or mutual groups such as rabo bank in the netherlands or the dg bank in germany. these amendments also exclude anyone with close links to a conglomerate. after the spanish banesto case we all know how important it is to include such cases in our supervision, in particular for intra-group transactions. a second group of amendments which i unfortunately cannot support concern the calculation of capital adequacy. they are amendments nos 15, 16, 37 and 39. i can accept moving the substance from the annex to the articles of the directive but i cannot support the additional substantive changes on the definition of capital. they would fundamentally undermine the capital standards for our industry. firstly, the english text of amendment no 16 changes one word in the original proposal and consequently opens the door to acceptance of all types of capital to cover risks, irrespective of the quality of that capital. i understand that this is a typing error – these things sometimes happen – but i should like confirmation that the french and german texts, which leave the original text unchanged, are the correct text. secondly, i cannot accept including minority interests in group capital – that is, the last sentence of amendment no 15 – if all the risks in the group are not also covered. the third group of amendments which i cannot support are those that give our financial groups the freedom to choose the way in which they calculate their capital adequacy. this concerns amendments nos 1, 13, 14, 38 and 51. that is the role and responsibility of our public authorities. they cannot abdicate this to the industry through self-regulation, and i agree that the authorities should discuss and agree with each group how its capital adequacy should be calculated. i am sure that we can work out an acceptable compromise, but i ask for your understanding if i say that these amendments go too far. in conclusion on this point, this is a most welcome and supportive report. i should like to underline that. with a few exceptions which i have mentioned, it really contributes to a rapid agreement and i am confident that it will be possible to work out acceptable compromises on the following amendments: nos 1, 4, 5, 11 to 16, 37 to 39, 49 and 51. progress is being made in the council and agreement between the institutions appears quite close. on the second issue, which is the report prepared by mr goebbels, may i begin by saying that very good progress has been made on this dossier. first of all i should like to thank the rapporteur, mr goebbels, very much indeed for all the efforts he has made while working under heavy pressure, as we all do, incidentally. it is a very complex and sensitive dossier and i should like to applaud the fact that the rapporteur has resolutely defended the aim of this directive, namely to enhance substantially the integrity of european financial markets. i should also like to thank the shadow rapporteur and the members of the committee on monetary affairs for their readiness to proceed as quickly as possible. i know we all agree that the european union has no truck with greedy financial cheats. we want stable, transparent, integrated and efficient european markets for all consumers and investors. this directive must contribute to increasing investor protection and to making european financial markets surer and more attractive in future. it must dispel the international concerns expressed over the last few years about insider dealing and manipulation on european markets. i am certain all members are aware that recent events have shown how important those issues are. it is not only the attacks of 11 september that have proven the necessity of high market protection. scandals such as the enron business, which has been referred to at various times tonight, clearly show the need for rules making markets safer and ensuring that they remain free of abuse and fraud. the smooth functioning of financial markets and public confidence in them are the conditions for sustained economic growth and wealth. market abuse increases the costs for financing companies, harms market integrity and dissuades investors. we must do everything to stamp it out. with this directive, we ensure high market integrity, and establish common standards against market abuse throughout europe, making european financial markets more attractive in future. the commission can agree with all the amendments that mr goebbels has proposed in his report, except those additionally proposed by mrs echerer, amendments nos 75 and 76, by mr lehne, amendments nos 88 and 89, and by mr herzog, amendment no 87. but the commission still has doubts about amendment no 2 regarding comitology. firstly, this amendment is not necessary, given that the commission has already accepted amendment no 1, which makes a clear reference to the declaration of president prodi at the last part-session of this parliament on 5 february and to my letter of 2 october 2001 to the chairperson of the committee on economic affairs, mrs randzio-plath. furthermore, during the debate on the report by lord inglewood concerning international accounting standards last monday, i once again underlined our full commitment to this important declaration regarding the implementation of financial services legislation. secondly, the amendment does not reflect all the elements that have been agreed between our institutions, and there are some differences between the wording of the declaration of president prodi and the wording of amendment no 2 which might result in misinterpretations. furthermore – and this is an important point – i would say to mr goebbels that i am not certain whether the different wordings would be agreed by the council. i cannot speak on behalf of the council, but it may well entail difficulties which we would be well advised to avoid. the commission would prefer, therefore, to stick to what has previously been agreed. let me state unequivocally that the commission will fully respect – and to the letter – the commitments it has made in its declaration in plenary, as well as in my own letter to mrs randzio-plath. should plenary adopt the amendments adopted by the committee on economic and monetary affairs, together with some of the additional amendments proposed for today's sitting, i very much hope that the council will be able to accept this text as well, so we would have only one reading. that would be a major achievement for the integration of european financial markets by the year 2005 to which we all aspire, as well as a sign of confidence for the barcelona european council. i hope that all members present here and those voting tomorrow will agree and adopt that directive. lastly, we come to the report prepared by mr huhne concerning the directive on prospectuses. may i again begin by thanking mr huhne for his report. many amendments have been proposed and they result in clarification of our proposal. good work has been done to improve the wording on eurobonds, which we broadly support. we can accept 46 amendments: amendments nos 1, 3, 4, 7 to 9, 13, 18, 19, 22 to 26, 29, 30, 38, 39, 41, 43, 44 to 56, 58 to 61, 63, 64, 66 to 72 and 75, but we may have to redraft some of them to ensure the overall coherence of the text. i am worried, however, by some points which parliament may adopt. firstly, i am concerned that, in the aftermath of enron, parliament may even weaken the fundamentals of current eu legislation, i.e. legislation which is now in force, namely, that companies wanting to raise capital among the public have to disclose information in a prospectus approved by the competent authority. that is what amendment no 35 is about. is this parliament really prepared to support an amendment to the effect that more than 75% of all companies listed in europe should be able to raise capital without publishing a prospectus? mrs berès made that same point a little while ago. that would mean that for some eu markets all but one or two companies would be exempted. how do we explain that to european investors? for the commission, the prospectus proposal has two aims: to facilitate fundraising for european issuers, and to ensure an adequate level of investor protection. both these aims are equally important. investor protection has been neglected too often in the discussion. the second cause for worry is that some of the amendments proposed will result in more fragmentation, not less. we must agree what is good for the eu as a whole, and we do not want any tinkering with national systems. let me be clear on this point, and let me be clear to this house, that my colleagues and i will spare no effort to reach an agreement with parliament on this crucial text for a single capital market, but we must resolve those problems. let me now briefly address possible solutions to some of the most important political issues. on small and medium-sized enterprises the commission is against their total exemption from the proposal, but it is willing to find a cost-effective solution that would ensure that smes should have access to capital markets at the right price and under the right levels of regulation with the necessary investor protection. we have always advocated adapting the disclosure rules to the specific nature of smes and we would be ready to contemplate an exemption for smes from the obligation annually to update the prospectus, as proposed by amendment no 71. as regards eurobonds we agree with the rapporteur's idea to exempt eurobonds with a high minimum denomination but we consider that the level proposed – eur 50,000 – is too low. we therefore support amendments nos 66 and 67, as it seems to the commission that good work has been done here. as regards the annual updating of the prospectus, we might contemplate exempting smes but we shall certainly stay firm as regards big companies, not least because 300 of our largest companies listed in the united states already comply with this obligation. our investors should have the same disclosure as united states investors. i am certain we all agree that our investors are not inferior to the american ones. lastly, regarding the competent authority, we want to retain our position of one administrative authority per member state. we are ready to clarify that a delegation of powers is possible provided that the ultimate responsibility remains with the single administrative competent authority. we do not agree with the free choice of the regulator which might lead to the famous "race to the bottom", but we would be ready to consider some flexibility for issuers of debt securities and those working out of third countries. to sum up, the commission rejects 29 amendments. they are amendments nos 2, 5, 6, 10 to 12, 14 to 17, 20, 21, 27, 28, 31 to 37, 40, 42, 57, 62, 65, 73, 74 and 76. regarding amendment no 10, we accept the amendments on comitology because they reflect our agreement on that matter, but we consider that amendment no 10 is redundant. the situation is the same as for amendment no 2, on the report by mr goebbels, so i do not have to repeat the whole argument. we consider amendment no 10 to be redundant given amendment no 1, which refers to the resolution of parliament and my letter to mrs randzio-plath.
- thank you, commissioner bolkestein. - i'll see you in a few. the joint discussion is closed. the vote will take place tomorrow at 12 noon.
- the next item is the joint debate on two reports from the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy: - a5-0061/2002 by mr pérez royo, on the proposal for a directive to ensure effective taxation within the community of savings income in the form of interest payments (com (2001) 400 - c5-0402/2001 - 2001/0164 (cns)) - a5-0048/2002 by mr della vedova, on the communication on the tax policy of the european union - priorities for the coming years (com (2001) 260 - c5-0597/2001 - 2001/2248 (cos)).
commission. – madam president, i should also like to thank mr della vedova for his report on the commission's communications, on our general tax policy and on company taxation in the internal market. i should also like to thank mr pérez royo for his report on the proposal for a directive on the taxation of savings. i should like to begin with the report of mr della vedova, which i very much welcome. it may help if i recall briefly the commission's motivation for producing these two communications. individual member states retain, quite correctly, responsibility for many aspects of national tax policy. but there is an ongoing demand for an eu tax policy in many areas. a key question is: what degree of harmonisation is appropriate? a high degree of harmonisation is necessary, it seems to the commission, in the field of indirect taxes. such taxes can create an immediate obstacle to the free movement of goods and the free supply of services within the internal market. indeed a certain degree of harmonisation in that area has already taken place. i am very conscious that this parliament has had its differences with the commission on the issue of rates of excise duty on tobacco and alcohol – an issue dear to the heart of mrs lulling. it is clear, however, that the existing wide rate divergences between member states constitute a strong incentive for both fraudulent transactions and legitimate cross-border shopping which lead both to distortion of competition and diversion of trade. i am therefore of the opinion that the permanent solution for these problems requires enhanced approximation of rates to a level at which the above effects no longer occur or are brought back to a sustainable level. on the other hand, there is no need to harmonise personal income taxes unless they entail discrimination or double taxation. but there is an intermediate zone of direct taxation of mobile tax bases – in particular the taxation of companies and capital – where the situation is less clear-cut and which may have direct effects on the internal market. the so-called tax package, of which the directive on savings taxation is an important element, seeks to address the potential erosion of tax revenues and eliminate harmful tax competition. but to achieve a balance in european tax policy, attention should also be paid to the concerns of taxpayers, both individuals and companies. that means addressing obstacles that hinder the exercise of the fundamental freedoms guaranteed by the treaty. this is where our communication devoted to company taxation comes in. in that communication we review the tax obstacles faced by companies who operate cross-border in the european union. we have identified a significant number of those obstacles, which, we must all agree is astonishing, given the fact that it is nearly 10 years since the internal market was established. we have also identified potential solutions. we have examined a number of possible solutions to this problem in the form of both targeted and comprehensive approaches. the latter would, in the longer term, provide companies with a consolidated, corporate tax base for their eu-wide activities. naturally i am extremely grateful for the support given to this principle by mr della vedova in his report. in pursuing the various objectives i have just outlined in relation to taxation at eu level, the commission will need to employ a variety of methods, including non-legislative mechanisms. again, i am very grateful for the unqualified support for this policy in mr della vedova's report. i now turn to the proposal for a directive on the taxation of savings. firstly, i am pleased to note that a substantial number of the proposed amendments correspond to changes agreed by of the ecofin council in the draft directive approved on 13 december 2001, for the purpose of negotiations with third countries. the large measure of agreement between the council and parliament on the text of the proposal cannot but strengthen the community's negotiating position in the discussion with third countries. the commission takes the view that the amendments agreed by the council on 13 december improve and clarify the legal texts, but that they do not substantially change the proposal for a directive. the commission therefore considers that these amendments do not justify presenting a revised proposal for a directive given the particular time constraints that we are operating under. a number of the amendments proposed by the committee on economic and monetary affairs related to the negotiations with third countries and member states' dependent and associated territories, and more particularly to parliament's role in these discussions. parliament may rest assured that i intend to keep parliament fully informed of these negotiations and that i have no intention whatsoever of undermining parliament's prerogatives. i fully share the view expressed by mr pérez royo that this proposal has an important international dimension. this is exactly why the commission asked for and obtained authorisation from the council to enter into negotiations with the six third countries mentioned in the faro european council conclusions. they are: the united states, switzerland, andorra, liechtenstein, monaco and san marino. the aim of these negotiations is to secure the adoption of measures equivalent to the system applied within the community. at the same time, member states are committed to promoting the adoption of the same measures by their own dependent and associated territories. the discussions with third countries and dependent territories should hopefully be finalised before the summer. i must emphasise that the faro agreement provides that these negotiations with third countries and dependent and associated territories should precede the adoption of the directive. the commission cannot therefore accept amendments that would make the application of the directive, once adopted, dependent on the outcome of such negotiations. there is a risk that such amendments might undermine our negotiating position vis-à-vis the third countries who quite rightly want to be sure that member states will implement the directive if they agree to apply equivalent measures. on the particular point raised by mrs thyssen, the discussions with third countries should precede adoption of the directive. those agreements are a political condition for adoption of the directive. therefore we have to live with that. in closing, i once again thank parliament for its support for the proposed directive on taxational savings. i assure parliament that i shall keep it informed of developments to the best of my abilities.
i would like to thank you, commissioner bolkestein. mrs lulling has the floor for a point of order.
thank you very much, mrs. thyssen. i think it would be good if the honourable members could meet afterwards to talk. the joint discussion is closed. the vote will take place tomorrow at 12 noon.
- the joint debate on the report (a5-0409/2001) by mr carnero gonzàles, on behalf of the committee on constitutional affairs, on the legal personality of the european union (ini 2001/2021).
in writing. - (sv) we are critical of the report by mr bautista ojeda on nuts and carob. we believe that the various amendments tabled lead in general to an outdated agricultural policy which does not meet the requirements and needs of today's global society. in view of this and in view of the imminent general review of the eu's agricultural policy, we have chosen to vote against the report as a whole.
in writing. -sv) the programme to support measures to improve the market for nuts, apricots and hazelnuts has made it possible to modernise production. this modernisation was justified by the fact that the sector is a major employer in certain regions of the mediterranean area. however, aid for modernisation cannot last forever. it is entirely justified to include these sectors in the general regime for the fruit and vegetables market. it is not normal, however, as the commission proposes, to create a permanent aid system in order to guarantee the survival of the sector. the commission must instead complete its analysis of the possibilities for competition in production (during the one-year extension of the existing aid scheme envisaged in the commission's proposal) and then decide on the action to be taken.
in writing. - (sv) we are critical of the cuhna report on leaf tobacco. we believe that the various amendments tabled lead in general to an outdated agricultural policy which does not meet the requirements and needs of today's global society. in view of this and in view of the imminent general review of the eu's agricultural policy, we have chosen to vote against the report as a whole.
in writing. - (sv) in the long term, it is unacceptable that money from european citizens should be used to support tobacco cultivation in some member states. it is essential to abolish support for tobacco growing, particularly from the point of view of public health. the high levels of aid are counter to adaptation to other crops. that is why the christian democrats support a gradual reduction in tobacco subsidies, as proposed by the commission.
in writing. -sv) the undersigned voted in favour of the report as a whole because we believe that the eu, for example, should be able to become a party to the european convention on human rights, which requires that the union be given legal personality. we believe that such a development can take place on its own, while maintaining the existing decision-making methods and the current institutional structure. but we do not believe that the three pillars which today make up the ec and the eu should be merged into a single pillar. the polish structure reflects the political distinction between supranational and transnational areas of cooperation, and the fact that the polish structure creates a complex situation from a legal point of view is not in itself a reason for abolishing the structure.
in writing. - sv) i voted against the carnero gonzález report for the following reason: i am in favour of interstate cooperation. the report is clearly supranational in nature. the european union's intergovernmental cooperation is in danger if the union is given legal personality, as the rapporteur proposes. the transformation of the eu into a legal entity is also a further step towards a european federation. i can't accept that.

the commission is aware of the seriousness of the problem and is therefore prepared to take action. i can say that this example will serve as an inspiration for other areas. the belgian initiative is based on a detailed evaluation of the european football championship in 2000, which was supported and financed by the commission's oisin programme and in which the commission participated. we believe that the establishment of national football information points and their close cooperation can be an effective means of preventing and combating hooliganism in football. the central objective is that member states can exchange information on groups of supporters of football teams and their movements to other countries, and also that information can be exchanged on the classification of these groups as peaceful or violent. the main purpose of this exchange of information is to help the countries hosting football matches to prevent possible violent demonstrations and to ensure the safety of all those who are fans of this sport and who want to participate in peace by watching football matches. the innovative aspect of this decision is that, in addition to allowing the continuation of the existing exchange of information, it is legally binding, which means that it is a step in strengthening legal certainty in the treatment of this information. as regards the exchange of information on personal data, care must be taken in its processing and safeguarding, especially in view of the fact that not only do the rules of international law on the protection of privacy apply to such exchanges of information, but that, in the commission's view, we should also integrate this exchange of information into a legal instrument regulating the protection of personal data in all matters relating to the third pillar, that is to say in all matters relating to judicial cooperation. i would conclude by thanking mr deprez for the sportsmanship with which he has devoted himself to this - the next item is the report (a5-0036/2002) by mr kirkhope, on behalf of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs, on the initiative of the kingdom of the netherlands with a view to adopting a council decision establishing a european network of contact points for persons responsible for genocide, crimes against humanity and war crimes (com (2001) 11658 - c5-0499/2001 - 2001/0826 (cns)).
commission. - madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the commission supports the dutch proposal to set up these contact points on the crimes of genocide, because we think it is a reasonable proposal which aims at a lightweight and not costly structure. this proposal does not affect existing legal mechanisms for judicial assistance but is an important complement in that it allows the member states of the european union to fulfil their obligations under the statute of the international criminal court which was established in rome by the 1998 convention and is about to enter into force with the ratification of the required number of member states. cooperation between the member states of the european union in this field, with a view to preventing such crimes, was already the subject of a common position adopted by the council on 11 june 2001. and so it seems to us that the dutch initiative is part of a coherent and consistent approach to these particularly heinous crimes on an international scale. the commission shares the emphasis put by the rapporteur, mr kirkhope, who i would like to congratulate on his excellent report, on the need for such cooperation mechanisms not to affect the guarantees of protection of personal data or to jeopardise the rights of defence of the accused. common rules apply here, from the outset the european convention on human rights and the relevant council of europe conventions, and this is another example of the issues that, together with those which were the subject of mr deprez's report, we are considering as regards the legal status to ensure the protection of personal data in the context of judicial cooperation in the european union.

- the next item is the report (a5-0082/2002) by mr coelho, on behalf of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs, on the proposal for a council decision establishing a framework programme on the basis of title vi of the treaty on european union - police and judicial cooperation in criminal matters (com (2001) 646 - c5-0694/2001 - 2001/0262 (cns)).
the commission is not prepared to accept this. i would like to concentrate on the argo programme and to begin by thanking oostlander for a good report. the committee considered a number of proposals which further strengthened the common dimension of the programme. it cannot be stressed enough, mr president, how important it is that the eu should have a common approach to asylum issues. it is therefore important that the administrative procedures operate in a uniform manner everywhere. people seeking to enter europe should be treated with respect, humanity and equality. these people cannot be shipped between the different member states as goods, as is unfortunately often the case today. the need for joint programmes and joint funding in the field of asylum has become clear in recent years. as other colleagues have said, training, the exchange of experience and the development of common practices are needed. the candidate countries, which will form the new external borders of the eu, are of course also important in this context. unfortunately, and i think it is worth noting, the eu's refugee and asylum policy has in recent years been characterised by opposite tendencies. member states have often acted short-sightedly and rashly, with disastrous human suffering as a result. sometimes one wonders whether some member states have been competing in the neglect of humanism and the rule of law. the liberal group has tabled an amendment, as oostlander pointed out, which concerns a broader definition of the concept of refugee in the geneva convention. the geneva convention is certainly the cornerstone on which asylum and refugee policy must be based, but there is a need to adapt practices to the changing realities. as robert evans pointed out, the experience of recent years has shown this. refugees must be able to seek protection from the state as well as from harassment in their home country, and therefore a broader definition is needed. we are therefore proposing a broader definition and we hope that the house will also accept our proposal.
the commission is not prepared to accept this. last week i visited bosnia and herzegovina and the republic of serbia. colleagues, it is only ten years since a horrific murder and massacre of innocent people began in the midst of a europe which we all assured would never again see mass graves of desecrated bodies. that's not what happened. mass graves are being found today and remains are being identified. i saw firsthand the remains of 500 people lined up in an industrial complex. mr president, never before has the eu's failure been more evident. when the refugee flows began, the eu acted slowly and was paralysed. there was a lack of a common approach. the marinho report and the commission's proposal are therefore of the utmost importance. i would like to commend the rapporteur for the work he has done and for the sensitivity he has shown. we in the liberal group accept the basic principle of the proposal on how to look at asylum seekers, how to deal with this and also the exceptions that exist. however, we want to make the rules more predictable and more responsive to the wishes of the individual. some of the proposals were also very favourably accepted by the committee. let me mention a few important points. individual asylum requests will be given greater consideration. we're dealing with people! the rules determining the country responsible must be based on legal certainty and predictability. a person other than a relative who is closely related to a minor asylum seeker shall also be able to receive custody if it is in the child's best interests. the country of asylum responsible can thus be determined on the basis of such factors. asylum seekers who have a family member in a particular member state should be able to have their application processed there. complaints about which country is responsible for the application must have a suspensive effect, so that the asylum seeker is not transferred to another member state during the waiting period. the commission has proposed that personal data relating to asylum seekers should be disclosed only to the member states concerned and not to all member states. let me highlight in particular amendment no 12, which mr klamt also supported. we believe that a partner and a cohabitant, regardless of their sex, should be counted as family members in member states which treat unmarried and married couples equally. the commission is not clear on this point and clarification is needed. i hope that the house will be able to support this motion tomorrow. in conclusion, i would like to put a direct question to commissioner vitorino. the proposal you have put forward would, in my view, make impossible the kind of scandals that are taking place at sangatte in the channel tunnel, with the transfer of people and responsibility between different member states. what are you doing to make sure that what happened at sangatte is not happening? what are you doing in liaison with london and paris to make this proposal a reality now?
mr president, i would like to thank you. it's very easy to feel powerless in the face of the asylum situation we have in europe. it is easy to feel powerless in the face of the fact that so many people need this protection, but also in the face of the inability we have in many places to integrate asylum seekers and immigrants into our member states. this is a very important issue and i would therefore like to begin by thanking my colleague mr marinho for all the work he has put into this report. i would also like to thank the commission for its proposal. i would like to stress what mr marinho said about the importance of member states now taking this matter seriously and turning words into action. the proposal on the responsibility for asylum applications is a small but very important step towards creating a common asylum policy in the eu. despite the schengen agreement and the removal of borders in this area, there are major differences between the reception of refugees by member states and the possibilities for asylum seekers to obtain residence permits. solidarity in the reception and processing of asylum applications is therefore of particular importance for asylum seekers themselves. it is therefore gratifying that the eu can now go one step further than the dublin convention has done. the shortened processing times reduce the risk of long waiting times, with all the uncertainties that this entails for asylum seekers. the proposals to strengthen the rights of children in their own family are particularly important. the children who are left alone to flee war and oppression must have all the support we can give them. an important contribution to this is the broader concept of the family, which we now have the power to decide on. finally, i would like to stress the importance of respecting the way people choose their family relationships, so that we also try to equalise different forms of cohabitation and partnerships.
in writing. - (sv) what is it? we, the swedish social democrats, abstained from voting on the amended motion for a resolution. we believe that a common legal basis should be created to combat environmental crime and we welcome the framework decision in this area. however, we have doubts about the need to adopt a directive in this area, as there is no legal basis for member states to be required to criminalise breaches of environmental legislation under the first pillar. at present, the treaty establishing the european community does not contain any provisions giving the community such a competence.
in writing. -sv) we swedish social democrats voted against amendment no 13 and abstained on amendments nos 14, 15 and 24 to the marinho report on the rules concerning the responsibilities of the member states for asylum applications. in amendment no 13 by the fl group, it is proposed that the asylum application be examined in the country in which the applicant has already applied for asylum or the applicant's family members have already been granted residence permits. we are in favour of family reunification and we believe that asylum seekers from the same family should be able to come to the same member state. however, we think it is too much to extend this right, as proposed, we believe, in amendment no 13, to cover also family members who are already in the country for different reasons, as that would include any legitimate reason for staying, e.g. study permits. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. amendments nos 14, 15 and 24 propose, contrary to the proposals of the commission and the committee on civil liberties and human rights, that the procedures for transferring an applicant from one member state to another be suspended if he appeals against a decision on the country which will examine his application. we believe that such a suspension is acceptable only in certain cases. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them.
in writing. - (sv) we abstained on amendment no 16 in order to support amendment no 12 by our own group. we support the idea of considering registered partners as family members, but we believe that this concept is covered by the term 'unmarried partner'. we would also welcome the amendments to the legislation of certain member states to recognise registered partnerships and cohabitation between homosexuals. in the context of the definition of family members, however, it is important to apply the principle of subsidiarity. it is not logical that the european parliament and the council should be able to change the family legislation of the member states by means of a directive dealing with asylum policy. we therefore propose in amendment no 12 that same-sex partners be recognised as family members where the legislation or practice of the host member state provides that unmarried couples, same-sex couples and married couples are to be treated on an equal footing.
in writing. - (sv) we voted in favour of the report on checks at the common borders, but we have some reservations about amendment no 4, which, in certain circumstances, authorizes the police to carry out identity checks and to make provisional arrests of persons on the territory of other member states. we would also like to draw attention to amendment no 1, which gives the police of another member state the right to continue surveillance when the host member state is not able to do so. the monopoly of power of the national police is a fundamental rule of any democratic society. the police of another country can only be authorized to intervene in a member state in very special circumstances, as also specified in the schengen convention. the fact that foreign police officers are granted the right to carry out surveillance or arrests in another member state necessarily means that there is room for improvement in the work. at the same time, it is important to remember that the legislation of the host member state prevails. the rules and traditions of the member states, in particular in the context of identity checks and arrest procedures, are different and should not be harmonised. it is necessary to ensure that the police do not abuse the right to supervise persons suspected of having committed offences outside their own member state, as well as full compliance with national legislation and rules. it is also essential to ensure that all the common requirements of the schengen convention are met before any foreign police intervention on the territory of another member state.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the nightmare in the middle east never ends. as we stand here and discuss, violence and killing are rampant. as you can hear, solana and patten, we all experience tremendous disempowerment, disgust and frustration. the suicide attacks are preventing the israelis from being able to move freely. drinking coffee with a friend or shopping for food is mr president, i would like to thank you. in our group we are very positive about the main features of this proposal. the waste problem is one of the major environmental problems we have to tackle and we like the idea of finding a comprehensive solution whose objective is to reduce the total amount of waste, increase recycling and at the same time reduce the emission of hazardous substances in the waste management. a well-thought-out directive can also act as a catalyst for better product design by the big manufacturers. we reached fairly broad compromises in parliament at first reading, and i think it has been a strength that we have been able to maintain the bulk of these compromises at second reading. this also means that we will be strong in our dealings with the council of ministers when we reach an agreement. i thank the rapporteur for the good cooperation and the open-minded attitude. as regards the directive on dangerous substances, we are particularly pleased with the proposal to ban dangerous substances such as lead and cadmium as early as 2006. nor should this be an obstacle to those countries which want to move faster and already have more progressive legislation in several of these areas. the principle for us in the weee directive is direct manufacturer liability, which should be as clear as possible. on the one hand, it is a matter of principle that manufacturers should pay their own costs, and on the other hand, direct and full producer responsibility means that there is a real drive for product development and for making the various products as easy to recycle as possible. the council's proposal could be improved on a few other points. for example, small producers should also be covered and we want to avoid the problem of 'free riders' where i think amendment no 92 could be a good solution. we also support the demand for higher recycling quotas and the concrete minimum levels of collection per person.
mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. i do not think it is acceptable that most environmental issues are dealt with this late at night. environmental policy and legislation could really use some prime time in this parliament. it is a great responsibility to make decisions on health and the environment that will apply for a long time to come. this directive and the report on brominated flame retardants are good examples of such responsibility. following the first reading in september 2001, the eu risk assessment of octabde and dekabde has been reviewed. this was done in november last year. the european chemicals agency considers that the risk assessment and the situation are now the same for octabde and decabde as they were for pentabde when it led to a total ban. these substances are long-lived and bioaccumulative. the scientific evidence for octs and odes is now so worrying in terms of health and the environment that the agency has recommended that member states make a political decision, i.e. to use the precautionary principle. in the european parliament we must also take up our responsibilities as co-legislators. after the review and the new risk assessment, we must also apply the precautionary principle. we of the green group, the left group and the socialist group therefore table amendment no 710 again, which means that the use of decabde must also be stopped. there's no reason to wait any longer. there are replacement materials. 80% of the use of brominated flame retardants involves the use of decabde. in the light of the new knowledge we have gained, we cannot continue to gamble with the environment and health. we therefore hope that the political groups will vote in favour of the amendments and that commissioner wallström will also confirm that the precautionary principle will be applied.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the rapporteur's proposal and the proposals adopted by the committee are a tougher stance than the council's common position, and i think that is a good thing. however, i believe, as does mr schörling, that we have today the argument to go even further. today we have the argument not only to ban pentacle and octacle but also decapelle. if we wait for the risk assessments, we risk waiting too long. in addition, we already have the arguments and we know that these substances which are dangerous are accumulating in the human body, that they are increasingly present in our environment. we actually have all the arguments we need to make a decision right now. there are also alternatives to these brominated flame retardants, which otherwise risk becoming a major health problem. i agree with mr bowe that we must make an intelligent decision. an intelligent decision in this case means we dare to go ahead and ban what we know is dangerous.
mr president, i would like to thank you. brominated flame retardants pose a serious threat to human health and the environment. after decades of industrial use, they're everywhere, in fish, bird eggs, polar bears, agricultural products, and breast milk. this might seem to be taken from a debate paper written by fundamentalist environmental organizations, but it is not. one of the signatories to that debate article is the director general of the swedish rescue service. the rescue agency is an expert authority in matters such as fire protection, and it is prepared to say that flame retardants have played their part. i think we should listen to this. the heavy argument in the debate against a ban on octabde and decabde is that we cannot expose people to the danger of not using adequate flame retardants in buildings, furniture, etc. at the same time, however, we know that there are alternatives, there is the possibility of using other materials and there is the possibility of producing in a different way than we do today. in some ways, it's almost absurd that we have to use chemicals to combat the chemicals that we use in our buildings today. the reason it burns as well as it does is because we already use chemicals in the products we have. the commission should think hard and still try to make a serious effort to ban octave and decade-long works too. the question is how long we will have to wait after the evaluation before a ban or restriction on the use of octabde and decabde comes. the commission has nevertheless said that it is prepared to act quickly. how quickly is the commission prepared to act?

mr president, i would like to thank you. a year ago i visited an asbestos factory in poland. during the communist period, it was known that one in four employees would sooner or later develop lung cancer or lung cancer. however, the information was classified, and the factory was closed only after the collapse of the communist system. now a gigantic cleanup job remains. in addition, former employees live in fear of future cancer. there are, i think, three beauty spots in this otherwise excellent report. firstly, it is proposed that national registers be established of companies which are suitable for asbestos removal. it is much better to have a certificate of competence for a remediation company, which can be used for contracts. records become bureaucratic and difficult to manage. secondly, it is certainly possible to require reversal of the burden of proof in the case of suspected asbestos-related disease in the case of asbestos and mesothelioma, but it is completely impossible in the case of any possible asbestos-related bronchial or gastric cancer. perhaps many years after exposure to asbestos, it is not possible to evaluate asbestos versus smoking and other factors that may have contributed to the disease. however, the link between asbestos and mesothelioma is very strong. thirdly, as a doctor, i am afraid that the eu will start to establish diagnostic criteria for diseases. we have a worldwide diagnostic system developed by the world health organization, and the evaluation of this system should therefore also be the responsibility of the world health organization. if the eu is to set diagnostic criteria, we are entering the field of health care, and then the eu simply lacks the power to legislate.

in writing. - we abstained in the final vote on the commission proposal on the freezing of the assets of persons suspected of terrorism. we have, however, supported the amendments which help to improve the regulation. the fight against terrorism is essential and receives our full support. the freezing of assets of persons suspected of terrorism or of collaborating with terrorism is an important contribution to this work on a global scale. the procedure which allows the un security council and the un sanctions committee to decide sanctions against individuals without them knowing the reason for them or having the possibility of being subject to legal review is unacceptable. in this way we risk jeopardizing legal certainty. all persons on the un list must be able to see firsthand the evidence and the official charges against them. they must be given the opportunity to have their case heard by a court. clear and precise criteria on how individuals are added and removed from the list should be defined. the eu member states represented on the un security council, namely britain, france and ireland, have a heavy responsibility to enforce legal certainty and to demand respect for human rights. the spanish presidency and the member states must exert all their weight in the un and vis-à-vis the united states to ensure respect for human rights.

in writing. - (sv) freedom and fundamental rights must never be called into question. this is particularly important in the fight against terrorism. the fight against terrorism cannot be effectively fought if democracy and human rights are also restricted. according to the un charter, all member states are obliged to comply with decisions taken by the un security council. these decisions are binding but never absolute. they must leave a certain margin of manoeuvre. the decision of the sanctions committee to take over the us list of suspected terrorists and terrorist organisations is unacceptable. the approval of the list, which has not been made public, by the sanctions committee if no one protests within 48 hours opens the way to unjustified decisions. freedom and fundamental rights are endangered by a non-judicial process when the persons concerned have neither the right to be informed of the charges against them nor the possibility of appealing against the decision. parliament's amendments are essential to guarantee legal certainty for eu citizens. i therefore support the council regulation, but only in its amended form.

the commission is not prepared to accept this. i want to emphasize the child's and children's need for community. it is the family and the relatives who can provide the child with a natural home and a natural upbringing in the most basic way. i would therefore also like to stress the urgency of the adoption of the epp-ed group's amendments in the european parliament. we refer to a resolution of 5 july 2001 which stresses that children are the first victims of the breakdown of family life. we also point to the fine draft final document of unicef, which emphasises that 'the family is the basic unit of society and bears the primary responsibility for the protection, upbringing and development of children'. we also want to stress what we said in the european parliament last year, namely that the world that suits children must be a world that suits the family. the breakdown of the family and family life is one of europe's greatest and most serious challenges and threats. in my home country, sweden, a leader of the third largest party in the country has publicly said to the swedish public: death to the family. the european parliament has a very different view and instead emphasises the family as the basic unit for providing care and love to the growing family. we can, as we did in 2001, highlight these values. it is a message to the world that even so-called modern europe still recognizes the family as the basic unit where love is given to the growing family. we need family and the understanding of its importance more than ever.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen of the council! two weeks ago, a joint resolution by the socialists, liberals, greens and left socialists on the situation in the middle east was voted through here in the european parliament by a narrow margin. we in the christian-democratic group of the epp-ed voted against. the truth is the only way to real peace, reconciliation and coexistence. in that resolution, the state of israel was criticized on 12 occasions, while the palestinians and the palestinian authority were criticized only three times. the resolution also calls for the eu to take action to combat terrorism and to ensure that the eu is able to take action against terrorism. nor was there any mention of the groups designated by the united states as terrorists, namely hezbollah and the al-aqsa brigades. the latter group constitutes the military wing of yasser arafat's fatah movement and is responsible for the suicide bombing in jerusalem on 12 april, which killed six israeli civilians. only three speakers mentioned these terrorist groups by name at all during the two-hour plenary debate in strasbourg on 10 april. yet everyone knows that these men of violence and suicide bombers - these people who want their sons and daughters to be sacrificed to kill other people and who are paid by iraq for this purpose - are not prepared to recognize the right of the state of israel to exist in the area at all. these terrorist groups, mr president, and the spanish presidency, are part of everyday life in the middle east. how do we deal with their existence? and human trafficking are on the increase. in west africa, the cocoa plantations are mostly man-made, with boys often doing the heavy work of harvesting the cocoa beans. the working days of these children are up to 15 hours long. so it is not just child labor, but children are also sold as slaves between the various cocoa producers. the council, the commission and the eu member states must act
