in writing. - (sv) the swedish social democrats abstained in the vote on the oostlander report on drug trafficking. we believe it is important that cooperation between member states in the fight against drug trafficking in europe should be strengthened. we therefore welcome the council's framework decision on the establishment of minimum provisions on the constituent elements of criminal offences and penalties applicable in the field of drug trafficking. unfortunately, the oostlander report contains amendments which limit the scope of the framework decision. we believe that it should cover all forms of drug trafficking, not just the most serious and international trade in narcotics. the fight against drugs and drug addiction must be carried out at all levels: prevention, the fight against crime and rehabilitation. we are concerned about the move towards a more liberal attitude towards drugs which we are currently witnessing in several member states, where so-called soft drugs are being looked at with an increasingly lenient eye. these drugs are the gateway to hard drugs, which lead to addiction and social misery. one of the conditions for effective drug policy is therefore that all types of drugs should be combated.
in writing. - (sv) we abstained in the vote on the report by mrs helle thorning-schmidt on the proposal for a directive on vibration. our position on the european union is positive in principle. as members of the swedish liberal party, we see european integration as a means of finding solutions to crossborder problems, for example, in the environment, trade, crossborder movement, human rights and conflict resolution. in our view, safety at work is one example of an area where the eu should have only limited competence, only in cases where the free movement of persons within the union is involved. the question of vibration at the workplace cannot be considered to be of a directly transnational nature. each member state must therefore bear full responsibility in this respect and have the right to set its own threshold values.
in writing. -{sv) we voted against draft supplementary and amending budget no 2/2002 for the financial year 2002. at the meetings of the committee on budgets on 19 march and 9 april it was decided to pay back to the member states eur 10 billion of the total surplus of eur 14 billion. given that the surplus for the 2002 budget amounts to eur 14 billion, it is not justified to give only eur 5.5 billion to the member states. we consider that this decision is not a logical follow-up to the discussion we had earlier in the committee on budgets. we shall vote against this proposal, since it does not provide for the refund of the sums to which the member states are entitled. we are also aware that the sum not paid today will, in principle, have to be paid at a later date. the commission has already indicated that the amounts to be paid for 2003 are so large that there is reason to fear that they will exceed the budget ceiling. this will involve very significant expenditure for the member states for the financial years 2003 and 2004, when budgetary needs will increase due to enlargement.
in writing. {sv) we swedish moderates in the european parliament voted in favour of report a5-106/2002 on the state of human rights in the world in 2001 and the european union's human rights policy. we do so because we support the great principles of human rights which we believe this resolution protects. we are, however, opposed to the points where it is said that globalisation is a source of economic and social disadvantage. we are also against the equation between the so-called positive rights (social, economic and cultural) and the more important negative rights (political and legal). we take for granted that 'state terrorism' refers to states that fund terrorist organizations. we are against the idea that parliament should have its own human rights ambassador. we would like a clearer general recommendation for the abolition of the death penalty. we dissociate ourselves from the insinuation that the prisoners held at guantanamo were not captured during the war in afghanistan. we voted in favour of amendment no 3 by the eldr group.

in writing. - (sv) parliament adopted today a report on the multiannual guidance programmes for fishing fleets. parliament regretted that several member states had failed to meet their commitments to reduce the size of the fleet. at the same time, it is being proposed, in a completely incoherent way, to maintain aid for fleet modernisation and modernization. sea depletion is one of the most serious and obvious problems facing the ecosystem today. the european fishing fleet, which is estimated to have overcapacity of 40%, naturally contributes to this situation. if we are to succeed in reducing the fishing fleet, we must radically reform our policy in this area. the generous subsidies that currently exist cannot be maintained, even though they have the merit of improving the working conditions and safety of fishermen. the commission has repeatedly stated that fleet modernisation and renewal also lead to an increase in fishing volume. this is in contradiction with the need to 'achieve a sustainable balance between resources and their exploitation', which, as the report very rightly states, is the first objective of these multiannual guidance programmes. we have not been able to support parliament's position on this matter and have therefore voted against the report.
mr president, i would like to thank you. what we are discussing now may seem like a dull, formal list, but it is in fact extremely important and represents a central prerequisite in the constant struggle for greater openness in the european institutions. transparency is maij-weggen's flagship and i want to congratulate her on her work. it is clear that parliament is at the forefront of efforts to make our institutions more open. our documents and our activities are public to the citizens of europe. we are an open institution with open meetings. i believe that we are one of the most transparent parliaments in the world, through a conscious commitment to formal decisions, but also through unconventional methods such as the initiative taken by some liberal members, open up europe, or the decision to publish the financial interests of members online on the principle of name and shame. through such methods, transparency can be achieved. today, the european parliament can be followed by computer from northern finland to the greek islands. but of course, there is much more to do. it is also about ensuring that there are clear rules so that access to documents is not dependent on arbitrary or benevolent interpretation of an application. the register is therefore an important tool for citizens to know what is happening and how to appeal. perhaps the document should clarify the further increase in transparency that this will bring. how should documents from third parties, from the member states or from official correspondence be handled? there is room for interpretation here. i hope that parliament's officials in general will interpret all applications generously and that we will soon have reports on the number of applications rejected. access to documents and broad transparency are prerequisites for confidence in the eu's work. i really hope that the commission and the council will have their records ready by 3 june and that we can then begin the evaluation under article 255 which will be completed in one and a half years.
the main rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. let me begin by apologising to the chairman of the committee on budgets, terence wynn, who has been hampered. he's asked me to speak for him, too. we both want to thank commissioner schreyer and director-general gazon for a very good cooperation. i think we'll benefit greatly from our collaborative climate this year. there are some problems that need to be solved. this is only a first discussion of the preliminary draft budget. we will return to this subject on several occasions, including at the july partsession and on a number of occasions in the autumn. it is nevertheless important to make a few preliminary remarks. firstly, there are a number of positive aspects which i think it is worth mentioning. the first, as regards this year's draft budget, is the improvement of the process itself. we are now finally moving towards a system of activity-based budgeting and thus also towards a more manageable, transparent system which makes it easier to present the union's budget to the citizens of europe, which i believe is extremely important for confidence in the european union. we are now taking the first step by using the old technology and the new adb technology in parallel, which is a great step forward. the second positive element is that we have succeeded in achieving a better political dialogue. the commission's annual policy strategy and the council and parliament's decision at about the same time on the political guidelines for the next year's budget have brought about an earlier stage of political exchange, political dialogue. this is actually progress. although there was a great deal of criticism in parliament about the lack of substance in the commission's annual policy strategy, i still believe that the method as such is a clear step forward. i also hope that we will be able to renew parliament's own budgetary debate by holding a larger debate in july where we will not only focus on next year's budget but also on the implementation of this year's budget and link the processes together, which i believe is very important for the future. i also hope that we can have a proper and exciting budgetary debate in the autumn, hopefully at the september partsession, in which we can also link the budget better to the political legislative initiatives. this is the procedure itself and it represents a positive development. another positive aspect is the broad consensus on the basic political priorities. if we compare the commission's basic priorities with the guidelines adopted by parliament, we see that we agree on a number of points. we agree that we must now put enlargement first. it will most likely be in force in 10 countries by the beginning of 2004 and is the largest enlargement we have ever undertaken. this is our most important task and we must start preparing it in the 2003 budget. we also agree that we must deal with security issues in a broad sense, firstly the whole discussion on how to deal with european security after the terrorist attacks of last september, and secondly a broader perspective on security issues - food security, transport security, etc. - which require a range of measures already in next year's budget. we also agree on a third area, namely the need to focus more on sustainable development in economic, social and environmental terms, which will require a range of action by the union. here we have been relatively weak so far, not least in terms of getting parliament's initiatives on small businesses, e-learning, and a number of other areas where i think we need to increase the pace considerably. in view of all those who believe that the eu budget is an ever-growing colossus, it is also worth noting, not least for educational purposes, that we now have a budget whose share of the member states' economies is decreasing. so while there are a number of basic positive signs, there are some points that we feel a certain concern about. the first is the overriding problem of budget implementation. we have arrears and other arrears, the so-called reste à liquides or accumulated outstanding commitments, which are only increasing. i would like to remind you of the debate on supplementary budget no 2 for 2002 and the fact that the member states are reclaiming eur 10 billion because the union has not been able to fulfil its tasks. in this respect, the commission proposes a greater increase in payments than in new commitments, which is of course a step in the right direction. we are concerned, however, that this measure is wholly inadequate to make up for the existing backlog. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the problem is of crucial importance and must be discussed. the second problem, which is also of concern and must be discussed, is the management of enlargement, and the most urgent are the efforts that need to be made within the european institutions to prepare for enlargement itself. the costs of enlargement as such are not charged to the 2003 budget, but we must prepare now, especially if 10 new countries will be in place by the beginning of 2004. the commission proposes in this context that we use the flexibility instrument to cover the increased administrative expenditure. we may have to do this, but i think we have to increase the pressure on the secretaries-general to come up with new, radical proposals, for example on increased interinstitutional cooperation, to bring down costs and thus avoid using the flexibility instrument. i believe that there are many new avenues, for example, in the area of language rules and the recruitment of new staff to the union. we should seriously increase the pressure before we decide to use the flexibility instrument. as regards safety - i mentioned food safety and transport safety earlier - we are also concerned about the enormous number of new authorities, so-called bodies. we have eurojust, europol, food safety, railway safety, air safety and so on. the institutions themselves are not a problem, but they cause problems in terms of budgeting and the ability to establish an overview of the union's expenditure. they risk doubling our costs by working in both the new authorities and the commission. they raise problems of accountability and transparency. we must review these aspects in depth before we continue to build up the fleet of new administrations, even though the tasks as such are certainly extremely important. another point which we must discuss, which the commissioner also mentioned, is of course foreign policy. the commission proposes a budgetary margin of eur 60 million. that may sound like a lot, but it's really not enough. we are faced with great uncertainties, including the continuing developments in the middle east. we do not know what demands will be made of the union in terms of action in palestine. we do not know with any certainty the detailed demands that will be made on afghanistan or the global effort to deal with poverty-related diseases, etc. we also do not know whether it will be acceptable to reduce the cards programme in the balkans at the rapid pace that the commission is now proposing. in this area too, i believe we need a more comprehensive discussion and evaluation of the union's actions before we take any decisions. to all these uncertainties we can add the fisheries agreements in category 4 which for another year are an issue we need to discuss. finally, i would like to say something about the longer term. two of the most important budgetary problems that we have to discuss, although they do not concern the 2003 budget, must nevertheless be addressed. the reform of the agricultural and fisheries policies on the one hand and the real costs of enlargement on the other. we shall have a special report from the committee on budgets on this subject later. let us begin this discussion in time. let us also try to preserve the extremely interesting cooperation and common approach we have in the exciting development towards enlargement. i think there is a good chance that we can find common solutions.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. when we laid down the guidelines for the 2003 budget a few months ago, there was a broad consensus in parliament on three priorities for category 5. the first was preparations for the forthcoming enlargement, the second was the need for institutional reforms and the third was that everything should be managed within the budgetary framework. these are priorities which are now to be translated into figures and calculations in euros and cents for parliament. if the european council, at its copenhagen summit in december, decides to make a big bang, that is to say, to bring ten new countries into the union in time for the next elections to the european parliament in 2004, this will be extremely demanding. no challenge is greater for the european parliament than this one. to allow for such an expansion, up to 147 observers must be able to be in place from the beginning of 2003. to this end, it is now proposed to place eur 6 million in a reserve. in total, enlargement-related costs for 2003 are estimated at just over eur 32 million. parliament has, through a forward-looking policy, worked long and diligently to make possible the enlargement to come. it is a work which is now continuing into the 2003 budget. this budget also contains a provision for the early payment of certain expenditure for the 2004 to 2003 budget. this amount is eur 51 million. in other words, this represents a total economic investment of eur 89 million in one financial year alone. but if all this is to be possible, enlargement must be the european parliament's top priority and this must also have a visible impact on budgetary work. the parliament's own reform process is of the utmost importance, which is priority number two. i think that many members, like myself, have over the years heard comments such as this is not a real parliament. in many ways this may have been a correct observation in the past, in the sense that the european parliament did not have much real legislative power. today, parliament is involved in more and more decisions. it is not difficult to predict that participation will increase further. if this is to be possible, parliament must be strengthened in its key functions. it is a question of the ability of members to exercise the powers which belong to every parliament in every democracy: budget, legislation, control. without a continued serious commitment to these functions, there will continue to be many people who claim that we are not a real parliament. that is why we must strengthen these elements, create better budgetary follow-up and increase support for members in the legislative process. if all the costs of enlargement and the necessary reforms of parliament are to be contained within the third priority, i.e. managing ever-increasing expenditure within the established budgetary limits, a strict budgetary process is needed. despite all the commitments of the biggest enlargement in the history of the eu, the 2003 budget is still managed within the 20% ceiling which has long been parliament's share of the common budget. for next year we see an increase of 1.6%, compared with an expected increase of an average of 3.5% for all the eu institutions. when we started work on the 2003 budget a few months ago, there was a large estimated deficit in category 5. a few months later, most institutions have responded positively to the calls from the parliament and the council to focus on the key activities and thus reduce their estimates. in fact, when we look at the budget figures from the other institutions, it seems quite possible that we will be able to manage within the current framework without using any flexibility instrument. let me make a brief technical correction. paragraph 22 of the resolution sets the amount of eur 15,5 million for the building reserve. in order to reflect the decisions taken today, the amount should be adjusted slightly, so that parliament remains under the ceiling of eur 1 051 million.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat unemployment. mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank trentin for a very good job under extremely difficult circumstances. the final version of the report arrived this morning and it has not been easy for any of us to work together between committees in these conditions. we do, however, agree on many points and trentin has very well taken up some of the points of view that have come from our committee. i agree with him that we must assume that the european economies are currently stagnating and need to be strengthened, and that we must invest in what is necessary and on which there is a great deal of political agreement. both trentin and marinos have spoken about education. i would like to stress in particular basic education and basic vocational training. it is also important to invest in improving the ecological infrastructure, which is good for the future, and in training which has a high employment impact. it is important to do not only the things that are needed immediately, but also those that are needed in the long term and have beneficial social consequences. it is therefore important to pay particular attention, more than in the past, to the importance of strengthening local labour markets that are less sensitive to cyclical developments. we shall have occasion to return to this in parliament a little later when we are dealing with a report from the commission on the development of local employment strategies. finally, i would like to say, and i regret it, that we have not been able to take sufficient account of the eastern european countries and their economies, as would be desirable in this process. admittedly, there are other measures which should be given priority in the short term, but as soon as we have a slightly longer perspective, it is necessary to discuss the economy from a pan-european perspective.
in the case of the tourism sector, the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of tourism. of course, we are in favour of promoting cooperation in the tourism sector, so that it can lead to economic and social development throughout europe. however, we support the principle of subsidiarity. we shall therefore abstain from voting in favour of the report, because the establishment of a common legal framework for tourism would be too much in breach of this principle.
in writing. - (sv) tourism is already an important sector in europe and its development potential for the coming years is significant. the commission's communication contains a number of useful proposals to promote employment and dynamism in this sector even more. the open method of harmonisation and the strengthening of the role of the advisory committee on tourism are important elements of the report. we also welcome the intention to make tourism a sustainable long-term activity through a tourism agenda 21. we do not think, however, that the report adequately respects the principle of subsidiarity. the introduction of a framework programme for tourism would impose additional burdens on the european budget, which is already tight. nor is it necessary to give a legal basis to tourism policy in the treaty. even if harmonisation is necessary, tourism is an issue that can be addressed at national and regional level, for example in terms of marketing. the report also shows a certain lack of confidence in the development capacity of the sector itself.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i bow to the rapporteurs who have done a very good job: qualified, knowledgeable and well thought out. i am very pleased that primary production has been included and that we are now finally covering the whole food chain. i am also very pleased that the individual responsibility of the producer is again specified in the food law. we will get nowhere with 'police' power alone unless people themselves take responsibility for their businesses, work and actions. i am personally very pleased with mr schnellhardt's willingness to compromise so that we now have article 4 (4) (amendment no 31) which allows europe's infinitely rich diversity of traditional food to remain on the market without giving individual member states the opportunity to bypass the rules and label all their products as traditional. we have also taken care in this way that it is not just small-scale production; some of europe's main food exports are in fact traditional cheeses and hams, etc., which require special production methods without being dangerous. i am very glad that we have got through this section, because it would be terrible if we had a food law in europe that would only suit nestlé and mcdonald's. finally, i would like to say that i am also very pleased that we have reached sensible and sustainable compromises on hunting. i live in the middle of the woods in sweden and would hardly dare to come home at all if any moose owner had got the idea that i had banned moose hunting. this is a highly valuable issue and i think we have reached a good compromise that both pheasant hunters and moose hunters can live with.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i'm a deer owner in lapland in northern sweden. if we don't hunt elk every year, the elk population will increase rapidly, the number of people killed by traffic will increase, and the damage to the forest will increase dramatically. when we go hunting in the morning in september, it is often freezing, and the climate at this time of year is equivalent to the temperature of a refrigerator. sometimes it's even freezer temperature. in my hometown, from an environmental point of view, there is no better refrigerator for the hunter than nature's own cold room. the larger the eu, the more difficult it is to regulate in detail through eu rules. eu directives must be good in all parts of the union. they must allow for the adaptation of the provisions to different circumstances. member states with functioning inspection procedures before wild game meat is placed on the market must be able to maintain their rules. in my country, politicians who want to create an opinion against european cooperation have used this proposal for a directive to cast suspicion on the eu as a threat to traditional hunting. it's important to point out that they're wrong. the union does not have legal powers to regulate the hunting of game for domestic consumption and the handling of game meat for private consumption. this further underlines the need for hunting regulations in general to be decided on the basis of the principle of subsidiarity. the schnellhardt report has made the section on game meat in this directive really good.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. i would first of all like to thank the shadow rapporteurs and others in the informal food safety group of the committee on the environment for the good cooperation. of course, the commission's initiative was also included in both reports. i shall first refer to the surveillance directive. it is necessary to have a clear and precise picture of the zoonotic situation in europe. we are talking about the food scandals we are facing, most recently an acrylamide scandal in sweden. we must remember, however, that while all these alarm reports are serious in themselves, they are actually more serious in psychological terms than in physical public health. the big threat ahead is the old normal, the one that people have lived with for millions of years and that the third world still lives with. in fact, the most common cause of infant mortality even today is infectious disease and diarrhea. for us in the rich world, antibiotic resistance is going to be the big problem. more and more of our simple infections tend to become serious again. there's a risk that if we don't -- and we should have slowed down 20 years ago, which is already too late, maybe even too late -- can radically and rapidly reduce the number of infections and antibiotic treatments, we'll soon be back to the days of my grandmother's mother, when almost every woman had to have four children in order for two to grow to adulthood. the problem is greatest among children and adolescents. i am therefore very grateful that we are now going to be mapping the antibiotic resistance of the zoonoses we are detecting in europe. today, statistics show that salmonella and other foodborne infections are slowly declining, while those transmitted by vegetables are actually increasing. that is why i am very pleased that in studies to trace an outbreak, for example, vegetable foods are included. the most important thing about the monitoring directive is that we have common standards so that the data collected is comparable between the different countries. we need to have standardization of sampling, reporting and handling. i would also like to remind you that new technology should actually make good reporting easier and faster so that we do not have to wait two and a half years to get the situation straight. more rapid information is often required to enable authorities, producers and citizens to be informed at a relatively early stage. this is perhaps the most important element of the surveillance directive. the control regulation is perhaps more controversial. the commission proposal was very soft and cautious and seemed to take account of the fact that some member states think that salmonella is about the same as a mild cold. there's an attitude. there are also member states suffering from salmonella panics of the kind that exist in my own country. that panic is not strange. about 50 years ago, we had the world's largest, or at least the largest, statistically, salmonella catastrophe ever. after that, sweden started to try to fix the problem, which is a natural consequence of disasters. it took our country 25 to 30 years to have a functioning system in place. norway and finland have been able to copy the swedish model. the swedish model gives us experience, but it is probably not enough and not applicable everywhere. sweden is a small, sparsely populated and open country, which perhaps made it easier for us to address the problems. i think the proposal is very cautious, it feels like you are "tossing" around the problem. i would have expected the commission's directorate-general for health and consumer protection to be tougher on this case. first, not all salmonella of public health concern are covered. it is strange that in some cases only two strains are included, even though these account for 80 percent of human disease cases today. or rather, they did it yesterday. what about the other 20 percent? why not include all those who affect or are dangerous to public health when you are still going to control salmonella? how can the commission know what type of salmonella will 'explode' tomorrow? is it going to be st. paul, hadar or someone else? we have a myriad of invasive salmonella that affect public health. i therefore want the wording 'important for public health' to be included, that all varieties be included. the commission proposal also lacks animal species. i am aware that i have brought all kinds of animals for safety reasons, but i will continue to fight for pigs and calves in intensive farming, because we all know that the amount of antibiotics used is very dangerous. we also know that in this culture there are both zoonotic and other bacteria that are totally resistant to antibiotics. there are also such simple and practical circumstances that even if we can keep the breeding stock and the stalls clean, it does not help a bit unless the fodder is clean. for example, we know that a very large source of salmonella aruba is brazilian soy. we know the story that salmonella normally dies at 67 degrees. aruba, on the other hand, has managed to survive at 68 degrees for ten years. we should not think that we will one day be completely risk-free, or that this battle will be won once and for all. it's actually about life. life that bites is as intelligent as we are, although the intelligence may not sit in the same place and express itself in the same way. i therefore appeal to the house to support my two reports as adopted by a large majority in the committee on the environment.
in writing. - i voted against the report. four of our member states have in their constitutions the principle of protection of the inalienability of life, a principle which means that research on stem cells derived from embryos, in other words, from unborn children, is prohibited. the european parliament, the commission and the council are not prepared to take this constitutional protection into account in the european joint research programme. i deeply regret that. i was one of the signatories of amendment no 92, all four parts of which were rejected (by 193 votes to 284, with 22 abstentions; 183 votes to 303, with 21 abstentions and 184 votes to 312, with 18 abstentions). it is therefore clear that taxpayers' money can now be used for activities which are illegal and contrary to the constitution of four member states, including germany. the ethical standards applicable to the sixth framework programme are therefore seriously lacking in research involving human embryos. the position of the member states on this type of research differs greatly and must be respected. it should therefore be understood that the union does not fund research on unused embryos obtained for in vitro fertilisation purposes, nor does it fund research on embryonic stem cells. the use of community funds must benefit all member states. member states should not be forced, as in this case, to fund a form of research which is illegal and contrary to the constitution of their own country.

mr president, i would like to thank you. clarity, clarity, comprehensibility - these keywords are self-evident in all regulations. they apply in board games, sports, constitutions and in society in general. strangely enough, these words have not really been a feature of the work on the european treaties and legal texts that have been added over the years. the eu's regulatory framework is not really a game of yachts, but it affects 370 million people. the result of difficult negotiations over the smallest comma is that anyone who wants to understand how the eu works will hardly choose the treaty of amsterdam, let alone the treaty of nice, as a bedtime story if he wants to be wiser. it is no wonder that people do not trust the eu, when it is so difficult to understand the decisions and mr president, i would like to thank you. it's late on earth. despite the rows of meetings and conferences, it can be argued that today we are far from the goal of sustainable development. we are actually now even further from the goal than when the brundtland report was presented in 1987 and when the rio conference was held. of course, it should be the other way around. the other day the oecd, which is not an environmental organization, said the following: all major global ecological indicators are negative. the situation is equally bad in terms of poverty and social development. the number of poor people is increasing, not the other way around. against this background, the johannesburg meeting is very important, but, as has been suggested here, the hopes are rather low. the negotiating machinery is sluggish and there is a complete lack of interest from the us administration. as mr hulthén said, the spanish presidency is unfortunately not represented here today, which does not indicate that there is much interest in this matter. what are the reasons why there is still a long way to go to achieve a sensible balance between economic growth, social development and environmental protection? there are two main reasons. firstly, there is strong opposition to implementing all the measures that the government has said it would take at a series of conferences, such as eliminating environmentally harmful subsidies in the economy, internalizing environmental costs in prices, establishing clear environmental criteria for our export credit boards so that we do not export unsustainable systems to developing countries, and encouraging activities that are environmentally friendly rather than the other way around. the economic regulatory framework is not going in the right direction today, but we are doing little about it. the lannoye report, which i welcome, contains a number of proposals on how this can be done. the eu must press on in this respect as well as in a number of issues relating to the fight against poverty, otherwise the outcome from johannesburg will be very meagre. secondly, progress is slow because, in my view, we have a model both for education and for the economy that is too vertical and reductionist. we have chosen to organize our society by focusing on specialization. there are more than 20,000 different disciplines in our universities, and specialization is, of course, good for solving different individual problems. however, there are very few experts in crossborder problem solving. the johannesburg meeting will now attempt to solve all these problems. so, alongside short-term measures, we must also look at the big picture and ensure that we develop a better systems thinking in our societies, and not least help the poor countries, the developing countries, to build up research skills so that they can develop on their own strategies which are both more profitable economically and more environmentally sustainable.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i have mixed feelings about this debate and the johannesburg summit. it is positive that the environmental summit is actually taking place and that the two rapporteurs have done a very good job in expressing the concerns, hopes, proposals and demands of the european parliament in the run-up to the summit. i would like to thank both rapporteurs for their very solid work. on the other hand, i feel disappointment and frustration that world leaders do not seem to take the state of the world seriously. with only a few months to go before the summit, the world's leading politicians have not even decided whether they will attend the johannesburg summit. the eu's sustainable development strategy and the world summit communiqué are also very late, given that the subject is about our future and concerns us all. however, respect for the democratic process and the possibility for civil society to participate in the debate should have been greater. i would also like to point out, as other speakers have already done, that the spanish presidency is not represented here. it also shows a lack of respect for this important issue of sustainable development. i also do not think that the eu has given the johannesburg world summit the weight it deserves. the barcelona meeting, which was to decide the eu's position, was also a disappointment. the eu must make clear in johannesburg how it intends to reform its agricultural and fisheries policies, which have major global implications, not least for the poor countries. the eu must also demonstrate that it has a credible strategy for sustainable development so that the world summit can produce the results needed to reverse the present growing environmental crisis. the eu must be a driving force in this respect. i believe that the eu countries have a particular moral and financial responsibility to show a sustainable path. many seem to rely on voluntary commitments from industry and big business, but this alone will not lead to the promises being fulfilled as they were ten years ago. voluntary commitments can help, but a world environmental organization within the un is needed to ensure that human rights and environmental rules are followed. international conventions on the environment and human rights must also take precedence over the rules of the wto, and not, as is the case today, the reverse. this is the line that we in the eu and all of us who will be attending the meeting in johannesburg must pursue.
commission. – i would like to thank mr papayannakis and mr lannoye for their comprehensive and very inspiring reports. it is a sad fact that progress towards the goals established at rio has been slow and in some respects conditions are worse today than they were ten years ago. against this background it is meaningful and hopefully also useful that johannesburg actually takes place. it is a way of attracting the world's attention back to these issues. we certainly want to make the best use of this opportunity. i agree with parliament that the preparatory process for the world summit is not going as well as we would have liked but the increase in development spending announced in monterrey should facilitate progress in johannesburg. at least having the debate on the financing of development behind us with some real result makes it more probable that johannesburg will be able to focus on sustainable development. however, we also have to be more creative in finding innovative means of implementation and financing. we still have a lot of work to do to ensure that the last preparatory meeting in bali at the end of may produces results. this is not certain. the debate is not concerned with objectives, as there seems to be a consensus that poverty eradication and sustainable consumption and production are the overriding goals of the summit, which – as a general feature – is further guided by the commitment to achieve the millennium development objectives. as always, the question is how to achieve these goals. the union is in the process of identifying a series of key priorities for common action derived from our sustainable development strategy. the commission will promote specific initiatives for partnerships in the following areas. let me note that it does not make much difference whether we talk about partnerships or deals. these partnerships we are promoting together add up to something that can meaningfully be called a "global deal". concerning water – with a view to better resource management and improving access to safe drinking water and to adequate sanitation, we will launch an initiative, based on integrated river basin management. concerning energy – the commission will propose an initiative to improve the provision of adequate, affordable and sustainable energy services. the core of the initiative will be the provision of technical assistance to developing countries to put in place appropriate energy policies through the development of partnerships. concerning health – the union will increase, over the next five years, the volume of development assistance targeting improved health outcomes and will invite recipient countries and the international community to join in such efforts. particular attention will be paid to maternal health. wto members should resolve differences on compulsory licences and work to ensure that pharmaceutical products can be made available to the developing world at the lowest possible prices. i remind parliament that a year ago we untied the procurement of essential medicines for aids, malaria and tb globally as far as the commission is concerned. concerning education – the union will also increase, over the next five years, the volume of development assistance in education and invite recipient countries to develop supportive policy frameworks and appropriate national budgets. we will support the world bank “education for all” fast tracking initiative, and strengthen partnerships especially with unesco to build capacity and monitor progress; we will also give priority to primary education and the closing of the gender gap. concerning trade – making globalisation work for sustainable development requires timely progress on the work launched in the doha development agenda, and concrete trade-related initiatives going beyond it. the union will argue for levels of access comparable to everything but arms to be decided by all oecd countries in favour of the least developed countries. concerning forests – the union will effectively contribute to the strengthening of international cooperation to fight illegal logging and trade in timber. here we need more than monitoring. we need policing. concerning fisheries – the union intends to contribute effectively to maintaining biodiversity, while securing the long-term viability of the fishing industry. we will fight illicit, unreported and unregulated fishing, and support sustainable fishery policies in developing countries in the context of poverty reduction strategies. at present we are in the process of renegotiating the traditional fisheries agreements with a large number of countries in africa and elsewhere. this renewal process is essential if we are to move in this direction. concerning governance – the union will contribute to the strengthening of global governance structures and institutions in the economic, social and environmental fields as well as their respective linkages, so as to ensure policy coherence, integration and increased effectiveness. in this context we will also actively engage in the emerging debate on global public goods and will explore innovative mechanisms for international financial solidarity and for incentives for reducing externalities. we will propose setting up a task force open to all actors to identify and discuss the appropriate supply of relevant global public goods. this was one of the elements that we missed in the monterrey consensus but which the commission and some of the member states took up during the week of discussions in monterrey, making sure that what was not included in the monterrey consensus was in fact re-injected in the international debate during the conference. we want to use johannesburg as the platform for moving these items up on the international agenda. all these initiatives will be implemented in the framework of the national and regional poverty reduction strategies of our partner countries which form the basis for sustainable development. in the implementation of these initiatives, the union will pay special attention to africa and african countries’ own efforts to achieve sustainable development. regarding the contributions from mr howitt and mr deva, together they make up a balanced presentation of the challenge. i will continue to listen carefully to both sides in this house on this issue. i agree with both of them so, in principle, this should not be impossible. the big issue is moving forward to create real international governance. this is also the main sticking point between europe and the united states. we find it natural and necessary to move forward in creating global governance on a large number of issues, where the us government, which is less multilateralist, favours a different course of action. this has to be discussed. we see these international conferences as useful arenas for pushing these views forward. several speakers, from all political groups in this house, have mentioned that we need to make progress in reforming the agricultural policy and the fisheries policy in europe in order to be able to present ourselves in a credible manner internationally in these areas. i could not agree more. these are two elements which will take up a lot of attention. the reform of these two areas is as important as any other contribution by europe to global development, as far as international consequences are concerned. preparations for johannesburg will be one of the main items for discussion at the seville european council in june. let us ensure that the european union plays a leading role in achieving an ambitious and action-oriented outcome at johannesburg. some speakers have mentioned the lack of clear decisions concerning participation from the highest levels of government. let me use this occasion to inform parliament that it is clearly the intention of the president of the european commission to take part in the johannesburg summit. a number of other commissioners will be there, making sure that our different areas of responsibility are given the maximum attention during the negotiations. the european parliament has always been an advocate of sustainable development and its integration in all union policies. your efforts help bring a sense of urgency to the preparations for johannesburg. this is needed. i thank parliament for the contributions today.
- thank you, chief nielson. the joint discussion is closed. the vote will take place at 12 noon today.
- the agenda calls for a joint discussion of a commission communication on the world food summit (fao).
commission. – let me start by saying that what mr bowis said is something i can agree with from one end to the other. real starvation, in most cases, relates to conflict situations. these are the low points. but it should also be possible to reach the goal of halving the food problems because those conflicts are man-made. we are now studying the situation very carefully in zimbabwe. our analysis is that even if it is true that there is a drought problem in the region, by far most of the problem of access to food in zimbabwe is man-made. it is a politically created, negative situation. that will not keep us from carrying out our humanitarian mission. we have to be clear what we are facing. strangely enough there is some optimism to be found in the notion that conflicts are one of the main causes of starvation. mr mulder mentioned the need for a correct pricing policy in the developing countries and the need to concentrate on africa. i agree. one of the reasons we want to move more towards giving food aid in the form of cash is to stimulate the development of adequate pricing structures and market situations in these countries. mr mulder also mentioned that giving food aid, as such, can be problematic. he mentioned both the european union and the united states and the agricultural policies here as part of the global problem. once again, i could not agree more. let me quote part of the editorial in the herald tribune yesterday with the headline "an awful farm bill": "george w. bush signed a farm bill on monday that represents a low point in his presidency – a wasteful corporate welfare measure that penalises taxpayers and the world's poorest people in order to bribe a few voters. bush said he was pleased to do this and delivered a brief statement suggesting that he is either dishonest or utterly ignorant of what he has just done." these are strong words. for europe complacency is also a real risk. we have some soul-searching and some reforming to do ourselves. in monterrey, the world bank, imf and wto top management were in fact the ones pushing and really arguing that the global distortion of agricultural policies in the rich world is a core problem. the figures are the following: we give about usd 50 billion a year for development assistance globally, whereas we are spending more than usd 300 billion on subsidising agriculture in the us, japan and europe. (applause) these are figures that are used deliberately by the management of those institutions to inject some realism, seriousness and proportion into the global discussion of north/south relations. this will also be an important theme at the world food summit in rome. clearly, we in europe need to draw the right conclusions. finally, on the need to move towards food security and long-term development instead of continuing old fashioned food aid, again i could not agree more. ethiopia, a few years ago, was, hopefully, the last case where we were more or less hijacked into pouring in wheat instead of attacking the roots of the problem. for this reason also i hope that the meeting in rome will produce real results. (applause)
- thank you, chief nielson. i have received six motions for resolutions tabled pursuant to rule 37 (2) of the rules of procedure. the joint discussion is closed. the vote will take place at 12 noon today.
in writing. -sv) the lamassoure report (a5-0133/2002) concerns the division of powers between the eu and the member states, one of the main issues dealt with by the assembly. the report largely confirms and clarifies the current situation, but adds other important aspects, such as the request that the open method of coordination be accompanied by parliamentary controls. we support the report in all its main points and its ambition to produce a constitutional text which clearly states in which areas the eu has exclusive competences and which it shares with the member states, without thereby prejudging the future development of european integration. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. we do not believe that the pillar structure should simply be abolished, as the report suggests, from what we understand. we do not believe that the financing of the eu's budgetary, foreign and defence policy and the entire third pillar should be the exclusive competence of the eu. we also oppose the proposal that the european parliament should differentiate between different regions by allowing regional parliaments, which have legislative powers, to participate in the activities of parliament's committees. we do not agree with the request in the report to ensure the right of the eu institutions and the member states to request that the ecj examine legislation concerning the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
in writing. -sv) we want to stress that the inclusion of eu budget financing in the areas of the eu's own competence should not, even in the long term, have the consequence that the eu has the right to impose its own taxes.
in writing. - (sv) i cannot vote for the lamassoure report, in particular for the following reasons: this report is clearly supra-national in nature. i am in favour of cooperation between states, but not of the supranationality advocated by the rapporteur. federalism also lacks popular support, as the recent european elections have clearly shown. i am also opposed to the idea of making the common foreign and security policy a community matter, of creating a special chamber within the court for questions relating to the european constitution, and of abolishing the principle of unanimity in the council. i also do not accept that regional parliaments with legislative powers should be given the opportunity to participate in the work of committees in the european parliament. finally, i am opposed to the compulsory harmonization of budgetary and financial policy. in its views on the convention, parliament should instead focus on the changes needed to enable decisions to be taken in the enlarged union.
mr president, i would like to thank you. in the shadow of the conflict in the middle east, horrific acts are taking place in indonesia, in the maluku islands. the terrorist group laskar jihad it is nothing less than a terrorist group is a grave-digger of all that is religious life. the group is a grave-digger for islam and has shown that it stands for nothing but hatred and violence and the wanton killing of the christian population of the maluku islands. i would appeal to the commission, here in the presence of mr nielson, and to the council, not to let the conflict in the middle east make the ethnic and religious cleansing of christians in the maluku islands that is now taking place go unnoticed and instead be highlighted. all efforts should be made to ensure that the indonesian government gives the christian population all the protection it deserves. i also want to emphasize that many muslims in the maluku islands have been affected by this invasion by the terrorist group laskar jihad. christians and muslims have lived together in peace before, and then there are warriors who in the name of allah and god spread hatred, violence and death. 5,000 to 10,000 people have been killed, and 25,000 are seriously injured. of the 10 million inhabitants of the moluccas, 350,000 are refugees. 400 churches and 80 mosques have been burned down. i have personally met catholic bishop mandagi and the head of the protestant churches, hendrix. i've seen their suffering right in their eyes. nielson, do everything the eu can to make indonesia a region of peace! find out who's funding these warlords of terrorism.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is important that not only spaniards participate in this debate. the liberal group welcomes the effort to improve relations between the eu and latin america. it is extremely important to develop further cooperation. we have much in common, not only in terms of history but also in terms of the future. as president fox said here in the house not so long ago, many latin american countries look to the eu as a model in human rights. the attitude towards international conventions, such as the kyoto protocol, and the international criminal court is much more constructive in latin america than in north america. this is an important alliance to build on. the fight against organised crime and drugs also requires deeper cooperation. in the field of trade, much remains to be done. the eu must open up its markets to latin america, particularly in the agricultural sector. we are always quick to criticize others for protectionism. often this is true, but what is our food market if not protectionism? the liberal group would also like to highlight the cuban issue and deplore the fact that it has not been included in parliament's joint resolution. we are glad that a resolution on cuba was adopted in geneva. this requires a much stronger commitment than today. we agree that the american embargo must be lifted. but we need to do more to strengthen the democratic forces in cuba. right now, the opposition is more united and more willing to discuss than ever. the referendum project, the varela project, is worth all the support. free libraries play an important role in providing cubans with literature other than the collected speeches of fidel castro. cuba is still a dictatorship, but there are democratic forces there that are strong, organized and in desperate need of support from the eu and from latin america. let us not fail them!
the commission is not prepared to accept this. first of all, i would like to thank mr rodríguez ramos and mr fiori for the enormous work they have put into these two reports. i have little to add to the rodríguez ramos report. but i have a few comments on fiori's report. i think it is important that we consider whether we should maintain or develop the agricultural policy. we must recognize that the improvements in both physical and electronic communications make us increasingly dependent on the world around us. this makes it impossible for us in the eu to isolate ourselves and to pursue our own agricultural policy. we have to take into account what is happening in our world. the wto negotiations are becoming increasingly important and are becoming the norm for what we do. this is underlined by the fact that the countries of central and eastern europe will soon be part of the eu. indeed, an enlarged eu will be the largest player on the world food market. we must therefore also assume the role of the main actor in driving development forward. after the united states has increased its agricultural subsidies, the eu must now try to push the issue of opening up the world market, bringing the developing countries into the world market and thus making it work better. this would also create an opportunity for developing countries to show themselves as a purchasing power on the world market through growth, because of their enormous food needs. mr president, i think it is important that we reflect on this both in the light of the interim report and in the light of a new agricultural policy after 2006. the fact that we are aware that a functioning world market is much more important to europe's farmers can be a much greater guarantee of their being able to get a decent income than an increasingly fragile political support system which we are no longer very much in agreement on wanting to build.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it's just sad. the skilful and kind cappato has almost become synonymous with a derogatory term among members and officials who have worked hard and long on this case. despite a compromise, the european parliament and the council are still far apart. unfortunately, it is difficult to be satisfied with the result, but national regulations would create even greater disparities and even more problems. there is a great danger and inconsistency in the council's position to bring the issue of the data act into the directive. third pillar issues simply do not work in a first pillar directive. the pillar system simply does not work, and the commission knows this, having just announced it. now it is said that a framework decision on general surveillance is planned. the citizens have reason to be concerned. the union must not rush into these important issues which, under the guise of data protection, risk undermining the protection of personal privacy. there is a need for a proper discussion between industry, politicians and interest groups before we move on to further new legislation. perhaps we just need to look a little closer at how it's done in other countries, like the united states. the questions must be subject to democratic and judicial scrutiny. i put my hopes in the european convention in this respect. i was already fighting for the cost during the last term. i returned to it in my report on the strategy for a safer information society, increased security of information infrastructures and the fight against computer-related crime (a5-0284/01). it's been a while. the costs of compulsory data storage and data retrieval must be highlighted! mr president, i would like to thank you. i am very pleased and satisfied that we now have a directive on public access to environmental information which is fully in line with the aarhus convention. i also think that the rapporteur, mrs korhola, has done quite right in reintroducing most of the amendments from the first reading, because they were adopted unanimously by parliament. they also make an excellent contribution to improving the commission's proposals. the new article 7, which sets out the quality of environmental information, is one of the most important amendments. as someone has already said, the person requesting information must be able to trust that the information is relevant, up-to-date and accurate, and that the methods used to produce it and the analyses used are reliable. however, i am not satisfied with the environment committee's decision on the cost of what the authority is allowed to charge. that is why we have tabled amendment no 48. i hope that our british colleagues and other groups will see this as a compromise proposal and that they too will vote in favour of it tomorrow.
mr president, i would like to thank you. tomorrow my group, and i hope the whole of the european parliament, will vote in favour of the conciliation committee's motion for a resolution on the sixth environmental action programme. the committee on the environment, with riitta myller as rapporteur, has done a great deal of work and has also been successful in concretising and improving the basic proposal from the commission. i would like to thank mrs riitta myller, all my colleagues and of course the entire conciliation committee for making this work possible. it is clear that my group would have liked to see a perhaps more comprehensive, targeted and concrete environmental action programme for the next ten years, a real breakthrough for environmental policy and for ecologically sustainable development. now we have a programme full of expressions of will, such as 'promote', 'strive for', 'encourage' and 'aim at'. to prevent this from becoming mere wishful thinking, more general policy statements must be translated into concrete proposals and then realised and implemented. we must put our hopes in the commissioner for the environment, margot wallström, and the thematic strategies which are now to be adopted by codecision. the programme shall encourage the various actors in society to address environmental problems in an active and responsible manner. but we as legislators have the primary responsibility for defining the objectives and means of achieving such a development.
the commission. - madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the transeuropean networks policy was conceived ten years ago and is designed precisely to facilitate the completion of the internal market, and it must also be taken into account in the context of enlargement. today, as some of you have pointed out, unfortunately, after 10 years, only 20% of the network has been completed. to complete the network currently envisaged would require an investment of some eur 400 billion. once enlargement is achieved, we will be talking about investment figures of some 500 billion euros, and the reality is that community funding, today, taking into account all the funds available, does not reach 2.5 billion euros a year. the distance is therefore enormous and we see that the final major funding effort has to be made at national level. and that explains some of the things i'm going to say later. but it must also show us that the limited resources at our disposal must be concentrated more clearly on political objectives so that they can serve as a lever to accelerate the provision of national financing, whether public or private, and the implementation of these projects, which have, i recall, a community interest in their relation to the completion of the internal market. the priorities are: rebalancing transport modes, thus promoting intermodal transport, which is one of its corollaries, reabsorbing bottleneck bottlenecks, which will allow, with less investment, to have a greater impact on increasing capacity and improving the functioning of the whole system, and taking into account the priority of this commission, which is enlargement, which will also be the priority and the most decisive political element in the coming years, not only now, at the time of preparation, but once this enlargement takes place. i would like to congratulate mr bradbourn on his report, on the quality, on the rigour of the work he has done, which i hope will allow for the rapid adoption of this revision of guidelines, which, i repeat, is an intermediate review, because the big review, with the plans and maps, is due to come in 2004. the need to promote these transeuropean networks is a concern shared by the heads of state and government, and this was expressed at the barcelona european council - just a couple of months ago - and it was requested that this text be adopted by both parliament and the council before december 2002. i am pleased that the report you are considering supports the amendment of annex iii. i would remind you that virtually all the projects in annex iii were already in the transeuropean networks in 1996, or had been requested by the european parliament, and what is now given to them is a priority. annex iii updates the list of 1994 essen projects, and i have particularly appreciated the rigor of the majority of the members of the transport committee of the parliament in dealing with this element of the proposal. more than half of the amendments tabled by the committee on transport are very good and actually improve the commission's proposal and reinforce the priorities outlined in the white paper, whether in order to promote short sea shipping, inland waterway navigation or the integration of these systems with rail transport. in this sense, the commission may accept them in principle, in part or with amendments to their wording. however, there are some amendments which present difficulties for the commission, and i shall refer mainly to them. in particular, the proposals nos 53 to 56 and 61 to 66 which seek to amend annex iii. the commission's proposal is based on the principle of subsidiarity, which is the basis for the principle of subsidiarity. in that sense, the commission cannot accept these amendments and i am grateful that the rapporteur and the committee on transport of parliament cannot accept them either. i would like to refer to the amendments concerning the strategic environmental assessment of the network. this question was discussed at length in 1996, when the decision we are now trying to amend was adopted. in this respect, despite what mrs fleming has said, the commission cannot accept the part of amendment no 20 and amendments nos 40, 41, 42 and 43 which place the responsibility for strategic environmental assessments on the commission, when european legislation, the directive adopted in 2001, states that the task of implementing it falls to the member states. the commission cannot substitute itself for the member states in the discharge of their duties, that is a basic principle, and therefore it is necessary that the member states do not shirk their responsibilities and do not try to transfer to us responsibilities which they may not fulfil. the community could, however, contribute by providing added value, for example by supporting the development of evaluation methods. in this connection, we are prepared to accept amendment no 20 in part, but only in so far as it proposes the continuation of the development of these methods of evaluation. the commission cannot support amendments which again call into question the objective of rebalancing between new modes of transport and community financial instruments. in particular, amendments nos 9, 31, 33, 35 to 39, 45, 52 and 57. as far as galileo is concerned, we cannot go on making cost-benefit analyses every year, and you are already aware of the analyses carried out by various consultancies, and i refer to them. therefore, we cannot accept amendment no 10. on the other hand, the commission could never agree to include the reduction of transport demand among the objectives of the transeuropean network: i am sorry, that is not my responsibility as commissioner for transport or the responsibility of the transeuropean networks, and, moreover, nowhere, in any european council has it been said that what we want is to reduce transport demand. it's not about that, it's about disconnecting demand growth from what economic growth is. the commission has already taken a number of important steps in this direction. amendments nos 12 and 34 go beyond the definition of environmentally sound transport laid down by the gothenburg european council. on the other hand, many amendments relating to the priorities of community action, to article 5, raise certain problems for the commission: for example, nos 13 and 49, nos 15, 21, 17, 27 and 18, which are rejected by the commission. as regards the definition of the waterway network, i agree with mrs peijs on the importance of waterways, and we are undertaking very important initiatives and one of the projects we have included is precisely the improvement of the interconnection between the danube system and the rhine-rhone system. the problem is that there is a small problem, because there are no navigable rivers, unfortunately, in all the countries of the european union and in all the areas of the european union. it would be much more viable if there were navigable rivers throughout the european union. i know a country, and i know it very well, where unfortunately we do not have these rivers, and we are very envious when we see them in other countries, not only because of their beauty but also because of the possibilities they offer for transport. it's true we have other things, but we don't have those important rivers. in any case, what i want to say to you is that the commission supports the idea of including 'river information systems', but we cannot commit ourselves to presenting a plan for their deployment, because, unlike the railway sector, there is no community interoperability regulation in the river system. however, i would like to say to mrs peijs that, following her remarks, i will be discussing this with the commission services and we will see if we can make progress on these matters too, because it would be interesting to do so. finally, i cannot commit myself, i want to see the cost-benefit ratio, the possibilities that exist and, if it were feasible, i would make a proposal at the appropriate time to seek interoperability in inland navigation systems. we cannot accept amendments nos 25 and 26 either because we do not want to alter the proposed maps of inland ports any further. finally, i would like to say that i fully understand amendments nos 29, 30 and 46, which seek to remove from the list of priority projects in annex iii those projects which, after 15 years, have not yet been completed. i understand them very well, and they would make sense, but there is a problem: these projects of transeuropean interest are often promoted by the commission and accepted by the member states, but they show very little interest. i don't want to give examples, but we all have some. if there were a possibility offered by these amendments, some states would use it when a project, which is of great importance to the union, is of little national interest. it would be different if we were always talking about projects of national interest. in this case, a period of 15 years or even 10 years would make sense, but these are projects of community interest and, i repeat, the countries concerned often have only a very relative interest. we must therefore reject these amendments, as well as amendments nos 44 and 50, which prejudge the content of the next revision of the guidelines. finally, the commission cannot accept amendments nos 59 and 60, which delete or amend the maps of the transeuropean network annexed to the proposal for a decision, since these maps are used by both the commission and the member states. that is what you have raised and i have already referred to it on other occasions, including mr sterck. similarly, amendments nos 47 and 58 cannot be accepted, because they interfere with the justification of annex iii projects or with the principle of subsidiarity. i would like to answer mr savary's question that the funds are insufficient. i believe that this will be a reflection that will have to be put forward in the context of the new financial perspectives. we are indeed concentrating more funds here, but not increasing the allocation of funds. and with the concentration of funds what we are doing is recognizing that we are contributing more because of the great community interest of the projects. it is true that some countries may have more interest in a particular project. i think that a more thorough examination of the matter would lead us to the conclusion that more money should be given to the transeuropean networks, precisely to encourage the creation of infrastructures of community interest. i would remind mr watts, who has just left the chamber, that the fact that a project is of european interest is primarily a call to attention to the countries in which the project is being carried out. because the co-financing we provide is only partial. it is a system to incentivise the countries in which the project is carried out. in the end, the determination of the specific project to be selected and financed is made in the committee on the financing of the trans-european networks, where all the countries are represented, where it is therefore done in a balanced way, and where it is not in any case geared towards financing a particular country, but is done in a balanced way and taking into account the community interest. i would like to thank, ladies and gentlemen, the rapporteur for the excellent work he has done, and the whole transport committee, which has worked diligently and effectively on this matter. the adoption of this text, i hope without delay, will make it possible to concentrate community action on the essential issues set out in the white paper, proposing coherence throughout transport policy, and to ensure that the community's transport policy is not simply a matter of taking decisions, but of making decisions. it will also make it possible to prepare, under the best conditions, for a second review of the guidelines which i hope the commission will propose to this parliament in 2004. the aim is to continue the effort to concentrate on the major corridors, including maritime corridors, and to integrate the priority projects of the future new member states of the union.
