in writing. -sv) we swedish social democrats voted against amendments nos 2, 4 and 5. we believe that the machines exempted by these amendments are particularly polluting and have a significant impact on the health of their users. it is important to take account of the increase in weight of these machines from an ergonomie point of view, but also their emissions, which mainly affect the users, should be taken into account. we think the common position is better. the commission is also proposing to extend the deadline for the adoption of the directive to include the possibility of a new directive on the protection of the environment. in the decision, however, the commission will have to establish whether such a long period is really necessary or whether a shorter period would suffice. that is why the amendments say nothing. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the fact that the community's energy policy is not in fact a policy of the member states.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the question of genetically modified crops and foods can be viewed from several angles. for our group, it's about biodiversity, the environment, health and food safety. this also applies to the fundamental right of consumers to choose what they want to buy and what they want to eat. the question can also be seen from the perspective of power. much of the power over our food and crops is held by multinational corporations, which sometimes sell genetically modified seeds that are directly dependent on their own pesticides. all these reasons may be grounds for consumers to opt out of gm products. there is no scientific consensus on the blessing of gm technology in the food field. the technology is controversial, and our knowledge of the field is growing rapidly. in particular, there is uncertainty regarding the impact on biodiversity and possible effects on allergy sufferers. the precautionary principle must therefore be applied and we support the right of member states to put a moratorium on the commercial cultivation of gm crops. the commission's modernisation of eu policy in this area has made rules on traceability, labelling and corporate responsibility a precondition for the policy. we believe that, as a consequence of this, and as a minimum requirement, no commercial cultivation of gm crops should be allowed until these changes are made and implemented in national law. the starting point for labelling and traceability must be the creation of comprehensive systems that give consumers the right to choose. this means that goods produced with gm products, such as meat produced with gm feed, will also be labelled. of course, the inclusion of residues from non-authorised gm products should not be allowed. as regards the unintentional mixing of residues from gm crops, the threshold should be as low as possible. we believe that the proposed 0.5 per cent is too high, but that it can be a starting point for reducing it in the future. it is noteworthy that those who normally defend consumer choice in the market do not want to give consumers this freedom of choice by effectively labelling gm foods. could it be that it is realized that most consumers would use their freedom of choice to opt out of gm products? a system of labelling gm-free products would be a much worse solution than the one now proposed. such a system is based on the assumption that genetically modified food is the rule and non-gm food the exception. we want the opposite. we know that there is a strong pressure on this issue from industry and from some national governments to vote against the line adopted in the committee on the environment. i would appeal to members to take their responsibility for the environment and consumers and to support the line of the environment committee on this issue and not give in to either industry or 10 downing street.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is good that we now have a couple of directives on the labelling of genetically modified organisms. i think, however, that they are too late, about 10 to 15 years too late, because gmos are already being used in the production of a very large proportion of our food. when man began to cultivate crops and keep domestic animals about ten thousand years ago, he also began to refine the qualities of nature. now we can not only choose between different plants and different individuals, but we can choose traits in the individual. it's the same goal we've had for thousands of years, but we have new methods. let me confess that i believe in science, i believe in this technology, and i believe that we should use our knowledge, but we should be aware that new knowledge always involves new risks and requires increased caution. two areas are important to consider in this context, namely human health and the threat to the environment. many people are feeling anxious. we have reason to take this concern seriously. the consumer demands labelling, and the consumer is always right. we politicians have to take responsibility. if we take the environmental aspect seriously, as i do, unlike trakatellis, then all food must be labelled, even where gmos cannot be detected, and even where they have only been used as an aid to production. i therefore fully support the proposals of the committee on the environment. i also support some of the amendments tabled by the pse group and the greens, who go a step further. i think it's very important that even those of us who believe in technology itself are honest.

mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, what is it? i welcome this proposal on behalf of the liberal group, which seeks to tackle the growing xenophobia and racism in europe by means of legislation. this grim development goes against everything that the eu stands for: freedom, democracy, respect for human rights, the rule of law. i think ceyhun deserves a commendation for a very good job. it is important to work together within the eu to strengthen the protection of those affected by xenophobic and racist crimes, to ensure that the perpetrators are punished and that we close the loopholes in the legislation. the differences in legislation in the various member states must not be exploited by those who commit the crimes. racism and xenophobia must be tackled on a broad scale, as this report does. crimes must be punished, and crimes involving xenophobia and racism must be punished more severely; the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, what is it? i am pleased that we have received another communication from the commission on developing the local dimension of the european employment strategy. i think this is extremely important and i have tried to develop these ideas in my report. there is an unfortunate tradition in employment policy of thinking in national terms, that is, in terms of national labour markets and economies, and of seeing the labour market as a single market. this, however, ignores the local labour markets, their differences and their importance. there is also a tendency for local labour markets to become a kind of residual concept dealing with slum areas or weak parts of the economy and the social economy. in my report i speak of local labour markets in a different sense, namely as labour markets which produce goods and services which are consumed locally. this includes healthcare workers and craftsmen, people working in transport and technical services, local merchants, small businesses, local public services and so on, and also includes important infrastructure in the form of training and other things. so these are important issues, also from an economic point of view. the area is also interesting because of its countercyclical economic importance. local labour markets are less affected by fluctuations in world market conditions. this means that in a period like the present, when the world economy is slowing down, local labour markets are doing relatively well. the recovery of the economy from the current downturn is particularly dependent on the proper functioning of local labour markets, which are the springboard from which new businesses can emerge and new activities can develop. i would also like to stress the importance of these local labour markets. in my report i point out that it covers at least 30% of total employment. the percentage may be higher, depending on how you count. the problem is not marginal, but a very important and economically very important part of the overall employment policy. i must also stress that these local labour markets are extremely important for the quality of life and social welfare in various ways. it is a labour market dominated by women, which is very important from the point of view of employment: if we are to promote and increase employment, it is not least the employment of women that must be strengthened. it is also the first job market for young people after school and the easiest job market for those new to the labour market. the local labour market thus plays an important role as a bridge to the rest of the labour market. finally, the local labour market is of great importance to the large industrial firms, perhaps even multinational companies, because it is embedded in the local social fabric and provides high-quality staff for those firms which need to compete at international level. there are therefore many strong social and economic reasons for focusing more on local labour markets. within the european member states there is a very large regional and local variation, which suggests that there is much that can be done. i would like to emphasize that this is as much a question of metropolitan areas and urban regions as of other areas. in the large cities, there are large local differences between poor immigrant and wealthy urban areas and between the labour markets associated with them. many of the social problems that arise are reflected in the employment situation. finally, i would like to comment on the three amendments that have been tabled. i have proposed local partnerships that are open to many different kinds of actors. in the first amendment, the proposers wanted to delete some of these operators, which i do not understand. perhaps i'll get an explanation later in the discussion. in the second amendment i am in favour of quantitative benchmarks for education policy, on which, not surprisingly, there may be some disagreement. i have no objection to the third amendment, but i am happy to support it. finally, i would like to thank the other political groups for the good cooperation they have had in the committee on this important subject.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would also like to join the chorus of congratulations to herman schmid for a good report and good cooperation within the committee. in my view, the big problem for the future of the labour market is that we see that in the not too distant future we will have a shortage of labour throughout the european union. this is already the case in some sectors. at the same time, we have a lot of unemployed in the union that we are not able to take advantage of. the local dimension comes into play here. we are developing strategies centrally which must be translated locally if we are to be able to match labour market policy with the needs we see in the labour market. we must do this to make the key objectives known locally, but also to make us aware at a central level of the problems facing local labour markets. i'll get in touch with this myself. as chairman of cross-border in the öresund region, i can share the objectives we have at the heart of labour market policy, but also listen to the problems that are seen in a border region on local labour markets. we need to get a match like that in the future. it is quite obvious that many actors will be involved at central level but above all at local level. examples of such actors are the social partners, grassroots movements and disability organisations, in order to design programmes for vulnerable groups. we should also act through the european social fund. i have seen that the social fund plays a major role in many cases in the local labour market in supporting the strategies we are developing at central level.

in writing. - sv) we support the ceyhun report as a laudable contribution to the fight against racism and xenophobia in europe. racism is directly opposed to the values on which the european union was built, namely human rights, equality, freedom and democracy. a common definition of racist and xenophobic offences, as well as a harmonisation of the classification of offences, could be an essential tool in combating all those acts which constitute attacks or persecution against individuals or groups of persons. at the same time, it is our duty as democrats to open the debate to racists. if we kill their arguments, democracy and tolerance will emerge in a remarkably large way. freedom of expression, freedom of association and freedom of the press are fundamental freedoms and may not be restricted in any way. in sweden, freedoms are enshrined in the constitution and any limitations must be carefully examined. it is therefore difficult to strike a balance between these freedoms and an effective fight against racism and xenophobia. in view of this, we are reluctant to support the proposal to ban the distribution of documents, as well as the proposal to criminalize in practice membership of certain organisations and parties.

in writing. - (sv) we have voted against the whole of the stevenson report. the attempt to use the ban on the use of animal proteins in animal feed as a pretext for applying for community support for lupins and other crops rich in vegetable proteins is unfounded. the demand for a 'safety net' or an income insurance scheme for oil and protein producers is equally unjustified. the commission's solution, namely almost exclusive recourse to the world market, would, in the commission's view, weaken the eu and make it 'vulnerable, in particular to climatic variations, crop failures and even price increases by the main supplier countries in the world (the united states, argentina and brazil).' we see nothing wrong or strange in this, just the opposite. as liberals, we believe in globalization, free trade and international solidarity. we cannot therefore support the stevenson report.

the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. thank you, ladies and gentlemen, and a big thank you to the danish presidency! as mr wynn said, we really appreciated the constructive attitude we met at the july conciliation, when for the first time in many years we actually reached a settlement with real substance. then why did we make this arrangement with regard to category 5? wynn's been in the little grand. we were under a lot of pressure from the member states and the council and finally decided to say ok to not using the flexibility instrument. there was another reason, though. we believe that we must make the utmost use of all the means at our disposal to rationalize, prioritise, redistribute resources, increase efficiency and create modern management within the european union as well. we must make the most of these possibilities, especially when we are faced with such an extremely important task as enlargement. i do, however, share the commission's view on a slightly longer perspective. it is difficult to put on the commission a number of large new tasks over the long term without also providing the necessary resources, for example in terms of staff and administrative resources. this will probably be a conflict in the future, but this year i think it was necessary to do it this way. what is the general budgetary situation for the autumn budgetary process? we must first of all discuss what parliament has been pointing out for several years, namely the weak implementation in many areas. each year, large budgetary resources are allocated and then not used. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the problem is that we have also set ourselves a certain level of ambition through the commitments in the budget, which we must try to achieve. this must also be an ambition of the member states and the council. the finance ministers cannot just sit back and applaud when they get back money that has not been spent. we have a political responsibility to actually implement the ambitions and programmes we have set ourselves in the european union. this is why parliament periodically increases the payments. we want to signal that more needs to be done and we need to put in more resources to actually implement the promises made. i believe that we will have a debate on this subject in parliament this year too. however, it is not certain that simply increasing payments is enough, and we have had some examples of this. sometimes, however, increased payments do not allow the commission to use those resources. we must therefore discuss the commission's human resources in the slightly longer term, as i have already said. we may also have to start discussing reducing commitments. in some areas, where we see that the commission is not doing what we have asked for, we may even have to start discussing whether we should cut staff or at least put staff resources into reserve. it is not possible to retain human resources for a certain level in the long term, even if it is not implemented. this problem of payments and implementation is also very serious in the field of foreign and aid policy. year after year we have seen the council add new ambitions. in 2000, it was kosovo, and then the interest in implementing the earlier promises concerning africa and latin america diminished slightly. in 2001, serbia was added, and interest in the old priorities waned slightly. in 2002, the global health fund was created. this year we're discussing afghanistan, the middle east and the police force in bosnia. we cannot add new tasks every year and forget the old ones. there is a limit to when new resources must be added if we are to maintain all the priorities we have set. this year we will have a very difficult time maintaining the priority for the global health fund. the budget already includes very large cuts in respect of kosovo, humanitarian aid, etc. this is unsustainable in the long run. if we are to add new priorities every year, new resources must also be added. let me say a few words about simplification. we have studied structural funds and a variety of policy programmes. i have travelled around, most recently in finland, and i have seen that regional organisations, universities, etc., give up on applying for funds from the union budget because it is so horribly cumbersome. i think this is one reason why the union's money is not actually being spent. it's time to get serious about this. we must sit down within the union and review our methods, documents and bureaucracy, so that the management becomes a little easier for all those people who need to use the union's resources. mr president, i would like to thank you. i believe that these are some of the most important aspects which we will continue to discuss in parliament, in the committee on budgets and in future budgetary debates.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. let me also begin by thanking the danish presidency for the excellent cooperation it has shown so far. since we started work on the 2003 budget early this winter, at least my objective as rapporteur and responsible for next year's budget for eight of the eu institutions has been to find a solution where the eu's administrative budget is in balance and where we are not going to recover extra money by using the so-called flexibility instrument. this has not been an easy task, as we had a very large deficit forecast in this budget at the beginning of the year. the agreement reached on 19 july by the two budgetary authorities of the eu, the council and parliament, has nevertheless proved that this is possible. now that we have achieved this objective, i would say that it is a very great success for parliament. we have received support for reforms that are necessary, not least in an enlarged eu. the flexibility instrument shall not be used in respect of the administrative budget of the union. we're going to respect the category 5 ceiling. the settlement now provides for a joint commitment to address the remaining problems in category 5 by means of frontloading. the latter is important, as a number of problems remain to be solved in the last months of the year. i am convinced, however, that we too will be able to do so. mr president, i would like to thank you. in this context, it is very easy to be blinded by all the details, and the budgetary work certainly contains a great many details. but together we have created better conditions for moving forward with the historic task of welcoming members from perhaps ten new member states to this parliament and to the other eu institutions in a few years' time. this is, in my view, the most important result of the settlement. as early as next year, these countries can send observers to what will also be their parliament. the result of the agreement is that we are not only living up to our commitments, but also to the priorities decided upon by the committee on budgets and parliament at the beginning of this year. firstly, it is about ensuring that the eu institutions are given the opportunity to prepare for enlargement. secondly, it is a question of implementing the necessary reforms. thirdly, it is intended that both these objectives should be achieved within the budgetary framework. mr president, i would like to thank you. my final comment on the agreement between parliament and the council is to the danish presidency, which has, in my constructivity in a way that has made an agreement possible. the council has shown a willingness to listen, which has not always been the council's characteristic in these contexts. for this i am mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, what is it? mr friedrich, citizenship is to be seen as a contract between the state and the individual and implies the granting of reciprocal rights and obligations to the parties. citizenship of the union is a logical extension of the long-established de facto rights and obligations of various kinds between the union and the citizens of the member states. a natural step in the development of citizenship of the union is, of course, a binding statute which clearly sets out and clarifies the fundamental rights and obligations. the charter should be seen as an important element of the substance of the principles of citizenship of the union. the charter is the very essence of citizenship. the need for citizenship of the union has become fully apparent in the light of the necessary curtailment of individual rights that we have seen in the aftermath of the disaster of 11 september. the need for a developed citizenship of the union is thus evident, one which better defines the basic contract between the individual and the union, and balances rights and restrictions within the same system of political decision-making. it is becoming increasingly incomprehensible that some social democratic governments are opposed to the merger of the community and the union, which is absolutely necessary to clarify the fundamental duties and rights. without that simplification, citizenship of the union becomes difficult to understand and more or less impossible to communicate to citizens of the union. it should be borne in mind that the outcome of the national elections currently under way may indeed have both developing and devastating effects on the rights of citizens. citizenship of the union can be an important tool in explaining the meaning of eu membership to europe's citizens. however, this requires a clearer and simpler treaty. in the interinstitutional committee on access to documents, i, as the representative of the european parliament, and mr haarder, the representative of the council, have reached agreement on information directed to the citizen. it is about informing citizens how and where they can get the information that will enable them to participate in european democracy, simple and concise information in the european newspapers on citizens' rights. the money is there, but the political will is too slow to be achieved. this could be a simple way of creating an understanding of citizenship. it is therefore unacceptable that this proposal should be delayed at a time which is so important for european democracy. the commission, which is responsible for the next interinstitutional meeting, should therefore organise such a meeting quickly, very quickly, so that the democratic development of europe is not in danger of being delayed. finally, i would like to thank mr coelho warmly for his very constructive contribution to the democratic development of europe, where citizenship of the union is so central.
the committee on employment and social affairs has tabled an opinion on the proposal. madam president, i would like to thank you. we're busy making long wish lists. we are talking about the coming knowledge society and lifelong learning, and there is no limit to what we want in full agreement. however, this is merely a kind of pirate policy, because the reality of education policy in the member states is quite different. there, training expenditure must be prioritised and weighed against other expenditure. right now, they're cutting back on education. well, then it doesn't help much that we're sitting here talking about our long lists. we should be prioritizing instead of making long lists. we should prioritize the most important tasks. i agree with those who say that we must then give priority to the needs of the less educated. the education system reinforces differences if it does not build compensation. i would like to highlight two aspects in particular which i think are important and which are perhaps a little under-emphasized in the long lists. firstly, there is a huge need for retraining for a whole profession. it's not enough to have a little training here, a little extra training here and an extra course there. training is needed from a profession that is no longer needed such as agricultural, forestry or fisheries workers to a profession in an expanding service sector. such a transition is not accomplished by a six-week course, but by long training programs. however, such training programs are quite expensive, and it is therefore difficult to obtain political support for them. this is extremely important, however, because we in europe are facing major structural change problems which will require the entire change of occupation. secondly, i would like to emphasize learning organizations or learning embedded in work processes. this is certainly mentioned in the commission's explanatory statement, but it should be given more attention. as we know that it will be difficult to obtain financial resources for a large amount of schooling, it is extremely important that we try to create forms of organisation and work which allow for continuous training, schooling and learning as part of the work process. it is difficult to send people to courses, but it may be easier, cheaper and better to ensure that people can learn on the job. the new it sector is an example. all the small it companies that were growing in the '90s could afford to let people experiment and learn in the workplace, and it was very successful. this is a lesson to be learned.

the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. it is now almost two years since the commission presented a very good proposal for a regulation in october 2000 on the use of parts of slaughtered animals not intended for human consumption. i have to say, it was shocking to discover how much weirdness and filth was still going on in the feed and food chain. this will now be an end to it. the commission's proposal was a good one. parliament, however, did it much better, much simpler, much more rigorously and much more transparently than it had done it from the beginning. at first reading we had 99 amendments, and the council accepted 90 of them. we tabled nine amendments at second reading, eight of which were adopted. the ninth, a compromise amendment, was eventually passed almost completely. we had a proposal, however, where we asked the commission to submit a proposal, and in that respect we in parliament probably went beyond our powers. the commission has nevertheless voluntarily promised to submit the proposal we asked for. the most important point made by parliament is that in future there will be a total ban on cannibalism in the feed sector. it will no longer be permissible to feed a species with proteins from its own species. such feeding is currently believed by scientists to be a possible cause of 'mad prions', i.e. bse, tse, creutzfeldt-jakob disease, etc. we were completely banned from using carcasses and parts of sick animals, both as protein flour and, above all, as fat. this fat is unusually disgusting. only fully food-grade raw materials will be allowed to be used in feed, cosmetics, pharmaceuticals, etc. it is only in cigarette filters, car tires and the like that cadaver fat may be used in the future. we have asked for and received a clear separation of the different animal by-products from the chain of distribution. for example, food-approved and non-food-approved by-products may not be handled in the same building, but in clearly separated buildings and clearly separated transport containers. we have also in fact achieved indelible colour and, where technically possible, odour marking of all non-food by-products. this is to protect our farmers in europe. it is impossible to see where the protein or fat comes from in a bag of processed feed, but now it will no longer be possible to deceive those who feed their animals. i must thank all the shadow rapporteurs and all those with whom i have worked in parliament. it's been fun and inspiring. we've had a fight, we've had a conversation, and we've come to an agreement. there has been a very good atmosphere and good debate in the groups. i would also like to thank the commission and in particular colombo for the intensive and good technical cooperation. i would also like to thank the working party of the council of ministers and those involved in the presidencies - the swedish, the belgian, the spanish and now the danish. we have all worked together, and we have done a very good job. i therefore recommend that we unanimously accept the conciliation proposal when we vote tomorrow. it is important that this work be completed now. the by-product industry employs tens of thousands of people across europe, mainly in rural areas. it is very important that they now start their investments and their reconstruction, and that they have the opportunity to continue with, for all of us, a very good industry.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. when the cartagena protocol was adopted a few years ago, it was a major step forward for both the european union and the member states. the protocol is based on the precautionary principle and respects the right of states to have more stringent national rules on trade in genetically modified products. the protocol is an important example of how global trade rules can take environmental and consumer protection as a starting point. this is a positive alternative to some of the practices of the world trade organisation. with the adoption of the draft law under discussion, the cartagena protocol will become eu law. the commission has also proposed that the eu should be able to provide for the free movement of goods within the eu. we're in a hurry to adopt the rules. the number of countries that have ratified the protocol is increasing so that it can soon formally enter into force. by adopting this legislation, the eu can set a positive example in this area at the global level. we in parliament would like to work closely with the commission and the council to ensure that these laws are completed as quickly as possible. as rapporteur, i believe that the commission's legislative proposal has several weaknesses. in important areas the proposals do not go as far as the cartagena protocol itself, and in other areas they create unnecessary ambiguities. that is why i and the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection are proposing tougher rules on a number of points. no seed or products not approved for use within the european union may be exported. exceptions should be made only for seeds for climatic reasons, for example. we cannot have different standards of safety and consumer protection for ourselves and for others. the acceptance of an export shall be subject to the express authorization of the importing country. a non-response should never be interpreted as an authorization, as is indeed the case in the commission's draft law. this is particularly true for countries with limited technical capacity in this area, as it is crucial to ensure that they are actually able to promote and manage imports. the actual exporter shall always be responsible for the notification. this is important to avoid unclear liability. it is also more in line with what the cartagena protocol actually provides. in the further negotiations under the cartagena protocol, the european union will support proposals for international rules clarifying the legal responsibilities of companies. the right of the public to information on what is being exported must be clarified and strengthened while respecting legitimate business secrets. the eu regulatory framework must also make it clear that exports to other countries must always respect the stricter rules that those countries are entitled to impose, in full compliance with the cartagena protocol. i therefore hope that as many of the amendments tabled by the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection and those tabled by the gue/ngl group, the greens/efa group and the pse group will be adopted jointly. i would also like to thank the responsible officials of the commission for the very good cooperation. i also thank my colleagues on the committee on the environment, public health and consumer affairs.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would also like to begin by thanking the commission for its proposal and jonas sjöstedt for his good and fine work in strengthening and clarifying what is really at stake in this case. the cartagena protocol on biosafety is intended to provide a framework for international trade in gmos. it is based on two important principles, the precautionary principle and the principle that countries which want stricter rules have the right to do so. some speakers here today seem to think that the cartagena protocol is something quite different, aimed at facilitating trade as much as possible. however, the cartagena protocol is an agreement on biosafety. the precautionary principle must be the guiding principle, there is no other way. it is therefore essential that the european parliament votes in favour of amendment no 52, which is also supported by the rapporteur. it states that the transboundary movement of genetically modified organisms 'shall be based on the precautionary principle in each individual case and at each stage'. we can't let this go to waste. my colleague evans raised another important point, namely the ethics of the cartagena protocol, which is based on the idea that countries which do not want to produce or consume gmos in any form must have the right to say no. the aid consignments with gmos, which were discussed at the johannesburg meeting, must not take place. we must help to ensure that people do not feel obliged to receive such broadcasts. the eu is also committed to the principle of subsidiarity, which is a fundamental principle of the eu. in this situation, i doubt whether the adoption by the european parliament of amendments nos 50 and 51 will be in the interest of the cartagena protocol or whether their content will be respected, since the intentions of the protocol will not be able to be implemented in this case. i therefore recommend that these amendments be rejected, since they are designed to ensure that trade is hindered as little as possible. the reason for the cartagena protocol is that gmos and trade require special precautions. we can never have too much security, but we can have too little. there must be no doubt that the precautionary principle is the one that applies in community law and that it applies in full. i fully support this report.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank both my colleagues and margot wallström for the views expressed in this debate, which i think give a fairly clear picture of the lines of division. i think there are a few factors to be borne in mind in this protocol. the first is, of course, that this is a matter of minimum rules. we must do what the protocol requires of us, but we are allowed, by protocol, to go further if we wish. this is the situation. this of course also means that other countries around the world can go further than the protocol requires. what i am saying in this report is that we will respect that. we demand that our exporters respect the cartagena protocol and therefore also the legislation that other countries may adopt, which may go further than that of the european union. if we did not, we would be in fact acting contrary to both the spirit and the letter of the cartagena protocol. i'm not sure that's possible. secondly, it should be noted that the cartagena protocol is under development. regular negotiations are ongoing. several areas of the protocol are not yet cleared up, only a course of action is being indicated. other areas are cleared. we must therefore bear in mind that we need a forward-looking policy that shows a direction for how the eu intends to develop the cartagena protocol in the future. the liability framework is precisely that. this is not about eu liability legislation, which we are discussing elsewhere. this is article 27 of the cartagena protocol, which says that we must start negotiating on global responsibility for this type of product. we in parliament support the commission and the member states in their efforts to promote a global system of liability in future negotiations under the cartagena protocol, which will of course benefit the serious players in this field. that is the kind of order that is required. as regards the amendments, i think that amendments nos 50 and 51 seriously weaken the report. they are designed to take away important areas from this. amendment no 50 in particular is in fact contrary to the cartagena protocol. we cannot accept that amendment. i would also like to ask those who see gmos differently from what i do to think for a moment. of course, we have different assessments of the possibilities and different assessments of the risks that may be associated with this technology. whatever we think about the future of this technology, i think there is something we have to consider, that we have to have a regulatory system that makes the companies that act credible. does exporting to countries that have not given their permission make you credible? does exporting to a country that has not been able to respond to the request make you credible? i think that an industry which has acted in this way is embarrassing. we have no interest and industry should have no interest in rules which actually circumvent the right of countries to respond to whether they want to import something or not! the next question is: do we gain credibility by exporting to others what is not good enough for ourselves? will we gain credibility by exporting what we have failed to do, for example, for reasons of environmental safety, biodiversity or consumer protection? of course we won't. even those who have a very positive view of this industry, even though this is not at all uncomplicated, there are both advantages and disadvantages, should see that it is in everyone's interest to have a comprehensive, credible regulatory framework. if the european union adopts this framework, it will have an impact not only on us and our exports but also on the global level. it shows that even the major global players are taking this issue seriously and are prepared to take their responsibilities. that is the purpose of my report.
in writing. - (sv) in view of the potential risk of gmos to the environment in general and to biological diversity in particular, the swedish christian democrats have chosen to support a restrictive policy. in accordance with the precautionary principle, we believe that the laws in force in importing countries must be respected by exporters. we also believe that it is of the utmost importance that common rules on civil liability be defined at international level. it is also clear that we cannot export gmos, which we ourselves prohibit from being present in the european union. the provisions of the regulation are to be applied irrespective of the date of entry into force of the cartagena protocol.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. as chairman of the committee on budgets, mr wynn, i am very grateful for this new debate. we launched the idea last spring, and we were very uncertain whether we would be able to get the idea through. we have had the overall aim of trying to reform the budgetary work of the european parliament, not least in view of the completely new method of so-called activity-based budgeting which will come into force next year. in this context, it has also been important to be able to renew the debate itself, perhaps the most visible part of parliament's budgetary work. this reform also means that we have sought to increase the involvement of committees in the work. i have been to virtually all the committees and discussed budgetary matters, and we are now also inviting the committees to take an active part in today's debate. we will now be tightening the requirements for the actual implementation of parliament's budgetary decisions by increasing the focus in working groups and in various types of reports on the degree of implementation and on the major shortcomings we have in the budget in this area. in this context, i welcome the way in which the commission, the council, a number of commissioners and the danish minister for finance, in his role as president, have presented themselves in the debate, and i hope that parliament's own representatives will also make a real effort to ensure that we have a more exciting and constructive debate than the rather predictable monologues that have been delivered so far. the eu budgetary system suffers from serious shortcomings, not least the lack of flexibility. i think that both parliament and, i am sure, the public find it difficult to understand why it is absolutely impossible to find resources for the offensive part of the agricultural policy called rural development, while billions are left over in the second part of the agricultural policy. that is why it has also been difficult for us to say no and to try to find a solution to the administrative expenditure needed for enlargement without using the flexibility instrument, knowing that there were some resources elsewhere which were not being used. consequently, the innovations of the year also include a more systematic attempt to identify and use unused resources and a more systematic effort to anticipate expenditure in order to relieve pressure on a heavily burdened heading in the coming years, which could, for example, facilitate the meeting of needs for enlargement. this is a temporary solution. i think we need a much more comprehensive budgetary reform. i also think we should raise this issue in the convention. the extremely inflexible budgetary ceilings with which we are living today should not be permanent in the future, they should definitely not be treaty-bound, and of course parliament should have the same co-decision rights in agricultural policy as we have in other areas. i would also like to thank the danish presidency for its very serious and constructive approach so far. we reached good agreements in july and we had an exciting trialogue yesterday on how to solve the problem of flooding. i would like to conclude this introduction by appealing for the same constructive attitude to be adopted in solving the major remaining problems, particularly in heading 4, external expenditure. we'd need a lot more flexibility. parliament will protest against our constantly adding new expenditure, for example on afghanistan and the global aids and health fund, on top of the efforts we have already made on the balkans, kosovo, serbia, etc., without adding new resources. i think that will be one of the main points of criticism by parliament of the way the commission and the council are handling the budget.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. the administrative budget of the eu has long been one of the eu's major budgetary problems. this year's work is about trying to find long-term solutions to this. at the beginning of the year we could foresee a large deficit in the 2003 budget. all the calculations pointed to this. now that we have reduced this deficit and are actually seeing a smaller surplus for next year, we can say that in the long term 2003 is probably the big problem. in accordance with the decision of the berlin european council in 1999, we have the possibility of using the so-called flexibility instrument and thus also of making additional funds available. in its preliminary draft budget the commission proposed this for heading 5. there were obviously reasons for this, not least because time is short and there is so much to be done. let me emphasize that there are now only two and a half months left until the final decision on the new membership countries is taken. there are six months left before 147 observers from the future member states can be present here in the european parliament. in just 15 months, 2004 will begin, the year in which everything changes, in which the eu will be a completely different organisation from what it has been up to now, in which we will have, hopefully, ten new members, in which we will take decisive steps towards the reunification of a europe that has always been divided into east and west. it's a huge commitment, probably the biggest one any of us will ever experience. it is therefore gratifying to note that the vast majority of the budgetary puzzle pieces are beginning to fall into place. enlargement is the top priority. the necessary reforms, not least in this parliament, are well under way and must be carried out within the budgetary limits and within the time available to us. however, while the final countdown has begun and time is running out, there is still a lot of work to be done, including on the 2003 budget. against this background, the commission's proposal to use the so-called flexibility instrument was of course very much in order. nevertheless, parliament and the council chose to oppose the commission's proposal. as a first step in the right direction, parliament and the council have agreed on the 'broad lines of the administrative budget'. this is something entirely new in the budgetary procedure, but it is an advantage in the work that is going on. the commitment that parliament and the council have made, and that the other institutions must now make, is to enable the commission to make the necessary efforts and preparations. this must be done with the underlying awareness and understanding that enlargement is not something that one institution can manage, but that it is the commitment of all of us. parliament has therefore tried to be particularly creative in order to make room for the necessary funding. in short, it is a matter of trying to make as much as possible already in the current year's budget in order to make the most of the 2003 budget, when enlargement is only a year away. the scope thus created will then be able to provide the basis for the necessary priorities for 2003. this is what we call frontloading. all the institutions, and i stress all the institutions, including parliament and the council, have been called upon to play an active part in this area. the reactions i have received so far have been positive. i am convinced that we will succeed and thus create the economic space which the commission, and others, need to make enlargement a success.
the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has tabled an opinion on the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you. i would first like to congratulate both rapporteurs on the great and innovative work they have done and are doing. the draft budget, as regards the responsibilities of the committee on economic and monetary affairs, is largely acceptable that is why we countries which are members of the union but have not yet adopted the euro. information in the candidate countries should be financed through budget lines relating to enlargement. as regards the budget line on industrial competitiveness in the eu, where the commission has proposed a 12% increase in appropriations, the committee on economic and monetary affairs proposes an amendment to the mr president, i would like to thank you. let me comment directly on commissioner nielson's remarks. it is not so simple that we, at least not on the part of parliament, can just wait and see. in parallel with the budget, as you know, commissioner nielson, we are working on the directive for which you are responsible and which sets the course for the next four years, where the commission has proposed a total amount of eur 300 million. this amount, according to the committee on development and cooperation, is far too little money, both for the necessary flexibility and to go in and support the global fund for aids and health, but also to ensure that the union's own work, through our own secretariat resources, is as ambitious as possible. i think it should be welcomed, both in the council and in the commission, that parliament has this very ambitious approach to an issue and to a problem which will undoubtedly dominate the development debate, not only in africa, but in many other regions in the coming decades. apart from this question, there are a couple of other factors which are extremely important to note when we discuss the part of the budget which concerns development cooperation. howitt has already mentioned the focus on poverty reduction, health and education. i would like to say to mr nielson that one of the reasons why we are coming back with clarifications in these areas is that the indications we are getting from the commission's work on the ground is that health and education in particular are still receiving a very small share of the aid, which we consider to be clearly insufficient and clearly wrong. finally, it is vital that we increase our flexibility within the european union and ensure that needs arising from war and disasters are not constantly being met and burdened with long-term development programmes. that is why the proposal to finance aid to afghanistan through the flexibility reserve is very, very interesting.

the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. my colleagues! i would like to start by saying that unemployment is increasing in europe, it is increasing in germany, it is increasing in france and it is increasing in other member states. the economy has stagnated and the european employment strategy faces major challenges. it has to be much more efficient. looking at the research on which the commission's report is based, it is clear that the main practical problem with the european employment process is that it is not sufficiently effective in the member states, that it has had only a marginal impact on national employment strategies and that it is largely unknown to the social partners. this pattern must be broken. the european employment strategy must be linked to the national employment strategies of the member states and must also be implemented at regional and local level. the question is how to do this. my answer is clear and unambiguous: the national and regional parliaments must be allowed to discuss and decide on the national action plans. it may seem obvious that national action plans should be decided and discussed in national parliaments, but in fact this is not the case in any member state today. this is all the more important since it is the parliaments which, when they approve the national employment policy, also allocate the financial means needed to make the policy a reality. a national action plan coming from europe and outside the parliaments, and the budgetary process that goes with it, will of course be ineffective. this is a very important question. i also think that the commission concentrates a great deal of its reports on how european cooperation should be developed, coordinated and synchronised. i think that is very good, but i would like to make two points. firstly, administrative coordination must also result in coordination in the world of work. it is not enough for officials to cooperate better across borders, something must also be done on the labour markets. new jobs must be created and more employment created, otherwise the result will be mere talk. secondly, to my surprise, the commission's synchronisation report deals with only two processes, namely the economy and employment. i wonder what happened to the social process. of course, coordination must be based on all three processes. they are equal and each has his contribution to make. if the social process were to be pushed out, we would soon have a purely employer perspective instead of a set of comprehensive solutions. the result is conflict and strife instead of constructive cooperation. i would therefore urge the commission to stick to the lisbon decisions, which emphasise both economic growth and social integration, which emphasise quality of work as much as quantity, and which make full employment a top priority with a sustainability perspective. i would also like to stress the value of the open method of coordination, which invites member states to participate in a learning and development process that can be far more effective than traditional legislative governance. the method is only at its beginning, it needs to be developed and implemented more consistently, not least within the individual member states. there it can be combined with entrepreneurship and development strategies that create both jobs and a skilled workforce. finally, i would also like to say to the council of ministers, which is not in session and which will meet on 8 october, that it is not enough to make some simplifications in a bureaucratic european cooperation process. political priorities and practical initiatives for action are also needed, building on the lisbon decisions. we also need important changes in the member states if we are ever to have a coordinated and effective european employment process. we therefore need a bottom-up process, complementary to the current top-down european process.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking herman schmid for the very good cooperation he has had in drawing up this report. he's been very constructive. i share the commissioner's view that the employment strategy has been successful as far as we can see. in the five years since it was introduced, unemployment has fallen, the number of jobs has increased and, for example, active labour market policies, which were previously at a very low level in europe, have now been significantly improved, in particular through lifelong learning. there have been great improvements. however, as the commission document states, much remains to be done. unemployment is still too high. there is a huge gap in education and lifelong learning. those who already have a high level of education also take the most part of the training in the labour market, and those with the least education do not receive an equal share. there are also major regional differences within europe. the review and improvement of the employment strategy is therefore necessary. i personally think that the pillar structure has been a good structural instrument, because some of the elements of the pillar structure have been more successful than others. the employability player has been relatively successful, and the gender equality player has also been successful in the sense that women's employment has increased, although much remains to be done. however, the adaptation player, which is a little harder to understand, has not been as successful. at the same time, this is an important area on which we must continue to work. this is where the influence on working life, the working environment and lifelong learning in the workplace lie. all these factors need to be improved in the future. it is good that the commission is now proposing a coordination of employment and economic policies, but i share herman schmid's view that social security, where we also have a european process, must also be involved in this coordination. when coordination is carried out, no area shall be subordinate to another. it is easy, both at national and european level, to put economic policy and the economic process ahead of employment and social policy. that cannot happen, they are equivalent processes which must be treated equally when coordination takes place. i regard open coordination as a complement to legislation, not as an alternative. however, we must improve the management of the open method of coordination itself. in the absence of a qualified majority on the council's objectives, it is reasonable that the european parliament should have a right of co-decision on these objectives. i share herman schmidt's view on the national action plans. it is obvious that they must be discussed and decided upon in the national parliaments. it is reasonable that we have democratic control both at european and national level on these important issues. it is also important to involve other actors. among these actors, the social partners are perhaps the most important. it is important that they are involved in policy-making at both the european and the other levels. it is also important, as mentioned in our proposal, that the candidate countries are involved early in this process. there are some differences between the candidate countries and our countries, but there are also similarities. demographic trends and the demands they place on the labour market are similar in the candidate countries. consequently, they need to be involved in this process soon. we also want a clearer link between employment policy and the existing financial instruments, namely the european social fund and equal. the employment strategy in those instruments must be clear, clearer than it is today. in general, we are positive about the development of this process, but as mentioned earlier, institutional changes are needed: a strengthening of the european parliament, a strengthening of the national parliaments, better coordination. we must not forget, however, that economic policy, employment policy and the respective councils must be on an equal footing.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. thank you to the danish presidency and to the commission! i would like to begin in a positive spirit and say that i fully agree with the commission and the council that we should not be too critical and negative about the outcome of the johannesburg meeting. after all, there is no way to measure the benefits of ensuring that more people have access to water, proper sanitation, food, and an environment with fewer chemicals. i think we should say that it is indeed a win and that we should share the honour together. if it had not been for the european union, i believe that we would never have got as far as we did in johannesburg. perhaps this is part of the frustration of parliament. in this parliament, we have been actively working for sustainable development since before the gothenburg summit. many have likened our wish list to a christmas tree that has been overly decorated. we have nevertheless agreed on the requirements and on the process we wanted the union to undertake in order to make the necessary changes. our frustration is that we can see that the results are not reaching the end. if our wish list was considered to be an overly decorated christmas tree, then it is safe to say that the result from johannesburg is more like a barred christmas tree but a christmas tree nonetheless. our frustration is also expressed, i think, when we are not allowed to use our capacity and our knowledge at the summits. i think the commission should take up this message. it is a question of the attitude between parliament and the commission. this fact, however, is worth a much lesser degree of frustration than the situation we should really be frustrated about, namely, the situation in which the world finds itself. this is the problem we should really be discussing today. the problems that may exist between parliament and the commission about who should say what at international meetings can be brought up in a forum other than this chamber, and we should now concentrate on the problem that we really need to do something about. it is good to stress that we must now begin the real work. we must ensure that the commitments and promises made by the eu in johannesburg are now reflected in our concrete policies and reflected in our budget. i would like both the council and the commission to come back to parliament in february to report on what has been done so far and what they want to see done in the future to fulfil the commitments made in johannesburg. in this context, i would naturally like to emphasize aid. the level of international aid provided by the member states is embarrassing. few people really live up to that. i would also like to say a few words about the other commitments the eu has made, for example in kyoto, and about the state of play of our own climate programme, which the commission released just over a year ago, a two-year programme which constitutes half of the strategy for combating climate change in europe. the other half, on emissions trading, we shall return to in october through moreira da silva. it is a pity that we cannot discuss the emission allowances in this context, but i should like to say something about the report published by the commission a little over a year ago. we are making good progress with what the commission has put year has passed. as regards our objectives and the 8% reduction we want to achieve, the basic assumption must be that climate policy must be the basis for energy policy. this will guide the design of our energy policy in the future. we must ensure that this is reflected in our research programmes and in the directives that the commission has promised, such as the directive on public procurement in the energy sector. transport remains the major problem. there are no good solutions, which could be described as embarrassing. less embarrassing are the local examples. the car-free day across europe on sunday is a testimony to a huge commitment, which we should be making much more of than we have so far. mr president, i would like to thank you. the previous speaker referred to voltaire. let me refer you to a danish author, h. c. andersen, who wrote a fairy tale that many of us have read, the emperor's new clothes. sometimes i get the feeling that the debate we're having is too grand to remind us of that particular tale. everyone sees the emperor is naked, but we dare not or do not want to say it. i am therefore one of those who cannot in any way see the johannesburg meeting as a success. i think it was good that the meeting took place, that a great deal of contacts were made and that some good decisions were taken. given the problems we face on this small planet, such as the continuing rapid spread of poverty, the spread of infectious diseases, the many environmental problems and, not least, the extremely rapid technological development in many areas, some form of framework and regulatory framework is required if it is to work well. what we achieved in johannesburg is not enough. i am afraid that we will meet here again in five or ten years' time and that we will then see that a long list of problems which have been very correctly identified in connection with johannesburg have change. what i had expected from johannesburg was a real commitment from the rich countries to help modernize the production and consumption patterns of the poor countries. it is about energy late. we will certainly increase the resources in this area in a few years' time, but they would be needed now. let me conclude by saying that perhaps we simply have too high expectations of this kind
