other ways, that we will have to identify countries that want to go further, including hopefully the european union. perhaps we should partner with companies that want to go further and faster, with grassroots movements, with researchers, etc., so that we can get better momentum in some of the areas where the situation is particularly critical. we simply do not have a political system at international level that is adequate in the current context, where so many problems of different kinds require common rules. i look forward to the commission's initiatives in these areas. i would therefore ask the commission to involve parliament at an earlier stage in each individual case. as regards the initiatives taken by the commission in the water and energy sectors, we never had the opportunity to comment, which is a bit frustrating given the unquestionable commitment and interest in these areas.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. in paragraph 21 of the report we call for a proposal from the commission during the summer of 2002. as summer has already passed, i would like to change this to 'at the latest before the end of 2002'.
in writing. (sv) the the delegation of the moderate party in the european parliament voted against the report by mrs smet on the representation of women among the social partners in the european union (a5-0279/2002). the principle of equal rights for all is one of the most important cornerstones of our society. rights are and will remain individual. the 'collectivization' of rights can be the first step towards their de-emphasis. the european parliament should have the principle of not making statements about how independent bodies such as employers' organisations and trade unions should be structured. the matter is between them and their members. the moderates also believe that the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities should be abolished and that its object should be taken over by the committee on employment and social affairs.
in writing. (sv) the we voted against the report by mrs hulthén (a5-0237/2002). for us swedish moderates, the most important environmental issue is to avoid development that can cause climate change on our planet. it is obvious to us that the use of coal for energy production should be stopped. environmental policy work needs to focus much more clearly on emissions of carbon dioxide and other greenhouse gases. we voted against this report because we believe that efforts to reduce carbon dioxide emissions and to achieve the objectives of the kyoto protocol are important. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. points 16, 26, 34 and 35 recommend the coordination and harmonisation of environmental and energy taxation. we are opposed to the eu administering the taxes of the member states. the commission will also be taking a decision on environmental taxes in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity. a carbon tax may need to vary between member states, as it may conflict with other measures that member states need to take to achieve lower emissions targets.
in writing. (sv) the we cannot support the report because it recommends coordination in areas which we believe should be dealt with at national level, including the coordination of employment and social policy. we believe that these issues should remain at national level, given that the differences between member states are huge and will increase even more with enlargement. a european policy in these areas cannot take account of the considerable differences between the member states. we also believe that coordination and harmonization in all areas could jeopardize continued cooperation within the eu. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full.
in writing. - (sv) the luxembourg process has initiated a new form of work within the eu. new initiatives have been taken to strengthen employment policy in the union. five years on, we can now see that this strategy has led to progress, although there is still much room for improvement. the open method of coordination is not based on legislative acts but on common policy objectives. this has proved to be an effective method for employment issues. this method does not give parliament the right to co-decide. a co-decision role, as called for in recital r of the report just adopted, would undermine this open method of coordination. as a swedish christian democrat, i cannot support this project. in recital e of the report, the very foundation of entrepreneurship is criticised, when it is stated that to focus on small and medium-sized enterprises is to adopt a too narrow perspective. this is in direct contradiction with the political priorities which the christian democrats have set for themselves in sweden. this is another reason why i did not support this report.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the brok report discusses the progress made in the common foreign and security policy. progress has certainly been made, but much remains to be done, as the rapporteur says. foreign policy is one of the most important areas where we really need strong european cooperation. only by acting consistently and speaking with one voice can the eu play a role on the international stage. it is important for us liberals that foreign policy be based on a clear strategy, characterised by the defence of democracy and human rights, respect for international conventions, free trade, conflict management and cooperation with other nations and organisations. we must be clear about our demands and values. if the famous human rights clause is to have any credibility, it is important to find the tools for how to use it. if we do not activate the human rights clause, despite the appalling human rights abuses in the countries with which we have partnerships, the clause will only be laughed at. for us liberals, transparency in foreign policy is also important. the elected representatives and the public must be able to monitor and control what is happening as much as possible. there are exceptions, of course, but my group is not entirely satisfied with the agreement reached on access to foreign documents. mr president, i would like to thank you. there is a lot of good to be said about brok's report. my group also fully supports the titley report. let me conclude by saying that we do not agree with the american administration on many things, and we have often criticised it in this house. the anti-americanism that sometimes comes out of this debate, however, echoes something hollow, as it is only used as an alibi to cover up our own failures in terms of a clear and common policy on, for example, iraq and the middle east.
the council. - mr president, i should like to thank parliament for the debate. it has been interesting to listen to the many comments and opinions. the overall assessment of the speeches makes it quite clear that we need more progress in the cfsp, we need more common foreign policy. i am glad that the conclusions of the parliament's report and of mr elmar brok's speech are the same. but i would like to say this: given that we are 15 countries for whom foreign policy is a sovereign matter, it is actually quite well done that we actually have a common foreign policy so often. the common foreign policy is in fact the rule and not the exception. this can only be achieved if we listen to each other and reach a consensus, but we all strive to do so. it is true that there are different messages in foreign policy, but when we come to the final decision on common foreign policy, it is normal that we take it together. it's about the middle east, and it's about iraq. there is a consensus despite the often divergent public statements. but it is clear that parliament is right in saying that we have the greatest influence when we stand together. and if you divide the eu, you lose influence too. parliament is right on this point. it should be stressed, however, that the eu has indeed become one of the leading actors in the efforts to bring peace to the middle east. it seems to me that it has been somewhat overlooked by this parliament that the european peace plan is the one currently being worked on. we haven't had one of these before, actually. we have had a frenchman and a german, we have had an american, we have had an arab, but we have not had a european. and the european peace plan also led to the creation of working groups, task forces in july, which have actually been gradually advancing the reform process in the middle east. money that has been returned to palestine after all. workers who, after all, have been allowed to leave palestine and work in israel. i admit that my opening speech was probably too optimistic, and i regret that. but it was also part of my speech that the optimism i had felt had been shattered by the events of the past week. i am sorry that i did not express myself clearly enough in regard to the pessimism which is once again taking over the middle east. then we can choose to be pessimistic and not do anything. it's the easiest thing in the world to be a pessimist. you're always right. we must work with optimism, otherwise we cannot work. and that is why we have created the peace plan that was adopted by the quartet and - as i mentioned the other day - is now also the plan of the security council. has the european parliament experienced this before in the middle east? i don't think so, actually. and this is because of good cooperation with russia, with the un, with the united states, where we are not looking for contradictions but are trying to find the common ground that carries. it is - as this parliament quite rightly says - and i agree with it, and the whole of europe, the whole world agrees with it, two states, two states living peacefully side by side, and one can wonder why so much blood must be shed in the area to reach the result that we all know will end up. it can't end any other way. and that is why we are continuing to make a strong appeal to both sides. we call on the terrorists to stop the terror and on the israeli army to stop the excessive use of force. that's the message we're always giving. this is the same message that kofi annan gave to the un general assembly the other day, when he said that the peace process must be driven by hope and action. i am not saying that the european union should not be involved in the negotiations, but that it should be involved in the negotiations. because if we don't, we're not gonna stop the terror. the european policy, the policy of the council of ministers, is therefore quite clear and corresponds to the policy which is also the global policy. i fully agree with mr cohn-bendit that we must not forget the responsibility of the palestinians to fight terrorism and we are actually putting pressure on both sides, as some have said. i talked to sharon for an hour the other night about how nothing was going to happen by making this siege that's going on. he doesn't achieve his goals that way. that has been said quite clearly. arafat has also been told that he must ensure that no terrorist enters israel. finally, i would say - the main subject was the middle east - that the european position on iraq has also been achieved. the common european position is that we must follow the security council. the european position is that we must support the security council. it also means, and this is the common position we adopted in helsingør, that we must listen to the security council and be willing to meet the wishes of the security council for us. there is no division in the european position as we have adopted it in the circle of foreign ministers. it is about following the security council. and that is also what has given the bonus, because the united states is also agreeing to follow the security council and go to the security council. finally, i would like to thank mr titley for the report on the code of conduct on arms exports. i agree with mr titley's concluding words that much has been done and more needs to be done. as regards transparency, which mr titley was so kind as to expect from the danish presidency, i would like to say that we are very happy to meet that request. the fourth annual report under the danish presidency will increase transparency by providing more statistical data and adding a compendium of adopted practices to show how the code is being implemented by member states. the report will also include new agreements on transit, end-user certificates and the production of military articles under licence in third countries. it is the duty of the member states to regulate the arms trade. several member states have introduced legislation, others are preparing such legislation. mr titley's report and the support it has received here teach me that parliament's support for the efforts of the danish presidency is very welcome and very valuable. thank you, sir.
council mr president, i too would like to thank you for the discussion and the remarks made, particularly in the area of the common security and defence policy, which have led to many direct and indirect conclusions on this subject. i would like to limit myself to two points: firstly, to the point raised by the honourable member, mr von wogau, who pointed out an interesting gap in the area of european defence and security policy, which has to do with the absence of a single market. we do indeed agree that this is an area where cooperation, whether in the field of production or in the field of research and development, and more generally overall cooperation between countries, can produce substantial results. it is a gap, as mr brok's report points out, in which we found all the points of interest in the area of security and defence and with which, as i said, we agree. we believe that europe, which has made such great efforts, and indeed with results in the field of r & d in the civilian sector of the economy, can make and extend this effort in the field of defence if it wants to gain greater autonomy, self-sufficiency and capacity for growth, and to derive economic benefits from it. the second point i would like to make is the question raised by the honourable member, mr lagendijk. as the presidency, i have informed you of the efforts being made to resolve the problem. i would like to point out that the solution to be sought will be the creation of a european public service commission which will be open to all third parties wishing to share this new experience of the european union. but it should also safeguard certain principles: the principle of the autonomy of the union's decision-making system and the integrity of its institutions. these are principles which parliament itself has called for and which are also contained in the treaties, and we are therefore seeking to achieve them. i am convinced that with the systematic effort that is being made and the pragmatic spirit that can be shown by all sides we will be able to arrive, and i hope soon, at a result.

distinguished and ever noble presence of my honourable friend and publisher, ever noble despite the constitutional machinations of new labour, i commend, as ever, the middle way. but if we are to take the middle way of european multilateralism providing more weight on our end of the transatlantic rope, being able to act as a counterpart and occasionally as a counterweight, then we have as european politicians to face up to the political and financial consequences of that. i hope that we will increasingly be prepared to do so. there is a second point i should make in response to the honourable member, mr barón crespo. he asked for a report on destroyed eu financial projects. he will recall that we tried to provide the figures for commission projects a few months ago. at present, we are in writing. - once again, we are witnessing an aggression by the israeli army in palestine. in europe we are stepping up the fight against terrorism, but we are silent in the face of state terrorism which kills innocent people every day who are resisting colonization and occupation! explain to me why we refuse to sanction israel, like freezing the association agreements? why are we silent when sharon is violating human rights, while we condemn those who, elsewhere, flout democratic freedoms? why is there silence when settlers occupy a state and their government ignores un resolutions? why are international conventions binding on all but israel, which carries out illegal arrests and tortures? why are we sending in anti-ethnic cleansing forces for some people and nothing for the protection of the palestinian people? we speak of justice for some and impunity for others! what are you waiting for to act before it's too late? an extermination, so that no one can claim a just peace, the right to have a country and live in it with dignity? after this new genocide, you will say: never again! stop this slaughter, you have the power!
- the agenda calls for the statements by the council and the commission on the international criminal court following the meeting of the union's foreign ministers in helsingør on 29 and 30 august 2002.
madam president, i would like to thank you. it is crucial that the eu firmly supports the international criminal court. first, article 98 of the rome statute of the international criminal court cannot be used as the united states proposes. it applies only to nato or similar organizations' status of force agreements to protect soldiers from sending countries, but it cannot be applied generally to u.s. troops or u.s. citizens. secondly, any country that makes a treaty with the united states is violating not only the rome statute but also international law. the united states, in its agreements with romania, etc., is proposing not only that american citizens should not be brought to trial, but also that covered agents should not be brought to trial, which would be devastating for international law. thirdly, the agreements leave it open to the united states government to decide whether to investigate or prosecute where appropriate. this is not only contrary to the rome statute but also to the general duty to prosecute international crimes. fourthly, it is not acceptable to undermine the role of the international criminal court by protecting citizens of the sole superpower from being held accountable for serious war crimes, which would create the belief that only the law of the victor applies.
the council. - the answer is yes.

mr president, i would like to thank you. in developing better vehicles from an environmental point of view, we are facing major and radical changes. new engines will reduce fuel consumption and improve exhaust gas cleaning in the coming years. modern sensor technology, such as fuel sensors, makes engines much better from an environmental point of view, but at the same time engines are also becoming more sensitive to pollution. these environmental improvements will not be possible unless they go hand in hand with improved fuel quality. cleaner fuels allow us to take advantage of all the environmental benefits of new motor technology. poor fuel technology, on the other hand, can be directly detrimental to the new generation of engines and can knock out the technology and engine function. this directive is very urgent and represents an important step towards less air pollution and a better environment. before parliament's first reading of the draft directive, i drew up 14 amendments to tighten up the requirements for fuel quality. the majority of the amendments were adopted, and i am very pleased that in the common position the council accepts most of parliament's first reading proposals. a large part of the debate at second reading has focused on the question of whether all vehicles should be covered by this directive. there is engine technology for mobile machinery and tractors, so that they, like other vehicles, can be covered by the fuel directive. no exemption is required for so-called sulphur-free diesel. it is a misconception that old engines need sulphur in their fuel, but that new engines cannot handle sulphur fuel. if these vehicles are to be covered by the directive in full, the environment will be the big winner. why not take that step too? a possible tax reduction could offset the higher costs of cleaner fuels, for example for farmers. mr president, i would like to thank you. the eu's auto-oil programme, together with this fuel directive, will go a long way towards reducing the negative environmental impact of vehicle traffic. this shows that the eu has the power and the competence to tackle important environmental issues and that the use of modern environmental technologies is better than prohibition. the rapporteur, mr hautala, has brought this matter to a clear improvement, and i hope that parliament will adopt the report from the committee on the environment.
mr president, i would like to thank you. let me start where the previous speaker left off. sweden and finland have considerably more lakes than all the other eu member states combined. in our countries, lakes are often sources of drinking water or very sensitive natural areas, such as bird nesting areas. in sensitive lakes, we are now meeting environmental requirements with restrictions on boating, such as boat bans in certain areas during bird nesting seasons. in sweden and finland, it is also common to use coastal boats to go up to the lakes. it is good that environmental standards are set high also for recreational boat engines, as is the case with this directive. however, we swedes, and my colleagues from finland, are concerned that this proposal for a directive opens up the possibility of higher environmental standards for recreational boats in inland waterways than in coastal waters. in our countries, this would be an impossible eu legislation, created by those who do not want to listen and absorb the message of the real conditions in the northern member states with their thousands of lakes. with a specific environmental standard for inland waterway vessels we are missing the very basis of the proposal for a directive, the common market. in addition, there is currently no possibility of achieving a higher environmental standard for recreational boating on lakes. the result will be that people will stop buying modern, environmentally better boat engines. this proposal may look good on paper, but in reality the result will be bad, and it will be bad for the environment. i am happy to raise the environmental standards for recreational boat engines in the future, but then realistic requirements must be set. in addition, the transitional periods must be adapted to the time needed for the development of new engine technologies. no such amendments have been tabled, so the only reasonable thing is that the commission may come back with new environmental requirements in a later revision of this directive. boats are often anchored, but environmental policy must also be anchored. it must be grounded in reality. if this is not the case, environmental policy will be in trouble, in the middle of a dead end, and the environment will suffer.
in writing. - (sv) the swedish christian democrats voted today in favour of the report to tighten the limits on gas and noise emissions imposed by the union on pleasure boats. however, we reject total harmonization of the rules because the national parameters are very different in each member state. the levels set must be minimum provisions and leave individual member states free to define stricter rules for the protection of the environment. we also reject the harmonised requirements for emissions from lake-going vessels because many pleasure boats, particularly in sweden and finland, are used for both sea and lake-going pleasure. one possible consequence of this distinction is that older, more polluting engines are not replaced by new engines, which do not meet the more stringent requirements. in countries where drinking water comes from lakes, emissions from boats are an even more acute problem. in these cases too, minimum rules would leave member states free to tighten their own emission requirements. they would also have the possibility of prohibiting or restricting the movement of small pleasure boats on certain lakes. finally, we do not think it would be appropriate to introduce new, stricter provisions on greenhouse gas emissions just three years after the directive has entered into force. minimum rules are a better way to get things done. it is an instrument which gives manufacturers the opportunity to adapt their production on their own and to upgrade their technologies to the highest standards applied in the union so that they can sell in all member states.
in writing. (sv) the in general, we think that mr brok's report is a good one. we do not agree with certain points of it, however. the commission shall adopt implementing acts laying down: for reasons of efficiency and to increase the likelihood of the eu raising a united voice, we, like the report, are in favour of merging the functions of high representative and commissioner for external relations. on the other hand, we are not ready at this stage to decide which institution will have this new merged competence. in any case, the common foreign and security policy is an intergovernmental matter. paragraph 14: we are in favour of the rule allowing member states to act in the context of enhanced cooperation, but it is not possible to apply this rule to security and defence policy. paragraph 20: the report should not encourage member states to increase their defence budgets. the following paragraph is inserted: we do not agree that article 28 of the treaty should be changed so that the expenditure of military operations under the common security and defence policy can be financed from the community budget. the following paragraph shall be added: we are not opposed to closer institutional relations between nato and the eu as a prerequisite for the eu to be able to carry out military operations in the context of crisis management, but we would draw attention to the fact that all military interventions must be accompanied by a relevant un mandate.
in writing. - (sv) in the report adopted today on the common foreign and security policy, parliament expresses its views on several points on the future direction it wishes this policy area to take in the future. i was forced to vote against the report because of several of these points of view. the current union has several neutral countries, including sweden and ireland. in adopting amendment no 2, which calls for the introduction in the future european constitution of the duty of mutual assistance laid down in the treaty on western european union, parliament openly declared that it did not respect the position of these countries and wanted to force them to renounce their neutrality. this is contrary to the accession agreements these countries have signed with the union. in recital i, the majority of the parliament also considers that foreign and security policy must become community policy, which, according to some previous positions, should be interpreted as a desire to see the community method applied and to give the parliament the right of codecision. i consider that such a development is neither realistic nor desirable. point 19 also calls for the creation of a european defence agency, a request which does not seem to me to be sufficiently substantiated.
in writing. - (sv) the report is dominated by developments after 11 september and the impact of this event on the common foreign and security policy. i welcome, of course, the statement by the eu foreign ministers against military intervention in iraq and i join them in saying that everything must be done to ensure that the un inspectors can return to iraq without conditions. i believe that international law must be respected and that any military intervention must be based on a decision taken by the united nations security council. the report contains a number of proposals which i cannot approve. this is point 11, which proposes to merge the tasks of the high representative of the council for foreign affairs and security policy and those of the commissioner responsible for these matters, and to create this new post within the commission. nor can i support the proposal in paragraph 23, which concerns the financing by the community budget of the common costs of military interventions in the context of esdp, nor the idea in paragraph 19 of creating a european defence agency. i also do not agree with paragraph 20, which deals with the strengthening of the defence budgets of the member states, or with the proposal in paragraph 14, which aims to introduce qualified majority decisions at council level and which lays down the rule of enhanced cooperation so that member states willing and able to carry out operations can act without a joint decision. i am also opposed to the proposal in paragraph 26 on strengthening institutional relations between nato and the eu and in paragraph 12 on the european diplomatic corps.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the fault of the us war threats against iraq is not in itself a desire to replace an undemocratic regime. the regime of saddam hussein is indeed repulsive, as i have observed myself on several visits, and it has only got worse. it is true that the democratic opposition should be supported and that economic boycotts can be tried, as we did against apartheid south africa, provided that this does not affect the victims more than the criminals, as is the case in iraq. sometimes one can even support an armed national liberation struggle against a dictator, as many of us did in the case of the european colonial powers in africa. but if we do not want to create anarchy, then all future interference in the affairs of a sovereign state must be based on an equal, global world order, which applies equally to all. therefore, the un security council must decide on any armed action. furthermore, military force may only be used if all the possibilities of non-violence have been exhausted. the weapons inspectors must therefore be returned to iraq without delay and the us threats of military action without a decision of the un security council must be condemned. otherwise, it is only legitimising the lawless wild west violence elsewhere in the world, such as israel's massacres of palestinians or perhaps russian intervention in georgia. mr president, i would like to thank you. if the eu is to live up to its claim to a legal culture as opposed to the american lynch-mob justice, it must now, with increased vigour, especially in the case of iraq, oppose the us global hegemony and uphold a global legal order within the framework of the un.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to address in particular the danish presidency, the minister for european affairs, mr bertil haarder, and the european commission's foreign affairs spokesman, mr chris patten. these are the two people i have the greatest confidence in. in my view, there are three main questions that we as europeans and democrats must answer. the first question is how we are to prevent weapons of mass destruction from being manufactured, disseminated, sold to international terrorists and used in europe, the middle east and throughout the world. how can we prevent this? the second question concerns the un security council. here in the european parliament many strong forces are working to limit the veto as far as possible. in view of the prospect of an enlarged eu with 25 member states, a large majority in this parliament says that we cannot take decisions unless the right of veto is abolished. at the same time, we are in a situation where a communist government in beijing, china, which has little record on human rights, actually has absolute veto power over the most important bodies of the international community, the un and its security council, and how they should act to prevent violence and to maintain world peace. the same right is granted in moscow, paris, london and washington. the third question is how we can bring about change in the middle east. the middle east can be described as a pressure cooker, where water bubbles and boils. the lid is still on the pot, but the moment when everything can be over is imminent. many in this parliament are of the opinion that military action against iraq will lead to the whole of the middle east being thrown into a panic and into a single catastrophe. however, is a conservative policy of letting things go on as they have been un security inspectorates are humiliated and humiliated and unable to do their job, and saddam hussein, the dictator and mass murderer who gassed five thousand kurds to death in halabja, remains in power in baghdad a path leading to democracy and human rights in iraq and the middle east? is it not time that we in europe had a responsibility to initiate a process of change for democracy, human rights and against the proliferation of terrorist weapons from the region in question? how would you answer these questions?
mr president, i would like to thank you. we are on the verge of war, a war that could have devastating consequences, not only for the people of iraq and the entire middle east, but also for the world at large. in that situation, common sense must prevail over simplistic expressions of emotion. after the devastation of world war ii, the nations of the world created the united nations as the instrument for world peace, and as patten says, there is no alternative to the un in maintaining global security. in international law, nations have agreed on the rules of military intervention to save world peace. it is clear from this that decisions on such intervention should be taken by the un security council. the choice made by the united states and salafranca sánchez-neyra to comply with un resolutions at the appropriate time has devastating consequences for the entire international legal system. this means ignoring and deliberately opposing the un. is this the justice system the conservatives stand for? the strength of justice and international lawlessness? or should all countries have the same right to intervene for so-called preventive purposes? what happens then? it is clear that iraq must comply with the un resolutions and allow the inspectors to carry out their work unconditionally. an agreement has also been reached between hans blix and iraq to allow the inspectors to resume the work which was actually interrupted when the us and uk began the bombing last time. the inspectors should be able to start their work immediately. there is no excuse for dictator saddam hussein's violation of un resolutions. iraq, like all other countries, must abide by the decisions taken by the un. this is of course also true of israel. if the motive is that iraq has not complied with un resolutions and therefore must be bombed, what then are we to do with israel which is constantly violating un resolutions? the eu must make it clear to the world's largest democracy, the united states, that international law must be respected by all countries and that the right to privacy is not accepted, even against dictators.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the council and the commission for the statement on the employment strategy, and i would also like to thank the two rapporteurs. i shall concentrate on the employment strategy. it's good to have an evaluation after five years. it is important to see what has worked well, what has not worked well and what needs to be improved. it is true, as the council says, that in the end it is the result that counts. the overall result of the past five years is, however, quite good. active labour market policies have increased, unemployment has fallen and employment has consequently increased. if we compare how we have handled employment during different stages of recession, we can probably conclude that this recession is much better than the last recession, even though unemployment is currently rising in some countries. as regards the proposed changes themselves and their scope, it is true that the pillar structure is not an end in itself, but it is important that we include various aspects of the employment strategy. this is of course the case with the objectives of increasing employment and reducing unemployment, increasing employment for women, quality of work and matters relating to work organisation. there is another aspect which has not been taken up by anyone, namely that sustainable development should also be integrated into the employment strategy as in the other two strategies. i share your view of stability. you don't go in and change every year. if nothing special happens, these processes will be allowed to continue for a few years. i share your view on focus. it's going to be difficult if we have too many indicators and targets. we need to focus on the most important objectives, and we need to look at implementation and outcome. as regards transparency and the involvement of the parties, i would like to emphasize what was said about the social partners. they already play a central role and there are grounds for increasing their involvement in the employment strategy. however, there are shortcomings, particularly in the area of democratic control. there is a lack of european parliament influence on the overall strategy and a lack of influence from national parliaments on the implementation and the national action plans in the member states. these are important questions. we cannot allow such important matters to be decided in closed rooms by civil servants' organisations. we must have proper democratic control. however, when we coordinate processes, no process is more important than any other. the economic process is as important as the social and employment policy process, and we must also include the social guidelines when we synchronise the processes. all of this is important! if anyone is to coordinate, it is the council (general affairs).
mr president, i would like to thank you. let me say a few words about the employment strategy. firstly, i am pleased that both the commission and the council are so clearly attaching to and defending the lisbon strategy with its clear objectives, with talk of economic growth, full employment and social considerations balancing each other. it is very important to have a balance in employment policy, where there are different interests side by side. secondly, it is good that the commission is so clear about the implementation perspective in terms of the employment strategy. we have had the problem that there have been many good ideas, but that there has been a lack of implementation in the member states. it is important that the european employment strategy is linked to national employment policies. in this context, parliaments are a key factor, as they allocate the budget for the national employment strategies. i therefore think it was good that hjort frederiksen, like the commission, emphasised the role of parliament. i would like to clarify, however. it is good that the role of parliament should be extended. parliament must be allowed to debate, must it be allowed to decide? parliament is the body that decides on the national employment strategy. is there any other european employment strategy that is different and that the parliaments should not be allowed to decide on? mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur for the very extensive work he has done, but above all for his ability to work with other groups and to try to find broad solutions. we really appreciate it. i think that the report before us now contains a whole series of improvements compared with the commission's proposal. however, this is a rather complicated subject. in the case of emissions trading, it is largely a question of taking measures that are as effective as possible. at the same time, there is a risk that this will be seen as a substitute for policies to reduce emissions, and this must not be the case. sometimes there is a superstition about trading in allowances in relation to the necessary measures that also have to be taken in other areas. the global emissions trading system under the kyoto protocol is a system with many risks. this could lead to the purchase of emission rights that do not exist in the future. we must discuss how this can be avoided in future. as regards the proposal itself, we want the system to be binding and the exceptions to be as limited as possible. we want to cover as many sectors as possible and naturally support the proposals for extension to more sectors. we think it is reasonable to include all six greenhouse gases and we think that part of the allowances should be auctioned off as soon as practicable. this is in full accordance with the principle that the polluter should reasonably pay for the pollution. we also support the idea of a national ceiling on trade and want effective sanctions. we shall therefore vote on the various parts of the proposal accordingly.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i also want to start by joining moreira da silva's fan club and thank you for a great job. i would also like to thank you for an excellent round table discussion last night, which i thought was conducted in a sane and balanced way and also added a great deal to the discussion. i would also like to thank the commission for the document, which is a industry, for which this system is being created, is also able to compete on equal terms. we also have international commitments, such as the kyoto protocol, to live up to and follow, and therefore we cannot change the rules of the game. for the system to work, i believe the following six points must be met: this system must be simple, it must be administratively simple. we cannot create a complicated system that the participants, particularly the companies, do not understand. the system must of course be compulsory. we can't create a chess game where some players say, "no, i don't want to be in right now, so i'm going to go outside and stand next to you instead". we need to know who is going to be in the system, and of course it has to concern countries and individual plants. i also think that it would have been desirable to allocate the allowances by auction. there are many views on how the allocation should be made. the compromise now before the vote is based on up to 95% voluntary participation and 5% auctioning in the first period, and i think that is a good solution. at least we have an element of auction, which is more in line with the polluter pays principle. it the next year. we also need to recall certain aspects and stress that this must be in line with the kyoto protocol. we can't come up with anything new. i would like to ask the council in particular to ensure that it takes a decision on 17 october.
mr president, i would like to thank you. this second reading is extremely important, since it is the twenty-third amendment to the directive restricting the use and placing on the market of substances classified as carcinogenic, mutagenic and toxic to reproduction, so-called cmr substances. the council, and the commission, have not been willing to listen to the european parliament in our request that the directive should also cover products. after all, products are the main source of exposure for the general public, such as toys, textiles or floor coverings. the risk assessment carried out in accordance with the commission's proposal is in fact based on the precautionary principle and clearly states that safety must be guaranteed for the public. this can only be achieved by also banning cmr substances in consumer goods. for some unfounded reason the commission and the council have not wanted to deal with this, despite the scientific evidence. the council also points out that this concerns a large number of products and that this would be a reason for not doing anything. actually, it should be the other way around. if a large number of products contain cmrs, we should of course act even more quickly. i would have liked to have seen a ban proposal as early as 2002, but we have not been able to get this far. it's always a pleasure to work with the gentleman dr. nisticò. we have been able to achieve a constructive amendment, a compromise between dr nisticò, my colleagues from the socialist group and the greens. i would urge my colleagues to vote in favour of amendment no 2. we can't wait any longer. it is not responsible to do so, nor is it in accordance with the precautionary principle. i appeal to my colleagues: i would like to vote in favour of amendment no 2!
in writing. (sv) we would like to emphasise that we currently consider cooperation through eurojust to be the preferred form of cooperation for prosecution within the eu. the case law on a possible common european authority for the prosecution of economic crimes must be limited to cases of fraud against the european institutions.
the commission is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter, and that it is not yet in a position to take a decision on the matter. as it points out, hacking, viruses and denial of service practices can have very serious consequences, whether as a result of individual self-interest or in the context of organised crime or terrorism. the commission proposal aims to approximate the criminal offences of unauthorised access or interference with an information system and was preceded by consultations with professional sectors, law enforcement authorities and expert discussions in the european union forum on cybercrime. the commission intends to establish a degree of approximation of legislation at european level, but in accordance with and consistent with the council of europe convention on cybercrime of november 2001. article 3 of our proposal makes unauthorized access to an information system an offence. it's about punishing computer hacking. such unauthorised access should be criminally punishable when it is carried out against an information system subject to specific protection measures, or with the intention of causing harm to a natural or legal person, or with the intention of obtaining an information technology benefit. we have also criminalised the unlawful interference with information systems, thus addressing the problem of virus and denial of service attacks and the deterioration of websites on the internet. the commission proposal requires that all offences be committed intentionally and without authorisation and focuses mainly on serious cases, the criteria for which must be decided by the court judge of each member state. the proposal contains aggravating circumstances and also requires member states to designate operational contact points, available 24 hours a day, seven days a week, to facilitate the exchange of information on attacks against information systems. the commission considers that the rapporteur is quite right in stressing that the acts which must be punished are not to be regarded as insignificant or of a minor nature. the 12th recital in the preamble to the commission proposal therefore states that excessive criminalization should be avoided, particularly for minor or insignificant offences. the commission therefore agrees with the amendment proposed by the rapporteur on this point. as for the question of data protection under the third pillar, we are working on it, but i would ask parliament not to suspend the application of this proposal for a framework decision until there is a specific data protection instrument under the third pillar. on the contrary, i think that a specific reference should be made to the respect of the principles laid down in the council of europe convention on data protection, which must be fully respected in this area of application of the framework decision. this was the solution adopted in the case of the sexual exploitation of children and child pornography. i would like to thank the rapporteur once again for her contribution to this important debate, which i believe is expected by public opinion in the member states.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner vitorino, we have been following each other for many years in the fight against cybercrime and we probably share many common core values. mr vitorino knows as well as i do that this is a framework directive. since the european parliament does not have the powers of decision it should have in the case of framework directives, but rather weaker ones, i see this as an opportunity to highlight this, as i have also done on the point raised by mr vitorino in connection with the comment on the amendment. i understand his point of view very well, but it is from this house that we must press for the convention and for progress. these are very worrying threats to our common union. there are reports of criminal organizations gaining access to vital infrastructure. they've got access to digital power switches, which means electricity, water, and transportation can be shut down. in particular, there have been incidents in the airspace affecting the functioning of the air traffic control tower. terrorists could shut off both gas and water, which would increase the damage associated with a physical attack. this is of course a situation which we must avoid at all costs. it's about cross-border crime. the commission's proposal is a good one. i admire vitorino for his ability to put forward good proposals, despite the deficient framework within which he has to work. the idea of these minimum-maximum penalties that we have traditionally used for harmonisation is reasonable and sensible. it could also lead to the disappearance of the possibility of criminals being convicted in countries with lower sentences, a kind of 'legal shopping' which we cannot of course rush through. it's also about intent. it is also clear and distinct, which i think is very good. there must be intent. the aim must be harm or gain. i think that's the right attitude. i welcome the commission's spirit, but i would like to reiterate that the decision is unacceptable. the court of justice has no jurisdiction under the third pillar. the system we have today makes democratic rights exceptions. human rights are beyond the control of the courts. that is why i am putting extra emphasis on this in an amendment, as i am sure mr vitorino will also understand. these crimes can have long-term consequences and have a direct impact on people's privacy. we must have democratic control, and that is why i would like this legislation to come into force from the day when we have proper protection in this area and also data protection in the third pillar. i would like to have that, of course, as of tomorrow. i fully understand that the operational contact points must be set up. i do not want to slow it down in any way, but i want to push the democratic process forward. this is as far as we can go with today's decision-making system. i hope, however, that a new constitution will bring order to these matters and that we can really have a legitimate system. the amendments have already been positively commented on by commissioner vitorino. this also applies to the nuanced approach to legal persons. i am also glad that we have a common position on the issue of minors. i would like to conclude by thanking all my colleagues. i have tried to find a balanced form with as effective a law enforcement as possible, while within our current framework i have tried to push for human rights and privacy protection. i want the convention to feel the pressure. that's why i wrote it this way. i am sure mr vitorino will understand that and i hope he will help to apply this pressure.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the new effective drugs have brought about a great revolution in health care. they have been a great help to many patients. for example, modern drugs for heart failure have not only increased survival but also radically improved the quality of life of the sick. twenty years ago, when i was a newly qualified medical practitioner, the information that i had access to as a doctor about medicines would be locked away so that unauthorized people couldn't get access to it. a lot's happened since then. today, anyone with access to the internet and sufficient language skills can access treatment principles and drug information. this development is good for health care. it is also a requirement of the patient organisations. the development of the medical profession is certainly demanding, but the best relationship is between the doctor and the patient, who has a good knowledge base. mr president, attempting to prevent, or limit information to patients is very much at odds with the principles of transparency in modern health care. finally, a comment to didier rod and the verts/ale group: your proposal for a double drug trial is not only bad, it could even be dangerous. the review of new medicinal products is not to take place every five years, but must be continuous.
commission. – mr president, firstly i would like to thank the rapporteur, ms evans, and the committee on the environment, for the report on the council's common position. i am satisfied that the report reflects a general agreement on the safety of the technical provisions relating to the management of the rabies risk, even if a few points of disagreement remain and may need some further clarification. the main differences between the original proposal submitted to parliament at first reading and the common position we have before us today fall into three categories. first, we have the provision facilitating movements of young animals between member states of equivalent status. second, the provisions for reassessment within a period of five years of the specific conditions for movements to ireland, the united kingdom and sweden. third, provisions amending the conditions applying to trade to align them with the conditions applying to non-commercial movements – to accommodate the wish expressed by parliament at first reading. allow me now to address in detail the amendments proposed in the report. they can be split into two categories – technical amendments and institutional amendments. i will begin with the nine technical amendments. amendments nos 1 and 6 may be the result of a misunderstanding. the setting of a maximum number of animals aims only to distinguish commercial movements from other non-commercial movements. it is not intended to be a dividing line between two different types of rules – the same rules would apply to commercial and non-commercial movements. so it would be incorrect to say that setting a maximum of, say, five would be any threat to public health. in effect, it means that once you go above five, the commercial rules apply but below five the rules set out in this draft legislation apply. amendments nos 3 and 4 would result in a prohibition of movements of young animals under the age of vaccination between member states other than the united kingdom, ireland and sweden, for which specific provisions exist. the conclusion of the discussion with the council was that those movements of young animals have always been allowed, that they represent a negligible risk and that this flexibility must be maintained. otherwise, the movement of these animals would be seriously undermined. therefore, amendments nos 1, 3, 4 and 6 cannot be accepted. amendments nos 7, 8 and 9 suggest the adoption of import conditions equivalent to those that apply to movements inside the community for third countries free of rabies as defined in the oie code. as already stated at first reading, this is not in line with the risk analysis approach that we need to adopt, so these amendments cannot be accepted. in fact, this would not be consistent with the conditions applicable to movements inside the union, as some member states are not 'rabies free' under the oie code definition, even if rabies in domestic animals is under control on their territory, as ms evans mentioned. we therefore need to take a similar approach to third countries with similar circumstances. finally, on the technical amendments, the commission can accept amendments nos 2 and 13. almost all member states consider microchipping to be a reliable method of identification, and i look favourably on automatic harmonisation after a transitional period of eight years. let me now turn to the seven institutional amendments. in a spirit of compromise, the commission could consider favourably amendment no 5, which aims to introduce a codecision procedure for the extension of specific provisions applicable to the uk, ireland and sweden. the commission could also have accepted amendment no 16 as an alternative to amendment no 12, and i regret therefore that amendment no 16 has been withdrawn. amendment no 12 unreasonably deprives the commission of any possibility to propose temporary measures, whereas amendment no 16 would have allowed this in a reasonable way. i cannot therefore accept amendment no 12. nor can i accept the other four amendments. amendments nos 10 and 11 would limit the executive competence of the commission to 'technical' aspects. this wording would very likely give rise to legal arguments on the interpretation of the word 'technical', which is not defined in the legislation. this would jeopardise the sound management and efficient implementation of the regulation. amendments nos 14 and 15 cannot be accepted because they go beyond the institutional agreement. i would also like to draw your attention to the fact that they are not consistent with the dual legal base of the regulation. i hope i have adequately explained the reasoning behind the positions i have taken on the amendments.
mr president, i would like to thank you. animal health conditions for the transport of pet animals are particularly important for member states which are currently completely free of rabies. i believe, however, that it is important to facilitate the movement and travel across eu borders for families and individuals who have a pet as a travel companion. i am glad that the council has taken into account in its proposal the views expressed during the first reading of the matter by parliament. it is incomprehensible to me that today there are different animal health conditions for the transport of pet animals for commercial purposes within the eu. the risk of rabies cannot be substantially different simply because the purpose of the pet's journey is different. it is therefore necessary to adapt the health rules for the transport of pet animals for commercial purposes to this directive. finally, mr president, i would like to thank jillian evans for her very good cooperation in this matter.
