(sv) the the delegation of the moderate party in the european parliament voted today in favour of the council directive on the establishment of minimum requirements for the qualification of third-country nationals and stateless persons as refugees or persons seeking international protection for other reasons and on the legal status of such persons. however, we voted against the amended proposal of parliament. we believe in the clear right of refugees to seek refuge from persecution and other forms of oppression by entering the eu. the establishment of minimum requirements is an important first step towards the establishment of a common asylum policy in the eu. however, it is important to stress that, beyond the minimum requirements proposed by the eu, member states should be able to provide protection for individuals for other reasons. transparency must be at the core of the creation of a common position on migration within the eu. the creation of a migration policy, in other words the establishment of rules on the possibility of moving within a country, is always a balancing act. the open borders can very easily lead us to the point of closing the door to dynamism and improvements. immigration is an asset. third country nationals who are not refugees must have more opportunities to migrate to europe as migrant workers.
in writing. - (sv) we abstained on the report by mrs fraisse on the importance and dynamism of theatre and the performing arts in enlarged europe. our basic position towards europe is positive. as swedish liberals, we see european integration as an opportunity to find solutions to crossborder problems in the areas of environment, trade, crossborder mobility, human rights and conflict management. here is a chance for european democracies to show the world that cooperation brings peace and increases prosperity. we also believe in the principle of subsidiarity, which means that decisions should be taken as closely as possible to the people concerned. that is why we actively support the cause of a constitution for the european union which clearly indicates to all the distribution of responsibilities. it must be very clear to all citizens that the union will deal only with the matters for which it has the greatest competence, namely crossborder cases. all other issues should be addressed at local, regional or national level. the theatre and the performing arts are important and we are in favour of increasing the mobility of people. we want to make it easier for foreign workers to obtain visas and residence permits, but we believe that all occupational groups should be able to cross borders under the same conditions. the proposal should therefore not be limited to actors and performers but should concern all professional groups. we also support cultural diversity, support for artistic creation, artistic freedom and access to culture for all citizens, but we believe that these should not be included in the future european constitution. at national level, we strongly support several of the ideas presented in the fraisse report.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. let me begin by thanking my colleagues, especially those in the committee on budgets and the secretariat of the committee on budgets. they have done a fantastic job with this budget and i think this is a good opportunity to highlight the extremely hard work being done by officials in this context. i would also like to thank the commission and the council for the long series of trialogue meetings, conciliations, working groups and other contacts which have helped us to have a good draft budget for 2003 to which we can give our opinion. i would also like to emphasize that we have reached a broad consensus on the vast majority of points in the committee on budgets, which is of course also a great strength for parliament. mr president, i would like to thank you. let me begin by giving some basic thoughts on the eu budgetary system. i must say that after working on this budget for about ten months i have come to the firm opinion that the eu budgetary system is in dire need of a major reform. we will have some improvements in the form of the new financial regulation. there will be some improvements in the form of the abb system, the business-based budgeting system. we have been able to remedy some of these ourselves by improving parliament's own internal workings. i've been working very hard to get a better dialogue with the committees. the dialogue between the committee on budgets and the committees and their presentation of the budget now functions as a virtually permanent process, from the guidelines in the spring, to the new work carried out this year with special working groups to follow up the current year's budget, and to the amendments for the first reading. i therefore believe that this year we have reduced the traditional conflict in which the committees on budgetary matters do not consider that the committee on budgets takes sufficient account of their political priorities, and in which the committee on budgets has in turn often felt that the committees on budgetary matters are fighting for unrealistic budgetary demands. this year we have made more progress than in the past, not least in the traditionally difficult section of the a30 appropriations for various european organizations. this year, through close cooperation with the committees and the political groups, we were able to present a unanimous proposal from the committee on budgets. we have also, as you know, reformed the budgetary debate and have already carried out the major budgetary debate at the september part-session. it was an experiment that i think went well, but it can certainly be improved upon. mr president, i would like to thank you. despite these improvements, i am still not satisfied. i would like to send a message to our colleagues in the convention. i think it is unreasonable to make permanent a system of longterm budgetary planning which has such rigid principles for sectoral budgetary ceilings as we have today. they are now giving rise, after only half the budgetary planning period, to an increasing number of ad hoc systems which risk making the budgetary methodology totally unmanageable. we have already had proposals from the commission during this year's budgetary process on the use of the flexibility instrument in three cases. in the july agreement we agreed on a more planned use of what is really an emergency reserve. we have solved the commission's problem of having sufficient staff for enlargement by means of a complex model of frontloading, which means that we take the right to unused resources for the current year and use them for costs that would otherwise have fallen in 2003. today, in addition, we have agreed with the council on another new flexibility instrument, the so-called european solidarity fund, to deal with the effects of natural disasters and so on. now, however, we must ask ourselves: how many such flexibility instruments and how many ad hoc solutions will we need before we realise on a broad front that the whole budgetary methodology needs to be reformed and made more flexible? the committee on budgets has also taken a stand. we have deliberately chosen to exceed the ceilings in three budget categories, partly to show the absurdity of the present closed system. i shall return to this in the explanatory statement. i would like to make it clear that i am not seeking to abolish the budgetary ceiling. i am not arguing that we should relax the requirements of budgetary restraint. but there has to be some middle ground between total laxity and what we live with today, a seven-year locked rigid planned economy! i hope that it will also become a matter of course that the codecision model will apply to the entire budget, including the agricultural policy, and that all eu expenditure will be included in the budget, including the european development fund. this is an important area which i hope we can continue to discuss in the coming years. let me mention one more general problem before i go into the different budget categories, namely the implementation of the eu budget. this is becoming a serious problem. we cannot go back to the citizens, to the taxpayers, year after year, and tell them that we are taking 15 billion euros out of them unnecessarily every year, which we are then paying back to their grateful finance ministers. i believe that a great deal of effort is needed in this area if the union is to have credibility in its policies and its budget and if we are to be able to live up to all the promises we make in the budget. we simply have to simplify the management of the structural funds, which we can start doing already in the current period. we have the word of commissioner barnier on this, but we can of course do it even more radically for the next programme period. i also believe that we need to improve the whole process when we start new multiannual programmes. we are told time and again that for multiannual programmes entering their third or fourth programming period - we are now in the sixth framework programme for research and development, the third life programme, etc. - it often takes a full year to get started when a new period begins. this is completely unreasonable! this sometimes means that five years of planned activity can be compressed into four years of implementation. a third area where i believe we must achieve improved implementation and simplification is in the area of human resources. this year we decided not to use flexibility for new posts in the commission. that does not mean that these services are not needed. in the long term, i believe that more services may be required, for example to manage the new, more decentralised organisation of the foreign service, but this of course also requires that the commission fully demonstrates that it is working effectively, that the commission reform is being implemented, that we are getting results for more efficient management, and that the second stage of the so-called relaxes reform is actually being implemented. without these efforts, it is hopeless to argue for more services for the commission, even though we are convinced that in the long term they are really needed. let me now briefly touch on some of the specific problems in each budgetary area. as regards agricultural policy, category 1, the committee has been arguing for a change in principle, a development of agricultural policy, for several years. as a demonstration, we have even chosen to exceed the budgetary ceiling of 20 million for category 1b, rural development, as a mark of our principled position. we believe that in future money, especially the money in surplus in category 1, should be transferred to the more aggressive rural policy. we also propose that aid for the export of live animals be reduced and be recorded separately. it should be clear how much money is actually spent on live animal exports. it will be very interesting to see, by the way, whether the governments which have criticised the transport of animals, etc., will now really take the opportunity to follow parliament's line. all of this is in line with the commission's proposal for a mid-term review, although in the resolution we choose to call it development of agricultural policy rather than reform. category 2 is the structural funds i have already mentioned the problems of implementation. i shall therefore only briefly touch on the fisheries question. i fully understand the danish presidency's priority for enlargement at the present time. this is the most important political issue, but nevertheless the fisheries policy must also be reformed. the eu must create a sustainable fisheries that can survive in the long term. in the meantime, we have decided to do two things. firstly, we are confirming that the promise made last year of a further 27 million for the conversion of the spanish and portuguese fishing fleets will be kept. at the same time, we have chosen not to take a position on the details of how it will be financed, but we will come back to that. as regards the budget line for the fisheries reform, we have chosen to establish such a budget line but not to provide it with a specific amount. it's called an "e.m. mail". in category 3, internal policy, i would like to mention in particular information policy. we do not accept the council and commission's proposal for a major reduction in information policy. on the contrary, we believe that the union needs better support from the citizens. then information is a necessary lubricant. but we are moving the money, and our priority is clear: it is about enlargement and the future of the eu. i would also like to point out the external authorities, the so-called agencies. we have taken a restrictive line there. we have followed the council's budget proposals in the vast majority of cases. this is because member states sit on the boards of these agencies and should therefore reasonably take account of the needs of these authorities in the council's own draft budget. unfortunately, we can see that this does not always work. so i think that is something that we will have to discuss a lot in the future. as far as pilot projects and preparatory measures are concerned, i think we have had a very good dialogue with the commission on implementation. we are now launching some new ideas, including an idea for a pilot project for cooperation between smes in the eu, not only with the candidate countries but also with countries such as russia, the balkan countries and in north africa, which i think will be very important. mr president, i would like to thank you. finally, let me concentrate on category 4, external action, where we have had to fight the same conflict every year since i entered parliament in 1999. the council and the commission are proposing major new measures. it has been about the balkans and about serbia. this year it is afghanistan and the world health fund, support for the fight against aids and other poverty-related diseases in the third world. but no one is taking responsibility for providing the resources needed. i mean, this is unreasonable. the budget ceiling is simply too low. the expenditure has been built up each year without adding any new resources. we have chosen this year to exceed the ceiling but to do so openly through a so-called asterisk amendment, in which we call for an additional eur 72 million for category 4 with direct reference to afghanistan and the world health fund. we have built this proposal as far as possible on a combination of the priorities of our two committees here, the committee on foreign affairs and the committee on development. i think this will be the main point of the negotiations with the council between the first and second readings. it must be resolved if we are to have an acceptable role for the eu's external action. this also applies to the common foreign and security policy. it is unacceptable that this activity should increasingly shift from administrative expenditure to operational activities, but without the budgetary influence that the european parliament should have in the ordinary external action. that is why we have chosen to set the cfsp budget at the minimum level of 30 million, plus 10 million, but we have allocated them through the cards programme so that we can establish the european police force in bosnia. i think this is something we have to discuss very carefully between the first and second readings. in conclusion, i would like to say that this is the last budget for an eu of 15 member states. it is therefore a clear priority to start preparing for enlargement in this budget. i am particularly pleased to say that we have solved most of the problems of enlargement. we have resolved them by broad consensus. all the political groups have presented their names, and members of all the national delegations have presented their names. i feel that this is perhaps the greatest success of this work this year. (applause) i would like to thank you.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. i would also like to thank you for the good cooperation and for the opportunity to contribute to the preparation of the 2003 budget. i would like to thank all the members of the committee on budgets, the very competent budgetary secretariat, the bureau, especially the president of the commission, and finally the commission, the council and, last but not least, the very pragmatic and demanding danish presidency. mr president, i would like to thank you. throughout the current year, the eu's administrative budget, what we call category 5, has been the budget's big problem child. we entered the year with a significant deficit forecast in the 2003 budget. it was said to be in the order of eur 140 million. in stages, this was picked in the spring. nevertheless, in its preliminary draft budget, the commission proposed that the so-called flexibility instrument be used with eur 66 million to cover mainly the commission's costs of preparing for enlargement. both the council and the parliament opposed such a solution for various reasons. firstly, because it is difficult to see that an administrative budget is such that a flexibility instrument should be used. it can hardly be said that this is a matter of unforeseen expenditure or that enlargement is something that has been hastily devised. secondly, because, together with other areas where the flexibility instrument is already subscribed, this would take half the use of the instrument and thus prevent its use in areas where it would probably be much better needed. thirdly, and this is not least important for responsible politicians, budgetary frameworks are indeed to be respected, even if it can be argued with some justification that the eu budget is too rigid. when we concluded this agreement between the council and parliament at a uniquely early date in brussels on 19 july, we achieved at least three things: we locked in the overall budget and respected the ceiling for category 5, we knew how big the council's budget would be, and we had limited the european parliament's own budget. in this context, the priorities which parliament had already set out in the guidelines became fully possible, namely two main things. the first and obvious priority was enlargement. with the decisions taken since then, we know that if the member states at the copenhagen council in december live up to their commitments on ten new member states and if the agreements are signed in march, then we will be able to have 147 observers from all these countries here in the european parliament as early as april next year. the necessary decisions will be clear, both in terms of real estate, premises and other matters. as the second priority, it was about implementing the necessary reforms. with or without enlargement, these will be necessary, not least for parliament. this is particularly the case in key areas where each parliament, whether national or european, must be strong. it is a question of strengthening the budgetary structure for the european parliament, not primarily for the committee on budgets, but we are strengthening all the other committees in their important budgetary work. it is also a question of strengthening the legislative function of parliament. in a very short time, this has become a parliament with considerable legislative power. then our conditions for exercising this power must also be improved. with all this in place, two important issues remained to be resolved in the context of the 2003 budget. it was a question of allocating resources to all the institutions, also in the context of the forthcoming enlargement as a top priority. it also sought to find a solution to the commission's unresolved problems with regard to the financing of preparations for enlargement. the latter is particularly important, since at the time of the settlement there was a party who appeared to be the loser, namely the commission. already in the context of the settlement, both the council and parliament made an explicit commitment to help resolve the commission's problems in the run-up to enlargement. this promise has since been repeated on several occasions, both by myself and by other representatives of both the council and parliament. it is in this context that the concept of frontloading comes into the picture as the instrument we came to use. it was a first step to get a figure from all the institutions on how much money is left in this year's budget, i.e. for 2002. it is these funds that we can use in a second step to reduce the pressure on the 2003 budget and which can be transferred to the commission to enable us to cope with the costs of preparing for enlargement. at a meeting with the secretaries-general of all the institutions, we thus obtained a total of some eur 77 million which was thus unused in all the institutions. the maximum amount that the commission can use to prepare for enlargement is approximately eur 72 million. let me say a few words about parliament's own part of this frontloading. the proposal put forward by the committee on budgets and parliament's bureau last night for the current year amounts to eur 43 million. it is money which we should have invested in early repayments on parliament's own premises, in line with the forward-looking approach to money adopted by parliament so far. this is what we're shooting one year into the future. that being said, we're also taking back parts of this a year later. of the 43 million thus transferred to the commission, 35 million will be taken back in 2003, in accordance with the proposal on which we are voting today. in this context, i should like to mention that the original proposal was for 40 million. so we are now proposing to change this to 35 million. this means that parliament's share of this frontloading, over two budgetary years, will be eur 8 million, a figure which i consider to be well balanced. it is generous, but at the same time well balanced in comparison with all the other institutions. it shows that this frontloading, as i see it, is developing into a significant success and should be able to be used in other contexts in the future. it creates the possibility of using a multiannual budget to optimise the use of resources. mr president, i would like to thank you. i mentioned earlier that we made a promise to the commission to help solve the problems of the costs of preparing for enlargement. a promise is a promise, and as you know, it must be kept! here we are with a completed frontloading that gives us the ability to live up to what we promised. i think that feels extraordinarily good. for me, all of this is also about investing in good relationships. enlargement will only succeed if we realise that this is something we actually have to do together. it is a joint commitment. as rapporteur, i am very grateful for the way in which all the institutions, large and small, have stepped up to try to resolve this very difficult situation. (applause) i would like to thank you.

mr president, i would like to thank you. in two weeks, it's november 9th. it is the anniversary of the fall of the berlin wall, the wall that symbolised the division of europe, the hostility and the distance between east and west. on that day, we celebrate 13 years since joyous people climbed over a cracking wall, and the long journey toward reconciliation and unity began. we're almost there. we're approaching the end of the line. the commission report confirms that ten countries may become members in just over a year. it feels great, but the joy is clouded by a nagging worry. it's coming together. as we approach the finish line, we notice that a lot of people are getting nervous. support for the candidate countries is declining and several member states, leading politicians and parties are talking about waiting, postponing, 'we are not done'. domestic considerations overshadow the historic task of uniting europe for the first time ever by agreement, negotiation and compromise not by threats, tanks or invasion. of course, there are many questions among many people across europe about how cooperation should work and develop. difficulties and problems will arise. who would have thought that unifying europe would be easy? in writing. (sv) the report deals with the problems of the working environment in the eu workplace and expresses concern that the situation is getting worse rather than better. the rapporteur proposes a number of measures to tackle the problem, including better implementation of existing legislation in this area. we fully support this proposal. it also called for new legislation on harassment at work and for the introduction of ergonomie requirements. we are opposed to the regulation of problems by directives, both in theory and in practice. the existing work on the working environment is not working, mainly because employers do not comply with it and because trade unions are too weak, often setting minimum conditions under which the social partners are attempting to commit themselves to agreements providing very low protection. if, on the contrary, we had strong trade unions, and national governments with social ambitions, we could enact legislation at the national level which would be more precise, provide greater protection and could be implemented more effectively. we have voted in favour of the report because we agree with its political ambition: to improve working conditions in the member states.
in writing. (sv) during the debate on regulation (ec) no 1049/2001 of the european parliament and of the council on public access to european parliament, council and commission documents, it was decided that access by the european parliament to sensitive documents would be covered by a separate interinstitutional agreement between parliament and the council, in order to enable the european parliament to meet its commitments under article 21 ec of the treaty on european union. all agreements concluded between the council and parliament must, however, respect the fundamental principles of legality and the institutions' obligation to provide each other with information. in other words, it is not fair for the council to take decisions on parliament's internal working procedures, which clearly establishes the hierarchy between the institutions. there is also the danger of concealing information from small countries and small political groups. the agreement limits the right granted to all members and the public by regulation no 1049 by creating a group with special access to information. the establishment of an interinstitutional agreement in this sensitive area should be accompanied by strict rules on confidentiality (consistent with nato rules). such rules could apply to documents relating to external security without altering the structure between the institutions in any way.
in writing. - (sv) today we voted against the proposal for an institutional agreement on parliament's access to sensitive council documents on security and defence. the main reason for this is that we feel that the proposed committee, which would have only four members, is far too small. the possibility for small countries and small political parties to be represented is thus greatly reduced. the loss of information for these should increase. in order to increase transparency and equality, there should first of all be several permanent members on the committee, while a number of members would be appointed in accordance with the procedure laid down in the draft agreement.
in writing. - (sv) we have chosen to support amendments which allow generic medicines, i.e. cheaper copies, to be introduced more quickly on the market and thus to reduce the increasingly high costs of medicines, which, for example in sweden, have increased by 10 per cent per year during the 1990s. the procedure for the registration of generic medicinal products should be facilitated in order to speed up their placing on the market at the end of the 10 years following the first authorisation of the reference medicinal product. the manufacture of generic products could therefore begin before the end of the above-mentioned 10-year period. we therefore also voted against the amendments which prohibit the placing on the market of generic medicines by protecting slight modifications in the original medicinal product. we also believe that the current ban on advertising medicines to the public should be maintained. patients need to receive reliable information from independent sources.
in writing. (sv) we voted against the report, as we support, in principle, the incorporation of the charter into a possible future treaty (constitution). however, the approval we gave must be seen in the light of the following elements. the report proposes measures to improve direct access to the court of first instance. we interpret this proposal as a right of individuals to bring a case directly before the court of first instance of the european communities under the rights provided for in the charter. this would be inappropriate, as it would risk opening the way to legal review of all the acts of community decisions (taking into account the extent of the charter). we would prefer a system like the one currently in force for referral to the court of justice of the european communities: the rights provided for in the charter are applied indirectly, through the right of national courts to refer cases to the court of justice. the report takes a firm position on the changes to the horizontal provisions of the charter. we think, however, that it is reasonable to accept even substantive changes to these terms, as we cannot think of any other way in which the charter could be made legally binding and effectively applied in practice. we share the view of the assembly's working party on this matter. in general, we would like to point out that the binding nature of the charter should in no way undermine the collective agreement model applied to the labour market in the nordic countries.
in writing. - (sv) i voted in favour of the report on the impact of the charter of fundamental rights of the european union and its future status. i am in favour of incorporating the charter into the treaty. i am opposed to the argument that the eu should have legal personality or that the charter should be a step forward towards an eu state.
in writing. - (sv) this report, which deals with the charter of fundamental rights, proposes that it be made binding. a binding charter of such a wide scope is a driving force for greater integration and supranationality. the european court of justice is given increased powers to hear cases under the military tribunal. a charter of primary law in principle opens the way to a secondary law (regulations and directives) based on the charter. i share the view of the swedish parliament which has decided to say no to a binding charter and believes that the eu should instead join the european convention. therefore, i cannot support the motion.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the fear and panic that terrorism spreads is the same, whether it's a lone gunman in washington or a large network like al-qaeda. terrorism itself is not a new phenomenon it was not invented on september 11, 2001. on the other hand, global terrorism with its worldwide network, high-tech weapons, and seemingly inexhaustible financial resources is a new and very frightening phenomenon. after the attacks in new york and bali, it is clear to everyone that the fight against terrorism must be conducted globally, in the form of police and judicial cooperation, intelligence and surveillance. however, it is also a matter of long-term, preventive cooperation to eradicate or at least minimise the causes of terrorism. democracy is the best antidote to terrorism. the international alliance must therefore give priority to massive efforts to strengthen democracy, the rule of law and respect for human rights. it also concerns trade, aid and cooperation in international organisations. peace in the middle east would be a very important piece of the puzzle in the fight against terrorism. the liberal group is fully aware of the need for increased cooperation in the fight against terrorism. much has been done, much more can be done. however, we must remember that the main target of terrorists is the open society. that is why we must not allow our zeal to fight terrorism to undermine the very heart of democracy. the rule of law must never be sacrificed on the altar of action. special rules, military tribunals and unclear reasons for why an individual or group is placed on a particular list cannot be accepted. it is scandalous that the prisoners at the guantánamo bay detention facility are still living in a legal limbo. i would like to ask the council what is being done to clarify the situation of european citizens in cuba. mr president, you quoted karl popper's the open society and its enemies. we must not become the enemies of the open society itself. because if we do, the terrorists win on the walk over.
madam president, i would like to thank you. terrorism is unacceptable. we all agree. it is our views on the methods of combating terrorism that may differ. the eu's design of its counter-terrorism strategy is of paramount importance. the rule of law and human rights must not be undermined in the vital fight against terrorism. it is therefore an important resolution that the two committees are jointly presenting here. the committee on foreign affairs has highlighted the key instruments available to the eu for conflict prevention, such as democracy development, trade and diplomacy. addressing the root causes of terrorism, such as poverty, human rights violations and oppression, is of course central. in the shadow of 9/11, there is a risk that fundamental human rights may be violated in the fight against terrorism. there is also a risk that the only superpower we have today will take unilateral liberties outside the un system. it is therefore of the utmost importance that parliament should adopt the un charter and that the right of self-defence of an attacked state is legitimate only if it is confirmed or decided by the un security council. to start slipping on the tongue and talking about the desirability of the un resolution would directly undermine the united nations. i am pleased to note that we are unanimous in our view that a strategy of pre-emptive strikes, which the united states government wants to use, is not an effective way of combating terrorism, and that we stress that a solution to the conflict in the middle east in accordance with un resolutions is a very important element in the fight against terrorism. international law is central to a society governed by the rule of law. this is especially true for smaller states. undermining the un can only lead to disaster. it is therefore crucial that the eu safeguards the un and its charter, even in difficult times, and does not undermine the un's central role in world peace.
i would like to thank mr bertil haarder and the danish presidency for this detailed and excellent reply. i personally think that it was of great strategic importance during the swedish presidency that prime minister göran persson actually visited kiev and met the ukrainian government as his last foreign policy trip. i see the danish line as a continuation of this policy. i have a few follow-up questions. first of all: is it conceivable that in the relatively near future ukraine could be offered a special agreement which could be an eastern european variant of a special economic relationship, i.e. a form of eastern european eea agreement or danish eea agreement? secondly, i would like to know how you feel that relations between the eu and ukraine have been affected by the allegations of military cooperation with iraq on the part of ukraine.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. i am very pleased that we have reached this compromise, which will now also enable us to reach a compromise with the council at first reading. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr blokland, the danish presidency and the commission for their real efforts to find a good solution acceptable to all of us, even though not all our amendments have been adopted. it is important that the intentions of the rotterdam convention are known and that it can be ratified as soon as possible, because it concerns trade in very dangerous chemicals. i also think that it is fair to say that the commission has done a very good job in presenting this proposal for a regulation to replace the current legislation on dangerous chemicals and pesticides with the rules of the rotterdam convention. the proposal goes further than the convention, for example by proposing that the export notification requirement be extended to include articles containing chemicals in so-called unreacted form and that the export of certain chemicals be banned altogether. i am therefore very pleased with the proposal. the purpose of this whole exercise and of the convention is to enable trade in dangerous chemicals to be better controlled and to make exporting and importing countries take a joint and greater responsibility. it is about protecting human health and the environment. i also think it is therefore reasonable and right that the committee on the environment, with the help of the committee on legal affairs and citizens' rights, has changed the legal basis from article 133 to article 175, which gives the european parliament the right to vote and at the same time allows member states to have stricter rules. i therefore sincerely hope that we shall have a large majority, perhaps unanimity, for this report tomorrow. i think we have every reason to congratulate ourselves.
general rapporteur. – mr president, before we begin the vote on the budget and the budgetary amendments, there are some statements i would like to make, some corrections and oral amendments that i would like put to the vote. firstly, i would like to inform colleagues in the chamber that we have a very tight margin in category 3, for internal policies. it is only eur 2.6 million. if, as a result of the vote, we pass the ceiling, i would feel obliged to propose offsets and further reductions on other budget lines. that is just a little warning. then there are some technical corrections. first of all, amendment 662 on budget line b53004 (new) should not be subject to a separate vote because nobody has requested that. it has been listed as a separate vote by mistake. it should instead be voted on as part of block 4. i will check this with all the political groups but, as i understand it, there is no problem. we also have a technical adjustment in heading 5. the amendments proposed reflect the announcement by eurostat on 15 october. we have new parameters for salaries in 2003. the 2.6% increase has now been reduced to 2%. the resulting adjustments to match the front loading in 2002 need to be made. i will quickly read the figures for the record. in amendment 525, the final amount should be eur 1 450 901 000 and nothing else. in amendment 556,the final amount is eur 68 890 436. in amendment 650 on budget line a360, the final amount should read eur 38 219 720. then we have two new amendments, because we had no amendments on pensions. these are on budget line a1900, where the final amount should read eur 412 887 000, and on budget line a6000, where the final amount should read eur 124 456 000. in amendment 562 on budget line a7000, the final amount should read eur 54 916 000. finally, in amendment 527 on budget line a200, the final amount should be eur 147 529 904. we also have some technical corrections on agencies. there is a separate remark for the agency enlargement costs. they have now been slightly amended and should read as follows: for amendment 583 on budget line b3-4201, the correct amount is eur 7 150 000. for amendment 589, budget line b5-3111, the correct amount is eur 300 000. for amendment 596, budget line b5-8301, the final amount is eur 3 522 711. finally, we do not need a figure for amendment 593, because it has been withdrawn. there is no enlargement cost in that case. there is one item which has to be voted on and that is budget line a-3014, one of the a-30 lines about european masters' courses in human rights and democratisation. the reference here to the university of padua is not correct. it should be replaced by the european inter-university centre. that is actually a new amendment to the draft budget and should be voted on at the beginning. i would also like to tell the house that, in order to be consistent with the resolution, i will ask the sessional services to replace the second part of the first paragraph of the grounds for amendment 644 on afghanistan by paragraph 26 in the resolution, so that there is no difference between the wording of the resolution and the amendment. i do not think that requires a vote, but i just wanted to point it out to the house. finally, a politically more important point, because it concerns north korea and the discussions we have had on kedo and nuclear issues. following the recent information on the declaration by the north korean government on the possible production of nuclear weapons, the committee on budgets, at its meeting this monday, authorised me to table an oral amendment in plenary. this proposes that the eu's total contribution to kedo, which is eur 20 million, be entered in the reserve, on the grounds that the north korean government has admitted it is working on the research and production of nuclear weapons. this is clearly at odds with the general objectives of eu participation in the euratom-korean peninsula energy development organisation kedo, which are 'to contribute to reinforcing and maintaining the international nuclear non-proliferation regime and to help achieve appropriate cooperation with both north korea and south korea to ensure the safe and peaceful use of nuclear energy'. the appropriations will be released from this reserve on the condition that, firstly, north korea proves itself willing to respect the international non-proliferation regime and, secondly, that north korea proves it has discontinued its nuclear weapons production programme. this is also a new amendment to the draft budget and must, preferably, be voted on at the beginning. finally, when we come to category 4, i shall comment on two specific aspects of that category, but that is all i wish to say at this point.
rapporteur. – mr president, before proceeding with the vote let me draw members' attention to the following oral amendments to heading 5. firstly, as the council is now making a contribution to the frontloading operation, and given that parliament will contribute almost eur 43 million instead of eur 40 million, which is what the bureau and the committee on budgets decided on monday evening, i propose that an amount of eur 35 million should be taken back in 2003. thus parliament's net contribution will amount to eur 8 million. secondly, following eurostat's new figure on salary adjustments, which is now reduced from 2.6% to 2%, the amounts entered in the different sections on staff expenditure also need to be adjusted accordingly. i therefore suggest the following adjustments to the following amendments to reflect these changes. amendment 493, section – parliament: a reduction of eur 1.9 million on item 1100. amendment 715, section – parliament: a reduction of eur 3.1 million on item 209. amendment 514, concerning the court of justice: a reduction of eur 500 000 on item 1100. amendment 516, concerning the court of auditors: a reduction of eur 36 000 on item 1100. amendment 520, on the economic and social committee: a reduction of eur 236 000 on item 1100. amendment 522, on the committee of the regions: a reduction of eur 102 000 on item 1100. finally, amendment 523, concerning the ombudsman: a reduction of eur 11 000. i will have to make further remarks on the resolution.
general rapporteur. – mr president, i would just like to say that we are now going into category 4 – external actions – and here we have a special situation because we have created a so-called 'asterisk' amendment. we are above the ceiling of eur 72 million. this 'asterisk' amendment means that if we make any more increases after this block 5, credits for afghanistan will be reduced. i just wanted to warn everybody about this. of course, this is a first reading position, but still i wanted to inform you of the situation.
general rapporteur. – mr president, this should, i hope, be the last time i speak in this vote. (laughter) last monday, we discussed east timor in the committee on budgets, and i just want to explain what we are doing here. to maintain a separate budget line for east timor – which is very politically important for the timorese government and for many others – a split vote has been requested on amendment 674. in order to clarify the vote, i should like to explain that the split vote means that the first part will include the amounts. if we vote in favour of the first part, we vote in favour of the increased amount, which is eur 25.5 million. as far as i know, that is acceptable to everybody. the second part of the vote concerns the nomenclature. if you want a separate line for east timor – which is the rapporteur's recommendation, authorised by the committee on budgets – you should reject the second part of the amendment by the committee on budgets.
rapporteur. – mr president, i hope this will be the only thing i have to say concerning the resolution. the first-reading resolution needs to be adjusted to reflect the vote. therefore, paragraph 8 needs to be amended as follows. the word 'intention' should be replaced by 'decision'. the figure of 40 million in the first line should be replaced by '42 690 000' and the figure of 40 million in line 8 should be replaced by '35 million'. secondly, a minor change is also needed in paragraph 19 in the verb tenses. in line 3, the words 'should be' should be replaced by 'are'. in line 4, the words 'should be' should be replaced by 'is'. in line 5, the words 'should conduct' should be replaced by 'conducts'.
in writing. - (sv) in the vote on the budget we voted in general against the commission's proposals to increase agricultural expenditure under heading 1. we believe that eu money can be used more appropriately in other areas where it is really needed. it is not fair that about half of the total budget of the union goes each year to subsidies for the production and export of agricultural products. the current common agricultural policy is not only costly for the union, but also contributes to higher consumer prices. export subsidies make eu food surpluses dumped on the world market, so that the food industry in many developing countries, for example, is at a serious competitive disadvantage. the fact that the union also grants a subsidy to tobacco cultivation is particularly noteworthy. the eu's actions and budget must instead address crossborder problems that member states cannot solve on their own. the eu's agricultural policy, in the form of export and production subsidies, must therefore be phased out and replaced to some extent by subsidies for biodiversity and ecological measures.
in writing. - (sv) we are pleased to see that, compared with the council's proposal, the budget for the fight against hiv/aids has been increased by some eur 200 million, or 60 per cent. this shows that parliament is genuinely committed to the commitments it made when the global health fund was set up. the swedish christian democrats largely share parliament's negotiating position on the budget. we would like to comment on a number of points of a quite different nature. we believe it is important that the commission gives a clear answer on how the aid to israel and palestine is being used before we commit the full amount. the fact that we are now approving only half the budget is putting pressure in the right direction. it is good that the decision has been taken to phase out subsidies for the transport of live animals, even though we would have preferred that these subsidies be abolished altogether. we regret, however, the reduction in aid to east timor, which is still in a consolidation phase, which follows directly on from the declaration of independence. we believe it is wrong to provide support to european parties, amounting to eur 7 million, as long as there is no legal basis for this and we regret that the proposal to remove the envelope was rejected by 393 votes to 76. we also supported the idea of a feasibility study on the establishment of a civilian peacekeeping force, but this was rejected.
in writing. (sv) the we swedish social democrats voted against the amendments today which seek to increase agricultural subsidies. we believe that agricultural subsidies should be reduced. a first step in the agricultural reform project would be to transfer resources to rural development. in the somewhat longer term, however, it is not enough to simply redistribute resources to the agricultural sector. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's agricultural subsidies are reduced. this is particularly true of tobacco subsidies. the eu must not subsidize the production of crops that endanger human life.
in writing. (sv) the for several years, the community sector in the member states has been the victim of major cuts. in this context, it is regrettable that the eu budget contains a particularly large number of unused resources and that there is a complete waste of money in some cases. we would like to see a large part of eu budgetary expenditure, such as agricultural subsidies, export subsidies for the transport of live animals to third countries, most regional aid, subsidies for tobacco growing and the functioning of the economic and social committee, and subsidies for european political parties, eu federalist organisations and the dissemination of eu/une propaganda, abolished in whole or in part. this list of what we believe to be totally unacceptable expenditure in the eu can be extended further. another detail which we insist on every year is that travel expenses for members of the european parliament should only be reimbursed in the event of actual costs relating to travel for eu work. it is unlikely that this issue will ever be resolved, despite the fact that so many people appear to support this principle. public money spent by the eu can be better used in the member states. we believe that the member states' contribution to the european union can be reduced.
in writing. - (sv) in sweden, freedom of expression has been protected by the constitution since 1766. the insider dealing and market manipulation (market abuse) directive has been the subject of debate and to some extent of concern, as the protection of journalists' right to freedom of expression is to continue even after the directive has entered into force. however, we feel that we have obtained sufficient explanations to justify why the directive is of the utmost importance, in particular to avoid financial speculation at the expense of individuals, as well as to ensure that the directive will have no impact on the swedish constitution. the directive in its entirety is an essential element in the creation of a free and more operational financial market which would offer good protection to consumers and investments. with the eldr amendments, financial journalism would be included in the scope of the directive, while leaving the responsibility for enforcement to existing self-regulatory bodies. that is why we voted today in favour of the insider dealing and market manipulation (market abuse) directive.
in writing. (sv) the we believe that, for the sake of fairness, two levels of customer compensation would be the best solution; in practice, one inside the eu and one outside the eu. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one.

there were many of us, not just here in europe but all over the world, who put our faith in president khatami. of course, this was primarily for the iranian people. we all hoped that he could lead iran on a more democratic, open and tolerant path. unfortunately, we've all been disappointed time and time and time again. the latest reports are frightening. journalists, intellectuals and critics of the regime are disappeared, tortured, imprisoned and subjected to frivolous trials. the death penalty is widely practiced. in that respect, iran is one of the worst offenders in the world. the latest reports of women being stoned to death under old sharia law are absolutely hair-raising. that this is happening in 2002 is totally unacceptable. the whole world is turning in disgust towards iran. the eu and iran have some cautious relations, which is good. however, if they are to be meaningful, the question of human rights must always be at the top of the agenda. i would also remind you that parliament has its veto power to approve any agreement, and we will use it if the situation does not improve.
mr president, mr president-in-office of the council, commissioners, i would like to thank you for your work. after the vote on the treaty of nice and the european council in brussels, there do not appear to be any serious obstacles to enlargement. true, there are some pebbles in the way of the tracks, but none of them seem to be able to overturn the train to copenhagen. what long seemed to be an unhappy love affair between east and west became a budding romance almost exactly 13 years ago, when the iron curtain fell and allowed the relationship to blossom to its fullest. now we are almost ready for marriage, and as a liberal i feel an immense pride that it is a liberal government that is finally blessing the union between these countries. i would like to congratulate the danish presidency on what it has achieved so far. i would like to raise three questions, mr president-in-office. firstly, there is turkey. the resolution we are voting on tomorrow is rather vague on turkey. i think it is important that we clearly encourage the forces of reform in turkey and welcome the positive signals coming from the new government in ankara, as you did. there is still a lot to be done before the copenhagen criteria are met, but i hope that the copenhagen european council can be a qualitative step forward in relations with turkey. it is in everyone's interest that we have close and close relations with turkey and that we are helped in the fight to strengthen human rights. secondly: the security clauses. the more i hear about them, the more confusing they seem to me. how are they to be applied? how are they to be interpreted and activated? are they really that thoughtful? what we really need in the eu is a kind of system of scrutiny that covers all eu countries, old and new, to make sure that we all live up to the democratic values and criteria on which the eu is founded: respect for human rights, non-discrimination, respect for the rule of law, good governance, etc. we know that there are member states which do not currently meet the copenhagen criteria. thirdly and lastly, it was good that an agreement was reached on agricultural policy. this was one of the main obstacles to the timetable for enlargement. the presidency thus proved that the doomsday prophets who had condemned the brussels european council were wrong. the eldr group would, however, have very much liked to see a clearer reform agenda with a clear support for commissioner fischler's proposals and for our commitments in doha. agricultural policy needs major reforms. there is still some work to be done before the copenhagen meeting. we should not underestimate the difficulties of this work. the candidate countries will then hold their own referendums. i hope we can all use the time until 2004 to explain, inform and convince citizens across the continent of the importance of european unity - not for our sake, but for theirs. it is as jean monnet said: we are not uniting states, but citizens and people.
mr president, madam president of the council, commissioner! the decisionmaking system we have in the european union sometimes brings unexpected and exciting situations. i would like to begin by congratulating the european council on reaching an agreement. it is extremely good and extremely important. however, i would also like to stress that this agreement represents a significant change in the whole debate on the future of agricultural policy which we did not really expect. a few words on mid-term review: there is a clear need for reform of the agricultural policy for budgetary reasons, for equity reasons, for environmental reasons and for other reasons - i will not go into details here - as well as for reasons linked to the doha outcome: to contribute to a better functioning of world trade and to facilitate the development of the economies of the poorest countries. it is of the utmost importance that this works. it is therefore essential that the commission's proposals on decoupling and better market alignment of agricultural policy can be implemented. it is also essential that modulation, i.e. greater investment in the environment and rural areas, can be implemented and that some money is taken from direct grants. a few words on the resolution of parliament: we have reached a compromise which essentially supports the commission's proposal. i think it is very valuable that the committee on agriculture and rural development has succeeded in doing this, even though we had quite different views from the beginning. there are, however, a few points which must be put to a separate vote. i can say, for example, on behalf of the eldr group that we cannot support the amendment of the interinstitutional agreement to use the budgetary ceiling of 1.27% of gdp. it's completely impossible. we must vote against those items on which we cannot vote, and i hope they will not be passed either. however, in order to continue the debate on a constructive change in the agricultural policy in the long term, it is important that the proposal put forward by the commission in its mid-term review be given life. i would like to ask both the commissioner and the president of the council what they intend to do to keep this debate alive, now that it is becoming much more difficult to discuss modulation, when we do not know where we are going to get the money from, and when it is becoming much more difficult to justify decoupling aid. how can we make this work and have a constructive debate on the future of agricultural policy?
mr president, i would like to thank you. the north korean admission that it has a programme under way to develop nuclear weapons is not only an affront to the credibility of international agreements but also a total defeat for the kedo project. the eu has believed that a totalitarian regime could be bribed with so-called peaceful civilian nuclear power to get it to give up its nuclear weapons. this naive confidence in nuclear solutions to serious problems has now paid off, making the eu look embarrassing and ridiculous. the verts/ale group has argued from the outset that the kedo project is ill-conceived. we've been terribly wrong. unfortunately, it seems that neither the commission nor the nuclear parties in parliament have the courage to face the truth and draw the logical consequences of the kedo fiasco and cancel the project. the joint motion for a resolution lacks a clear recognition that kedo was from the outset flawed, ill-conceived, dangerous and counterproductive. support for the severely tormented people of north korea must be given in entirely different forms. the eu must openly and clearly admit its fatal mistake of joining kedo and inform the other kedo members that the eu is withdrawing. in fact, those responsible for kedo should be ashamed of themselves. it is true that we must proceed with caution in order not to damage the so-called sunshine policy and that we must talk to the pyongyang leadership in order to push the normalisation process forward. but there is no need to cling to a project as completely skewed and discredited as kedo.
mr president, commissioner, colleagues and rapporteur! i would like to thank you for a good report and an interesting debate. i can say that as the last speaker. regional policy is very important for the union of today, but also for solidarity in the eu of the future. the closer we get to 2007, the more intense the discussion in our regions about what the future will bring. i can confirm that there is a lot of concern in the regions about what will happen to the future of structural policy. we still have problems, as the commission's reports show, including unemployment and the widening gap between metropolitan areas and other regions that are lagging behind. we must use the funds available today effectively, and this also applies to future structural funds budgets. it is therefore important to have partnerships with a real influence on the regions in how the money is used and to simplify administrative procedures so that the confusion does not prevent the regions from using the money well. the report is very positive in that it emphasizes that regions with geographical handicaps should be given priority in future structural funds. we know that many regions are far from the major markets and have to live with that geography. i also think that amendment no 10 by my own group, the pse group, is very important. it points out that indicators other than gdp should also guide the use of the structural funds. finally, i would like to stress the importance of all policies promoting regional development. if they do not, regional policy will be toothless. agricultural policy, and transport policy in particular, must promote regional equalization.
in writing. (sv) we, the swedish members of the group of the party of european socialists, who have signed this explanatory statement of vote, choose to vote against the entire resolution on the mid-term review of the common agricultural policy. we are in favour of a radical reform of the agricultural policy and we support the commission's proposals for reform. we believe, however, that the way in which parliament's resolution is worded sends mixed and sometimes contradictory messages. the reason we take this position is that we do not want to make it even more difficult for developing countries to enter our market with their agricultural products. paragraphs 6, 32 and 34 refer to 'protection' against 'unfair external competition' and to 'special external protection', which we cannot support. we are also opposed to the allocation of more money to the common agricultural policy. paragraph 21 requires full use to be made of the ceiling of 1,27% of gdp. in round figures, this would mean the allocation of an extra 25 billion euro to this budget.
in writing. (sv) we in the moderates voted today against the resolution on the mid-term review of the common agricultural policy. we have done so because we believe it is very important that the expenditure on the common agricultural policy should not be increased in the way proposed in the resolution and that the present interinstitutional agreement should be respected. we cannot support a resolution which proposes tariffs in the agricultural sector. the way the resolution is worded weakens the commission's proposals. we regret that we cannot support this resolution because, while broadly supporting the commission's proposals, it proposes significant changes to the common agricultural policy. the commission's primary objective is to create a competitive agricultural sector which will benefit consumers, taxpayers and the agricultural sector as such.
in writing. -sv) we welcome the resolution's desire to reform the common agricultural policy and the emphasis on the environment, food security, animal health and protection throughout this policy. it is also positive to see that the european parliament is willing to support young farmers and traditional family farms, especially in the less favoured regions. we would also like to make it clear in general terms that food production must not be placed on the same footing as industrial production and must therefore be protected by specific trade measures, particularly with a view to strengthening the environmental perspective. we welcome the rejection of the original paragraph 21, which calls for an increase in the eu budget up to a ceiling of 1.27% of gdp, as proposed by the commission (356 votes in favour, 135 against). this would have resulted in an increase of some 150 billion swedish kronor, which is totally unacceptable. the resolution does not indicate that the current agricultural policy is protectionist and is hurting many of the world's poorest countries. unfortunately, it is in favour of a defensive policy: the european parliament says that agriculture must be protected against unfair external competition and must be given qualified external protection. we therefore voted against paragraph 21 of the resolution as a whole and abstained from voting on the resolution as a whole.
in writing. (sv) we have today voted in favour of mrs schroedter's report. we do not, however, accept recital b, which makes subsidies for cohesion policy final. in the context of enlargement, it is difficult to know exactly what is needed in terms of resources for the different forms of regional aid. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the application of the principle of subsidiarity to the member states is a good one. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area.
in writing. (sv) we moderates voted in favour of mrs schroedter's report today because it stresses the need to maintain the budgetary ceiling and also the need to abolish regional aid in regions where gdp levels are no longer below 75% of the eu average. it is important, however, to stress the fact that regional policy must be reformed. the process by which the eu is being transformed into a capital transfer union must not continue. paying money to the eu simply to be repaid in the form of structural aid is not a proper way of solving the problem of the less developed regions. the eu's benefit to the new member states does not consist in the distribution of subsidies at the expense of the taxpayers. instead of providing targeted subsidies to weaker regions of the member states, there should be reductions in contributions resulting from the member state's status for regions whose income is much lower than the eu average. in this way, no member state will fail economically, and furthermore, the possible harmful effects on their economies caused by the heavy dependence on subsidies will be avoided. as the report rightly points out, there are irregularities and fraud involving the channelling of money through the eu budget. the regulations governing subsidies must therefore be clear and less ambiguous. the eu must also support crossborder regional cooperation, especially between countries that have been in conflict with another country in the past or have strained relations.
mr president, i would like to thank you. at this point in the debate there may not be much new to add, but i shall try anyway. it is urgent to create common conditions and rules for tobacco advertising. in the absence of such a system, there is often room for arbitrariness and for the various actors to act at their own discretion. it is therefore good that we now have proposals which are also considered to be legally viable. there are many difficult considerations that must be made to ensure that we do not find ourselves in the same situation as with the previous proposal for a directive on tobacco advertising. the public health perspective is very important when it comes to tobacco marketing. regulating tobacco marketing can protect public health. at the same time, however, it is important to send the right signals to children and young people in particular. tobacco is an addictive product that causes over half a million deaths a year in the community. by being very restrictive in tobacco advertising we can help to prevent young people from starting to smoke and save suffering and lives. article 95 (3) of the treaty requires the commission to take as a starting point a high level of health protection in its proposals for the establishment and functioning of the internal market. there is no doubt that tobacco advertising increases consumption, both by encouraging children and youths to start smoking and by preventing current smokers from quitting or reducing their daily consumption. studies have also shown that children and adolescents are particularly vulnerable to tobacco advertising and tobacco-related sponsorship. a partial ban on advertising is therefore not sufficient to protect public health.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by saying that the green group supports the commission's proposal for reform of the common fisheries policy. the measures and proposals presented should have been put forward long ago. that is why they are now absolutely necessary. the only thing we have doubts about and see problems with is the commission's proposal on how to reduce fishing fleets with the simple 'one boat in, one boat out' proposal. i do not really understand how this is going to work and how we could get a smaller fishing fleet through this proposal. i can also understand the commission's frustration in view of the difficulties that have arisen in holding the council and the member states to account. for so many years, ministers and the council have failed to see the link between fisheries policy and a decline in fish stocks. they have not taken any responsibility for sustainable development and the conservation of fish stocks, which is the only way to save the fishing industry. for example, in the irish sea, over the last 10 years, a quota has been allowed that is 28 per cent higher than what scientists have considered acceptable. it does not matter whether the fishing fleets come from ireland or spain or any other country. i would therefore say that we are now facing our fate in terms of fish stocks. the council has not wanted to decide on restrictions on fishing quotas, which have been totally unrealistic. the commission has not had sufficient control programmes and has not called for a reduction in fishing fleets. we already know that this is the root of the whole problem. what are we to do? i think we must be able to accept the commission's proposal. sweden is planning to introduce a unilateral moratorium on cod fishing in the baltic sea. it is a demand that the greens made when they formed the government. cod is threatened not only in the baltic sea, but also in the kattegat and the north sea. the international council for the exploration of the sea considers the eu's recovery plan to be inadequate and that the situation is urgent. then we must take drastic measures, such as imposing a moratorium. i hope that the commission will support this proposal. i also take it for granted that the commission will give a positive response to the possibility of using the structural funds for compensation. this is in line with the commission's own proposal to act responsibly to ensure and conserve fish stocks for the future.

violence and coercion were used to impose european unity. the berlin wall, on the other hand, was a reversed way of preventing people and ideas from meeting and coming together. what is unique about bureaucrats, not to soldiers, and that is quite well put. but the eu is not just made up of bureaucrats, it is also made up of politicians with visions and ideas. the european parliament's common idea and vision has been its conviction of the benefits of developing european cooperation together. we have consistently and persistently pushed for enlargement with clear dates, demands and positions. sometimes there have been storms in governments, in domestic politics, but we have stood firm. the brok report is a clear expression of this consensus. the liberal group supports the report. for us liberals, enlargement, or reunification, has been the most important priority for many years. it is a natural continuation of the fall of the berlin wall and the breakthrough of democracy, freedom and market economy in eastern and central europe. the eu has long been incomplete, with only western european members. only by acting together can we defend and stand up for the common values and aspirations: freedom, democracy, human rights, a functioning market with growth and prosperity in a europe that puts the citizen at the centre. the major challenges ahead, environmental problems, crossborder crime, europe's role in the world and our responsibility for a fairer world order also require joint efforts, as does the work of reforming european institutions to make them more democratic and effective. i look forward to the contributions and fresh ideas of the new colleagues in this field. mr president, i would like to thank you. in a united europe, we are all different, violations are never excusable, and we welcome the great progress made in the candidate countries. we will continue to monitor these areas, i.e. respect for minorities, freedom of the press, good governance in the old member states as well as in the new ones. we believe that this is the way the safeguard clauses should be used, that is, by continuously reviewing how we all apply and respect mr president, i would like to thank you. ladies and gentlemen, i welcome the report by mr brok on the whole. i would also like to thank the commission, the european parliament and, in particular, the applicant countries for all the work they have done to make enlargement possible. today, however, i would like to recall the sustainable development strategy adopted at the gothenburg summit and also the united nations environment action programme adopted in johannesburg. the eu has pledged to take the lead in implementing this action programme. the focus on ecologically sustainable development must now be reflected in all eu policies and in all plans and programmes to meet the need for a change of course in the eu, but also to meet the needs of environmental investment in the candidate countries. the proportion of resources from the structural funds and the cap allocated to the environment must therefore be considerably increased. the commission has already set aside some 10% of the structural funds until 2006 for environmental infrastructure, such as waste water treatment. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' to cover all transport services. this will come into force in 2003 in the member states. this is absolutely essential if we are to achieve sustainable development throughout the whole of the european union.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the rapporteur for an excellent report, as usual. this is a historic day in this parliament. enlargement is the biggest issue we have to deal with in the european union. europe will thus be safer for future generations and democracy will be stronger in our part of the world. however, there are many sceptics in some quarters about this cooperation, which has been accused of being a cooperation only for the richer countries in our part of the world. we are now opening up this cooperation to all the countries in our part of the world and showing that this is for the whole of europe. as several speakers have already said, i too believe that we can all be winners in this situation. it is clear that enlargement will entail costs in the short term, but i am absolutely convinced that we will see economic gains. i myself come from the baltic region. i see the economy growing in our part of europe thanks to enlargement. in the field of environmental policy, the big gains will be made by taking action in the countries that are now joining the union. it's good for them, and it's good for us. the same applies to the labour market, where there is a risk of job relocation due to poor conditions. that situation is improved by giving us all common minimum conditions. the other day, i was at a seminar on cross-border crime. we shall also be able to do so by working closely together. we all have the potential to be winners, both the current eu member states and the candidate countries. but it depends on ourselves and how we manage the challenge we now face. it is a europe of greater diversity and a europe of greater economic, social and cultural wealth.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i have a question for commissioner diamantopoulou on the european employment process and its most practical difficulty, namely the problem of implementing the policy in the member states. most of the players in the labour markets across europe know nothing about the european employment strategy. the commission is aware of this and has begun to stress the importance of implementation, which i very much welcome. the last time i heard mrs diamantopoulou speak in our committee, she said that further consideration would be given to how to include both the european parliament and the national parliaments in the european employment process. i think that sounds great. my question is whether the commission has made progress and has a clearer idea of what is to be done. if you look at each individual member state, they all have a kind of national action plan for employment in the sense that their parliament adopts a budget which sets out the measures for which there are funds available under labour market policy for the coming year. since two different national action plans cannot exist, the national action plan, which forms part of and builds on the guidelines of the european employment process, must therefore be coordinated with the political and economic work of the respective parliaments in creating a national employment policy. i wonder why the commission is not more clear on this point. i know that we cannot tell the member states how to conduct their decision-making process, but we can of course make recommendations, not least in the context of the open employment process. with this to be introduced in the candidate countries, where there is a weak administrative capacity, it is all the more important to place the political responsibility for the employment process clearly in the political process and to emphasise the crucial responsibility and influence of the national parliaments in this matter.
